# Imperially Compiled Balanced Interpretations of the Zhou Changes

## Juan Twelve · 卷十二

Commentary on the Images, Lower Part: Influence through Before Completion. [Chinese source](../source/juan-12.md) · [Translation conventions](README.md)

**Voices:** “Original Meaning” is Zhu Xi; “Cheng's Commentary” is Cheng Yi; “Editorial Judgment” belongs to the imperial compilers. Translator’s notes appear separately at the end. Great and Small Images are distinguished by their headings; the line labels are reading aids.

## 31. Xian 咸 — Influence

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4670 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:001 -->

**Commentary on the Images, Lower Part**[^j12-layer]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:002 -->

### Great Image: A lake upon the mountain: Influence. Thus the noble person receives others with an open heart.[^j12-xian-openness]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:003 -->

**Original Meaning.** A lake upon the mountain: openness allows communication.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:004 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** A lake's nature is to moisten what lies below; earth's nature is to receive moisture. The lake is upon the mountain, and its moisture gradually penetrates throughout: the vital energies of the two things influence and communicate with one another. The noble person observes this image of mountain and lake communicating their energies and empties his heart to receive from others. With openness within, a person can receive; when filled up, nothing can enter. An open heart means freedom from self. With no private master within, no influence fails to find a response. Accommodating others only to the extent of one's own measure, selecting what agrees with oneself before receiving it, is not the sage's Way of responding freely to every influence.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:005 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:006 -->

**Cui Jing:** The mountain is high and descends; the lake is low and rises. Mountain and lake communicate their energies: the image of Influence.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:007 -->

**Lü Dalin:** A lake occupies low ground and a mountain high ground. Yet a mountain can bring forth clouds and occasion rain because it is open within, allowing the lake's energy to pass through. Thus when the noble person stands above others and their feelings communicate with his, he too receives through openness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:008 -->

**Guo Yong:** Only openness makes receiving possible, and receiving makes influence possible. Failure to influence comes from inability to receive; inability to receive comes from inability to be open.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:009 -->

**Hu Bingwen:** Receiving others through openness is influence without a contriving heart.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:010 -->

**Chen Chen:** A lake upon the mountain influences the mountain through its moisture; the mountain receives that influence through its openness. This is the image of Influence. The noble person embodies it by opening his heart to receive the influence of others. With no private master in the heart, every influence communicates freely. But if even a hair's breadth of private intention obscures him, what entered first becomes the master, blocking the workings of influence and response. Even what he receives may not be what he ought to receive, while what he ought to receive he rejects as disagreeable.[^j12-xian-readings]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:011 -->

**He Kai:** Among the six lines, thought is mentioned once and intention three times. How could thought be dispensed with? Yet when it reaches the point of companions following one's thoughts, there is no openness. How could intention be absent? Yet an intention directed toward the extremity, toward what is outside, or toward following others is not open. To carry the matter to its utmost: Heaven and Earth influence things through openness, and the sage influences human hearts through openness. The Way of the Three Powers is fully expressed here.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:012 -->

**Wu Yueshen:** Openness is Influence's steadfast correctness. Heaven and Earth's constancy consists in extending their heart to all things without a contriving heart; the sage's constancy consists in according with every affair without private emotional partiality. Both are simply openness. The noble person's learning is expansive and wholly impartial, responding fittingly as things arrive. This is what is meant by receiving others with an open heart.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:013 -->

### First Six: Influence in the big toe: the intention is directed outward.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:014 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The first line's intention stirs in response to the fourth; hence it is directed outward. Although the intention stirs, the influence is not yet deep. Like the movement of a big toe, it is not yet enough to advance.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:015 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:016 -->

**Yu Fan:** The intention directed outward refers to the fourth.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:017 -->

**Kong Yingda:** It corresponds to the fourth: what influences it is outside.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:018 -->

**Yu Yan:** The first and fourth influence and respond to one another, joining together. Thus the intention's destination lies outside.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:019 -->

### Six in the Second Place: Though there is misfortune, remaining brings good fortune: following what is right brings no harm.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:020 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The second occupies the center and is correctly placed, and its correspondent is also centered and correct. Its capacities are fundamentally good. Yet because it occupies the time of Influence, is yielding in constitution, and responds above, it is warned that moving first to seek the ruler brings misfortune, whereas remaining and preserving itself brings good fortune. The Image further explains that this does not prohibit mutual influence. Only follow principle, and there is no harm: preserve the Way rather than take the initiative in moving.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:021 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:022 -->

**Gu Xiangde:** Though there is misfortune, remaining brings good fortune because one can follow principle in responding, unharmed by impetuous movement. Remaining does not mean exclusively keeping still; it means merely not moving recklessly.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:023 -->

### Nine in the Third Place: Influence in the thighs: it too does not stay still. Its intention is to follow others; what it holds to is lowly.[^j12-xian-also]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:024 -->

**Original Meaning.** “Too” is used because the preceding two lines both desire to move. Those two lines are restless yin, so movement accords with their character. The third nine, however, is yang-firm and occupies the utmost of Keeping Still. It should be still but moves: humiliation indeed.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:025 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The word “too” is used because the Image statements were originally collected separately, not placed beside the text of the Changes. Consequently, the meanings of successive line Images sometimes continue one another. Here “too” follows the preceding line statements: “Influence in the big toe: the intention is directed outward”; “Though there is misfortune, remaining brings good fortune: following what is right brings no harm”; then “Influence in the thighs: it too does not stay still.” The preceding two yin lines both move under influence; though the third is yang, it does likewise. Hence “it too does not stay still.” Not staying still means moving. Possessing a firm yang constitution yet unable to govern itself, it instead directs its intention toward following others. What it holds to is lowly indeed.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:026 -->

### Nine in the Fourth Place: Steadfastness brings good fortune; regret vanishes: it has not been harmed by influence. Restless coming and going: its influence is not yet radiant and great.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:027 -->

**Original Meaning.** Being harmed by influence means that responding without correctness brings harm.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:028 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** With steadfast correctness there is good fortune and regret vanishes: one has not been harmed by private influence. Attachment to a private correspondent injures one's capacity to respond. Restless coming and going means influencing others through a partial heart. The Way of influence is then narrow; hence it is not yet radiant and great.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:029 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:030 -->

**Lu Jiuyuan:** Because Influence's fourth nine occupies the place of the heart, the sage speaks here of influence and communication with particular completeness. The line says, “Steadfastness brings good fortune; regret vanishes,” and the Image explains this as not being harmed by influence. When private influence has not done harm, the heart in its original condition is correct wherever it goes and communicates with everything that moves it. The line says, “Restless coming and going; companions follow your thoughts,” and the Image calls this not yet radiant and great. A private heart restlessly coming and going necessarily influences only a narrow circle: those who follow its thoughts are merely its private companions. Does not the sage's cleansing of the heart mean washing away this private restlessness and preserving its original correctness whole? Thus he withdraws into the hidden, yet can share the people's life and engage with things without falling into the one-sidedness of clinging to them or becoming submerged in them.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:031 -->

### Nine in the Fifth Place: Influence in the flesh of the back: the intention is at the extremity.[^j12-xian-back]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:032 -->

**Original Meaning.** An intention at the extremity means inability to influence things.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:033 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The warning to turn one's back upon the heart and confine influence to the flesh of the back is given because the heart's concerns are shallow and peripheral: attached to the second and delighting in the top, it responds to private desire.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:034 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:034 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:035 -->

**Li Dingzuo:** “Extremity” means the top. The fifth adjoins the top; therefore “influence in the flesh of the back: the intention is at the extremity” means that the fifth's intention responds to the top.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:035 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:036 -->

**Zhu Zhen:** In a hexagram, the first is the root and the top the extremity.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:036 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:037 -->

**Wang Zongchuan:** The fifth is said to have the image of influence in the flesh of the back because its intention is directed toward the hexagram's extremity. Hence it is desired that influence remain in the back's flesh, turning away from that attachment.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:037 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:038 -->

**He Kai:** This means that the fifth intends to respond to the top. The Appended Statements says, “Its first is hard to understand, its top easy to understand: root and extremity.” The Judgment Commentary on Great Excess says that root and extremity are weak. Clearly the extremity refers to the top six.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:038 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:039 -->

### Top Six: Influence in the jaws, cheeks, and tongue: giving free rein to the mouth's persuasions.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:039 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:040 -->

**Original Meaning.** The graphs *teng* 滕 and 騰 are used interchangeably.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:040 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:041 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Only utmost sincerity can move others. Instead one lets yielding persuasions rise and spill from mouth and tongue. How could this truly move anyone?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:041 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:042 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:042 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4670:043 -->

**Wang Bi:** The Way of Influence has turned toward its extremity; thus nothing remains but mouth, tongue, and words.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4670:043 -->

<a id="eee-4671"></a>

## 32. Heng 恒 — Constancy

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4671 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4671:001 -->

### Great Image: Thunder and wind: Constancy. Thus the noble person stands firm without changing his direction.[^j12-heng-direction]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4671:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4671:002 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The noble person observes thunder and wind working together to constitute Constancy and makes his virtue enduring. He establishes himself in the Way of the great mean and lasting constancy, without changing his proper direction or place.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4671:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4671:003 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4671:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4671:004 -->

**Lü Dalin:** Thunder and wind may seem inconstant, yet the manner in which they work together is constant.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4671:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4671:005 -->

**Hu Bingwen:** Though thunder and wind change, something unchanging remains. To embody their changes as the means of preserving what does not change in oneself is to embody thunder and wind well.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4671:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4671:006 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Those who explain this Image by “not becoming lost amid violent wind, thunder, and rain,” and those who explain Shock's Image by “changing one's demeanor at sudden thunder and violent wind,” are both mistaken. Thunder and wind are Heaven and Earth's changes without loss of their constant order. Standing firm without changing direction is the noble person's passage through countless changes without losing his constancy. Repeated thunder is Heaven and Earth's stirring energy; fearfulness, self-cultivation, and self-examination are the noble person's stirred heart.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4671:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4671:007 -->

### First Six: The misfortune of seeking depth in constancy: demanding depth at the beginning.[^j12-heng-depth]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4671:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4671:008 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** At Constancy's beginning, one demands too much from the person above. This is knowing constancy while utterly failing to assess the circumstances. Misfortune follows because yin's obscurity does not find what is appropriate to constancy.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4671:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4671:009 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4671:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4671:010 -->

**Zhu Zhen:** The first is at Xun's bottom and takes deep penetration for constancy; the top is at Zhen's utmost and takes agitation for constancy. Demanding depth at the beginning and delighting in movement at the top are both courses of misfortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4671:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4671:011 -->

**Guo Yong:** Progress in the Way must be gradual before it can endure. At Constancy's beginning, digging down and demanding depth is not that Way.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4671:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4671:012 -->

**Wang Shenzi:** A Way capable of enduring comes to fulfillment over time. Demanding depth at the beginning, applied to oneself, means that haste prevents attainment; applied to others, it means making demands too suddenly. Hence misfortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4671:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4671:013 -->

**Su Jun:** When people first undertake the work, an excessively sharp resolve and an overly urgent demand for results create the faults of haste and forced growth. Hence “demanding depth at the beginning.” Mencius's statement that deep attainment must proceed by the proper Way has precisely this meaning.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4671:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4671:014 -->

### Nine in the Second Place: Regret vanishes: it can remain enduringly in the mean.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4671:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4671:015 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Regret vanishes because it can remain constant and enduring in the mean. A person able to do so does more than merely dispel regret: this is goodness of virtue.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4671:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4671:016 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4671:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4671:017 -->

**Hu Bingwen:** Only the second nine is singled out as able to endure in the mean. From this one can see why the other lines, lacking the mean, do not endure.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4671:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4671:018 -->

### Nine in the Third Place: Not constant in one's virtue: there is nowhere to find a place.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4671:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4671:019 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Once a person lacks constancy, where can he find a place? If he cannot endure where he ought to remain, how could he endure where he has no rightful footing? Thus a person without constancy has nowhere to settle himself.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4671:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4671:020 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** “Nowhere to find a place” resembles Clinging's fourth line. Both mean that conduct has no constant measure and one behaves as though there were nowhere to settle. They do not mean that other people refuse to accommodate one.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4671:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4671:021 -->

### Nine in the Fourth Place: Enduring in a place not one's own: how could one catch game?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4671:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4671:022 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Occupying a place that is not one's own, what can one obtain, however long one stays? Hunting supplies the comparison; hence “how could one catch game?”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4671:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4671:023 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4671:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4671:024 -->

**Wang Bi:** Persevering in a place that is not one's own brings no gain, however much one labors.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4671:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4671:025 -->

### Six in the Fifth Place: A woman's steadfastness brings good fortune: following one to the end. A man determines conduct by what is right; following the woman's way brings misfortune.[^j12-heng-woman]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4671:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4671:026 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Following as the fifth follows the second is correct and fortunate for a woman. A woman takes following as correctness and compliance as virtue, and should preserve her following of one person to the end. A man, however, determines his conduct by rightness. Following a woman's Way brings misfortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4671:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4671:027 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4671:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4671:028 -->

**Xiang Anshi:** The second nine takes centered firmness as its constant course, and so regret vanishes. The fifth six takes centered yieldingness as constancy. This would be permissible in the second place; but in the fifth, one is husband, father, and ruler. Could it be permissible there? A woman following her husband has good fortune; a husband following his wife has misfortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4671:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4671:029 -->

**Yang Qixin:** The line statement says only that a woman has good fortune. The Image adds the word “steadfastness,” making clear that the steadfastness of being constant in one's virtue is here a woman's steadfastness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4671:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4671:030 -->

### Top Six: Agitated constancy at the top: utterly without accomplishment.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4671:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4671:031 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The Way of occupying the top requires enduring virtue before accomplishment is possible. With restless movement and no constancy, how could anything be achieved? Lack of constancy in one above brings grave misfortune. The Image further says that nothing can be established or completed; hence “utterly without accomplishment.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4671:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4671:032 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4671:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4671:033 -->

**Wang Anshi:** Ending in movement means taking movement itself for constancy. To take movement for constancy while standing above others does great harm.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4671:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4671:034 -->

**Wang Shenzi:** This is what is meant by the world being originally without trouble until an ordinary meddler disturbs it himself. Is it not the fault of coveting achievement and manufacturing affairs? Thus the sage checks it with “utterly without accomplishment”: agitation in a time for preserving constancy can assuredly accomplish nothing.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4671:034 -->

<a id="eee-4672"></a>

## 33. Dun 遯 — Retreat

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4672 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4672:001 -->

### Great Image: A mountain beneath Heaven: Retreat. Thus the noble person keeps petty people at a distance, without hostility, yet with dignity.[^j12-dun-distance]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4672:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4672:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** Heaven's expanse is without limit, while the mountain's height is limited: the image of Retreat. Dignity is the noble person's constant manner of preserving himself; petty people are then naturally unable to draw near.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4672:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4672:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** A mountain lies beneath Heaven. The mountain rises from below and then stops, while Heaven advances above and moves away from it: the image of withdrawing and avoiding. The noble person observes this image to withdraw from petty people and keep them at a distance. Harsh words and an angry expression would only provoke their resentment. The Way of keeping them distant lies solely in solemn bearing and dignified gravity, so that they know respect and apprehension and naturally remain distant.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4672:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4672:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4672:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4672:005 -->

**Shi Jie:** Without hostility, yet with dignity: compliant outwardly and correct within. If one prizes hostility, petty people become hateful; without dignity, the correct Way declines.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4672:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4672:006 -->

**Master Zhang:** The word translated “hostility” is read as in “hatred.” In distancing petty people, one must not show hatred. Hatred brings trouble upon oneself; how could one then keep distant? Dignity means treating petty people with respect while keeping them at a distance.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4672:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4672:007 -->

**Yang Shi:** A mountain beneath Heaven refuses nothing, accommodating even what is diseased or defective, yet ultimately nothing can overtop it. This images the noble person's keeping petty people distant without hostility, yet with dignity.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4672:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4672:008 -->

**Guo Yong:** In a time of Retreat, the noble person fears the harm petty people may do and intends only to distance himself. How is this done? By maintaining the distinction firmly without hating the person. Confucius says, “Hatred carried too far brings disorder.” Without hostility, there is no such hatred.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4672:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4672:009 -->

**Yu Yan:** What else does observing this image to keep petty people distant require, beyond upright conduct and modest words? Modest words are free of hostility and give no cause for resentment; upright conduct has an inviolable dignity that prevents disrespect. This is the noble person's Way of distancing petty people.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4672:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4672:010 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** To take the mountain as petty people and Heaven as the noble person does not seem entirely apt. A mountain beneath Heaven rises steeply toward Heaven. Its lofty steepness never rejects people, yet they cannot readily climb it. Hence the image of dignity without hostility. Yang's explanation appears to have this meaning.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4672:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4672:011 -->

### First Six: The danger of retreating at the tail: without going, what calamity could there be?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4672:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4672:012 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Recognizing the first signs and withdrawing early is certainly good. To be at the tail of the retreat is dangerous. Since going brings danger, it is better not to go but to remain hidden and obscure, thereby avoiding calamity: one is already in a perilous situation. Many ancients in low and obscure stations concealed themselves in disordered times without departing.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4672:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4672:013 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Cheng's Commentary takes not withdrawing as the means of escaping calamity; Zhu Xi takes remaining obscure and undertaking no action as the means. Thus, when Zhu Xi once wished to impeach Han Tuozhou, he obtained this line in divination and stopped.[^j12-dun-readings]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4672:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4672:014 -->

### Six in the Second Place: Holding fast with yellow oxhide: making the intention firm.[^j12-dun-binding]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4672:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4672:015 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Those above and below bind themselves firmly together through the Way of centered compliance. Their hearts and intentions are exceedingly steadfast, as though bound with oxhide.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4672:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4672:016 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4672:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4672:017 -->

**Hou Xingguo:** Responding above to the honored ruler, one intends to assist the age. Rather than withdraw with others, one alone preserves centered uprightness, firm as a binding of hide. No one can loosen one's hold upon this intention. The Shang's Elder Tutor corresponds to this line.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4672:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4672:018 -->

**Cai Qing:** This means making one's own intention firm: one cannot be enticed by the honor of a stipend.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4672:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4672:019 -->

**Supplement — Kong Yingda:** Making the intention firm means firmly securing the withdrawing person's intention so that he does not leave oneself.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4672:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4672:020 -->

### Nine in the Third Place: The danger of entangled retreat: illness and exhaustion. Keeping servants and concubines brings good fortune; great affairs cannot be undertaken.[^j12-dun-entanglement]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4672:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4672:021 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Retreat encumbered by attachments necessarily leads through exhaustion into danger. Its illness is exhaustion: its strength is already insufficient. Such intimate attachment may bring good fortune in keeping servants and concubines, but how could it meet the demands of great affairs?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4672:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4672:022 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4672:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4672:023 -->

**Zhang Qingzi:** When retreat is called for, attachment brings illness and danger, even exhaustion. Only by preserving oneself through firmness, restraining the two yin below, and keeping them in the manner appropriate to servants and concubines can one obtain good fortune. How could one undertake great affairs?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4672:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4672:024 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Inability to undertake great affairs means that one cannot yet carry out one's intentions directly, speaking and acting with unqualified uprightness. This connects with the Judgment's teaching about limited steadfastness and the Great Image's dignity without hostility.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4672:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4672:025 -->

### Nine in the Fourth Place: The noble person retreats despite attachment; the petty person cannot.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4672:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4672:026 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Though the noble person has attachments, he can retreat without departing from rightness. The petty person cannot overcome private inclination and falls into what is not good.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4672:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4672:027 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4672:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4672:028 -->

**Yu Yan:** The line statement says, “Retreat despite attachment: good fortune for the noble person, not for the petty person.” The line Commentary omits “good fortune” because it means that only the noble person can make such a retreat; the petty person cannot. Once one can retreat despite attachment, withdrawal brings success. Its good fortune need not be stated.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4672:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4672:029 -->

### Nine in the Fifth Place: Admirable retreat; steadfastness brings good fortune: making the intention correct.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4672:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4672:030 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** With a correct intention, action necessarily follows correctness; this makes the retreat admirable. Occupying the center, attaining correctness, and responding to centered correctness mean that the intention is correct. Hence good fortune. Whether withdrawing or remaining, a person need only make the intention correct.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4672:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4672:031 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4672:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4672:032 -->

**Master Zhang:** Correctly placed and centered, it can make its intention correct, thereby obtaining the good fortune of steadfastness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4672:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4672:033 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** The noble person's intention is not fixed on favor or profit. Thus he advances according to ritual propriety and retires according to rightness: this is making the intention correct.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4672:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4672:034 -->

### Top Nine: Ample retreat; nothing is unfavorable: nothing remains doubtful.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4672:034 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4672:035 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The retreat reaches far, with no hesitation or impediment. Being outside, it is already distant; having no correspondent, it has no encumbrance. Thus it is firm, decisive, and free of doubt.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4672:035 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4672:036 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4672:036 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4672:037 -->

**Hou Xingguo:** At the outermost limit, with no correspondent within, the heart has no hesitation or attachment. Rising far above the world, content with the time and free of distress, it enjoys ample retreat in which nothing is unfavorable.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4672:037 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4672:038 -->

**Zhao Rumei:** Among the four yang, the third is bound to yin and the fourth and fifth correspond to yin; all necessarily have some hesitation. Only at the top do doubts and anxieties disappear entirely. Thus nothing is unfavorable.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4672:038 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4672:039 -->

**Li Xinchuan:** “Nothing remains doubtful” is said both here and of Pushing Upward's third nine. Here one is decided upon withdrawal; there, upon advancement. Each is simply what its time requires.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4672:039 -->

<a id="eee-4673"></a>

## 34. Dazhuang 大壯 — Great Strength

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4673 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4673:001 -->

### Great Image: Thunder above Heaven: Great Strength. Thus the noble person treads no path contrary to ritual propriety.[^j12-strength-self-mastery]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4673:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4673:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** One who overcomes oneself is strong.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4673:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4673:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Thunder shakes above Heaven: great and strong. The noble person observes the image of Great Strength to put his own strength into practice. Nothing is greater in the noble person's strength than overcoming the self and returning to ritual propriety. The ancients said that overcoming oneself is called strength. Of harmonizing without drifting with the current, and standing in the center without leaning, the Mean says in each case, “How firm his strength!” Rushing into boiling water or fire and treading upon naked blades may be within a warrior's courage. But overcoming oneself and returning to propriety is possible only through the noble person's Great Strength. Hence “the noble person treads no path contrary to ritual propriety.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4673:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4673:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4673:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4673:005 -->

**Master Zhang:** No strength surpasses overcoming oneself and returning to propriety.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4673:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4673:006 -->

**Zhu Xi's Classified Conversations:** Thunder above Heaven is tremendously majestic. To overcome oneself, a person must be like thunder above Heaven; only then can what violates propriety be overcome.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4673:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4673:007 -->

**Xiang Anshi:** The noble person's nourishment of firm greatness consists simply in treading no path contrary to propriety.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4673:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4673:008 -->

### First Nine: Strength in the toes: its exhaustion is certain.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4673:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4673:009 -->

**Original Meaning.** This means that distress and exhaustion are inevitable.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4673:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4673:010 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Being at the very bottom yet using strength to advance, one can certainly expect exhaustion and misfortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4673:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4673:011 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4673:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4673:012 -->

**Wang Shenzi:** Occupying the lowest place yet employing strength, relying on firmness and resolutely advancing: its exhaustion and misfortune are certain indeed.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4673:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4673:013 -->

### Nine in the Second Place: Steadfastness brings good fortune: because it is centered.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4673:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4673:014 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** It has steadfast correctness and good fortune because it attains the middle Way. Centeredness does not lose correctness; how much more when there is yang firmness and the body of Qian!

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4673:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4673:015 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4673:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4673:016 -->

**Kong Yingda:** Centered and treading humbly, its conduct does not violate propriety. Thus it attains correctness and good fortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4673:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4673:017 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** The hexagram says that Great Strength benefits from steadfastness. The second nine alone has firm virtue, which is greatness; a vigorous trigram body, which is strength; and centeredness, which is the correctness for handling strength. It governs the hexagram. Thus the Commentary says “because it is centered,” showing that Great Strength's correctness lies in the mean.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4673:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4673:018 -->

### Nine in the Third Place: The petty person employs strength; the noble person does not.[^j12-strength-wang]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4673:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4673:019 -->

**Original Meaning.** The petty person is ruined through strength; the noble person is trapped through disregarding obstacles.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4673:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4673:020 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** In the petty person this is employment of forceful strength. In the noble person it is employment of disregard: a fiercely firm will, contempt for circumstances, and no consideration or apprehension.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4673:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4673:021 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4673:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4673:022 -->

**Xiang Anshi:** Explanations of “the noble person employs *wang*” differ. Yet consider the line statements' conventions: “good fortune for the petty person; the great person has success in obstruction”; “good fortune for the noble person, not for the petty person”; “good fortune for the woman; misfortune for the man.” All are contrasting expressions. Moreover, the Image's “the petty person employs strength; the noble person does not” has exactly the same construction as “the noble person retreats despite attachment; the petty person cannot.” In the Odes and Documents, *wang* is interchangeable with the negatives *fu*, *wu*, and *wu*, all having a prohibitive meaning.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4673:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4673:023 -->

**Yang Jian:** The third nine advances further. Although its position is strong, the noble person's heart pays no regard to that strength. Only the petty person takes pleasure in his own strong position, becoming ever more unrestrained and forceful. This is what is called employing strength. *Wang* means absence. The noble person's employment differs from the petty person's; hence “the petty person employs strength; the noble person does not.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4673:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4673:024 -->

**Gong Huan:** Great Strength is named for four yang growing in power. The third nine further occupies a yang place with yang and is excessively firm: strength upon strength. Such employment of strength is the petty person's behavior, not the noble person's Way. Hence “the noble person employs *wang*.” The Image explains it as “the petty person employs strength; the noble person does not,” with the same sense as Retreat's fourth nine, “the noble person retreats despite attachment; the petty person cannot.” Retreat's fourth nine is precisely the reversed counterpart of Great Strength's third nine; both speak of noble and petty people together.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4673:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4673:025 -->

**Yu Yan:** Confucius feared that later readers would see the two occurrences of “employs” in the line statement and suppose the noble person's employment identical with the petty person's. Therefore he expressly removed one.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4673:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4673:026 -->

### Nine in the Fourth Place: The hedge breaks open; no entanglement: it continues onward.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4673:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4673:027 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The growth of firm yang must reach its utmost. Though the fourth is already powerful, its advance has not stopped. Advancing with strength at the height of yang's vigor, it meets nothing able to resist it. The hedge breaks open and does not entangle or exhaust its strength. Continuing onward means that its advance does not cease.[^j12-strength-punctuation]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4673:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4673:028 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4673:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4673:029 -->

**Xiang Anshi:** The fourth nine occupies yieldingness with firmness. It has the good fortune of being able to remain correct and no regret of excessive firmness. “Steadfastness brings good fortune; regret vanishes” already says everything. Why add that the hedge breaks open without entanglement and strength lies in the great carriage's axle-binding? Lest people take occupying yieldingness to mean not advancing. Thus “it continues onward” makes the point clear.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4673:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4673:030 -->

### Six in the Fifth Place: Losing the ram through ease: its place is not appropriate.[^j12-strength-position]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4673:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4673:031 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Yielding harmony must be employed because yielding yin occupies the honored position. Were firm yang, centered and correct, in that position, no strength below would confront it. Because the fifth six is not appropriately placed, the meaning of losing the ram through ease is established. Broadly, one cannot govern excessive strength through firmness. The relative powers of ruler and ministers, above and below, are unequal. If the ruler's authority sufficiently controls those below, even forceful and overbearing people cannot be called a problem of strength. Only when the ruler's power falls short does one speak of governing strength. Therefore the Way of governing strength cannot rely on firmness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4673:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4673:032 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4673:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4673:033 -->

**Wang Anshi:** Firmness and yieldingness establish the foundation; adaptation and free passage answer the time. In answering the time, there are cases of an appropriately correct position bringing blame and misfortune, and an inappropriate position bringing no regret. In Great Strength's time, attaining the mean and handling matters through yieldingness can cause obstinacy to disappear.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4673:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4673:034 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** The Changes often borrows a line's appropriate or inappropriate position to explain whether its virtue suits its time and circumstances. Here “its place is not appropriate” means more than yin occupying yang and being unable to bear forceful strength. Four yang have already passed; thus the fifth does not occupy a place suited to further strength. Yielding centeredness occupies it, and so there is losing the ram through ease.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4673:034 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4673:035 -->

### Top Six: Unable to retreat or advance: insufficient deliberation. Difficulty brings good fortune: the fault does not endure.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4673:035 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4673:036 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Occupying a place not one's proper place makes both advance and retreat impossible. This is insufficient care and deliberation in placing oneself. Difficulty brings good fortune because yieldingness meets hardship and stands at Strength's end: it should now change of itself. Change restores the appropriate measure, so the fault does not endure. This is good fortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4673:036 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4673:037 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4673:037 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4673:038 -->

**Hu Bingwen:** Approach's third six and Great Strength's top six both say “nothing is beneficial,” and both say that the fault does not endure. The third six's concern and the top six's difficulty show that what is prized is not never erring, but correcting error.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4673:038 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4673:039 -->

**Yu Yan:** When people treat an affair as easy, they do not examine it carefully; when they treat it as difficult, they do. Former lack of careful examination brought fault. Now, with careful examination and without rashness, the fault does not endure.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4673:039 -->

<a id="eee-4674"></a>

## 35. Jin 晉 — Advance

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4674 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4674:001 -->

### Great Image: Brightness emerges above the earth: Advance. Thus the noble person makes his own luminous virtue shine.[^j12-jin-light]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4674:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4674:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** “Makes shine” means bringing to light.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4674:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4674:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** “Makes shine” means bringing to light. The tradition says, “Make virtue manifest and block what violates it,” and “make its measures manifest.” The noble person observes brightness emerging above the earth and growing ever more abundant, and makes his own luminous virtue shine. Removing obscurity and extending understanding illuminate virtue within oneself. Making luminous virtue shine throughout the world illuminates it outwardly. Here luminous virtue is made bright within oneself; hence “his own.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4674:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4674:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4674:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4674:005 -->

**Hu Bingwen:** Nothing surpasses Heaven in vigor; the noble person takes it as the model for strengthening himself. Nothing surpasses the sun in brightness; he takes it as the model for illuminating himself.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4674:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4674:006 -->

**Yu Yan:** Luminous virtue is the noble person's inherent virtue. Making it shine oneself means possessing this virtue oneself and bringing it to light oneself. Human virtue is originally bright. Human desires obscure it, so some darkness necessarily follows; yet its original brightness has never ceased. Knowing how to illuminate oneself makes that original brightness like the sun emerging above the earth. Its manifestation neither adds nor subtracts anything from it. The Great Learning's “making luminous virtue shine” and “illuminating oneself” have the same purport.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4674:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4674:007 -->

### First Six: Advancing, then checked: pursuing correctness alone. Spacious patience brings no blame: no commission has yet been received.[^j12-jin-commission]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4674:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4674:008 -->

**Original Meaning.** The first occupies a low position and has not yet received a commission to hold office.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4674:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4674:009 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Whether advancing or held back, one simply pursues the correct Way. Spacious patience brings no blame because one is only beginning to advance and has not yet assumed a position. The noble person's advancement or withdrawal may be slow or swift, but follows whatever rightness requires and is never without spaciousness. The sage feared that later people would misunderstand this and take neglecting an office's duties for spacious patience. Hence he expressly says that the first six is blameless in its patience because it is only beginning to advance and has received no commission to discharge official responsibilities. Someone entrusted with an office who lacks the superior's trust and cannot fulfill its duties must not remain a single day. Yet affairs cannot all be reduced to one rule: duration and speed depend upon the time, and there may also be signs that something can still be done.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4674:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4674:010 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4674:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4674:011 -->

**Master Liu:** In maintaining correctness, the noble person cannot alter his measure merely because others fail to recognize him.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4674:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4674:012 -->

**Zhang Zhenyuan:** “Pursuing correctness alone” traces the reason one is checked. Broadly, in the world, bending oneself makes agreement easy, while an upright Way is difficult to accommodate. It is precisely correctness that brings the rebuff. Yet how could a rebuff justify abandoning one's correctness? This and the line statement illuminate one another.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4674:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4674:013 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Not yet receiving the commission resembles Approach's second nine. Approach and Advance both concern the noble person's Way growing and approaching employment. Yet the noble person is not eager to seize an advantageous situation and hurry after the time. In approaching, he influences others through utmost sincerity, as though forgetting his power; in advancing, he preserves the Way at ease, as though prepared to remain so for life. Hence one says “not following the mandate” and the other “not yet receiving the commission.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4674:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4674:014 -->

### Six in the Second Place: Receiving this great blessing: because it is centered and correct.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4674:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4674:015 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** This great blessing is received through the Way of centered correctness. A person able to preserve that Way must find passage in time. How much more when great brightness above shares the same virtue: great blessing must follow.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4674:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4674:016 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4674:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4674:017 -->

**Yang Shi:** The second six occupies the company of yin with yielding compliance, yet alone has no correspondent: it is not recognized. Hence advancing with sorrow. Nevertheless, it occupies the center, preserves correctness, and acts from its own station; the spirits will bless it. The Odes says, “Reverently fulfill your place; cherish what is upright. The spirits will hear and grant you great blessing.” This is the meaning.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4674:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4674:018 -->

**He Kai:** The Erya says that one's father's deceased mother is called the *wangmu*. Small Excess's second six meeting the ancestress corresponds to the *wangmu* mentioned here. The second and fifth share virtue and correspond in position. The second receives great blessing because it occupies the center and attains correctness.[^j12-jin-ancestress]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4674:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4674:019 -->

### Six in the Third Place: The multitude trusts it: their intention advances upward.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4674:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4674:020 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Advancing upward means compliantly adhering above to great brightness. Following the greatly luminous ruler above is the shared intention of the multitude.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4674:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4674:021 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4674:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4674:022 -->

**Li Guo:** The first's lack of trust means that the multitude has not yet assented. The second's sorrow means regret still remains. The third's virtue wins the multitude's trust; it advances as desired and regret vanishes.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4674:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4674:023 -->

### Nine in the Fourth Place: The great rat; persistence brings danger: its position is not appropriate.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4674:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4674:024 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** A worthy person with correct virtue properly occupies high position. To occupy high position without correctness is to hold what is not rightfully one's own. Greedy and afraid of loss, one fears others. Persisting in such a position is plainly dangerous.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4674:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4674:025 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4674:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4674:026 -->

**Lu Xisheng:** Occupying a position not one's own, one clings to favor and stipend. The great rat's intention is only to steal and eat the grain.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4674:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4674:027 -->

### Six in the Fifth Place: Do not be anxious over loss and gain: going brings blessing.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4674:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4674:028 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** With the virtue of great brightness, one wins the adherence of those below. Extending sincerity and entrusting responsibilities to them can accomplish great work throughout the world. This is going forth and bringing blessing.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4674:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4674:029 -->

### Top Nine: Use it only to attack one's own settlement: the Way is not yet radiant.[^j12-jin-settlement]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4674:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4674:030 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Using it only to attack one's own settlement already gives good fortune without blame. Why also say that persistence brings humiliation? Because the Way is not yet radiant and great. Judged by correct principle, it remains humiliating. If the Way were radiant and great, everything would be centered and correct; how could there be excess? Here excessive firmness governs oneself. Though it achieves something, the Way is not yet radiant and great, and so there is still humiliation. The sage speaks of the Way of complete goodness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4674:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4674:031 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** The Way's lack of radiance traces the reason one's settlement must be attacked. At advancement's utmost, the Way must not yet be radiant: weighty power and position may encourage presuming upon completed achievements; entanglement in rank and stipend may sacrifice the resolve to pursue one's aspiration alone. Hence private partiality must be mastered. Only then can one stand high without danger and escape overreaching regret. Breakthrough's fifth-line “not yet radiant within” has the same meaning.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4674:031 -->

<a id="eee-4675"></a>

## 36. Mingyi 明夷 — Darkening of the Light

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4675 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4675:001 -->

### Great Image: Brightness enters the earth: Darkening of the Light. Thus the noble person governs the multitude by employing obscurity while remaining clear.[^j12-mingyi-governing]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4675:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4675:002 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Brightness illuminates, and nothing escapes the noble person's illumination. Yet excessive use of brightness becomes intrusive scrutiny. Excessive scrutiny searches everything to its limits and leaves no measure of spacious tolerance. Thus, observing brightness entering the earth, the noble person does not press discerning scrutiny to its utmost when governing the multitude, but employs obscurity. Only then can he accommodate others and harmonize the multitude, so that they feel close and secure. Employing obscurity is therefore the means of being clear. If he relies upon his own brightness and scrutinizes everything, he cannot master his own indignation and aversion and lacks the virtue of generous tolerance. People become estranged, suspicious, and uneasy. Losing the Way of governing the multitude, he thereby becomes unclear. The ancient sages established pendants before the eyes and screens before entrances because they did not wish illumination to exhaust what ought to remain hidden.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4675:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4675:003 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4675:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4675:004 -->

**Kong Yingda:** The crown's pendants hang before the eyes, and yellow floss stops the ears. With noninterference, clarity, and quiet, the people are transformed and do not deceive. But when a ruler puts his acuteness into operation and displays his cleverness, the people escape his close-woven net, and deceit and fraud increase. Is it not by hiding brightness and employing obscurity that he instead attains clarity?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4675:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4675:005 -->

**Master Zhang:** Not relying on minute scrutiny, yet not losing good government.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4675:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4675:006 -->

**Lin Xiyuan:** Employing obscurity while remaining clear does not mean taking obscurity for clarity, nor does it mean darkening one's own light. Though clear, one employs obscurity; though employing obscurity, one remains clear. It means simply not using one's brightness to the utmost. Used to the utmost, it becomes excessive scrutiny and loses the Way of spacious tolerance. Only by remaining clear while employing obscurity does one avoid both muddled darkness and prying cleverness. Though nothing is beyond illumination, some things are not illuminated exhaustively. This was the ancient sovereigns' art of governing the multitude.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4675:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4675:007 -->

**He Kai:** Darkening one's brightness means concealing light within obscurity. Remaining clear through obscurity means giving rise to clarity from obscurity. The meanings illuminate one another.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4675:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4675:008 -->

### First Nine: The noble person sets out: rightness may require going without food.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4675:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4675:009 -->

**Original Meaning.** Wherever rightness lies, going without food is permissible.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4675:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4675:010 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The noble person withdraws into concealment and suffers hardship because rightness requires it. Precisely because it is required, he rests content without distress. Even going without food is permissible.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4675:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4675:011 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4675:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4675:012 -->

**Wang Shenzi:** If rightness forbids accepting sustenance, it is also clear that rightness requires taking flight and going elsewhere. How could departure fail to be swift? Such were the affairs of Boyi and the Grand Duke.[^j12-mingyi-sustenance]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4675:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4675:013 -->

### Six in the Second Place: Its good fortune: compliance joined to a rule.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4675:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4675:014 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The second six obtains good fortune because it handles its situation compliantly while possessing a rule. The rule is the Way of centered correctness. Able to be compliant and attain centered correctness, it preserves good fortune in a time when brightness is injured.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4675:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4675:015 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4675:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4675:016 -->

**Xiang Anshi:** Among Darkening of the Light's lower three lines, only the second six sincerely seeks to rescue the situation. Among the upper three, only the fifth six has no intention of leaving. Both are centered and compliant ministers.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4675:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4675:017 -->

**Wang Shenzi:** Handling the situation through yielding compliance without losing the rule of centered correctness: was this not how King Wen employed the Way of Darkening of the Light?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4675:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4675:018 -->

### Nine in the Third Place: The intention of the southern hunt: greatly fulfilled.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4675:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4675:019 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** When brightness below removes darkness above, the intention is simply to eliminate harm. Did Tang of Shang and King Wu of Zhou have any intention of profiting from the world? Obtaining the great chief means removing harm and greatly fulfilling the intention. Were the intention otherwise, it would be an act of rebellion and disorder.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4675:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4675:020 -->

### Six in the Fourth Place: Entering the left side of the belly: obtaining the heart's intention.[^j12-mingyi-left-belly]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4675:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4675:021 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Entering the left side of the belly means approaching the ruler through a crooked and improper Way and gaining his heart and intention. Having gained his heart, one ensures that he never awakens to the truth.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4675:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4675:022 -->

### Six in the Fifth Place: Jizi's steadfastness: brightness cannot be extinguished.[^j12-mingyi-jizi]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4675:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4675:023 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Jizi concealed his brightness without losing steadfast firmness. Though he met adversity, his light endured of itself and could not be extinguished. To abandon what one preserves under the pressure of calamity would be to lose one's brightness: that is extinction. Among men of former times, Yang Xiong was such a case.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4675:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4675:024 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4675:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4675:025 -->

**Su Shi:** In relation to the top six, the fifth lacks the power to correct it and the strength to rescue it, while rightness forbids departing. This is the most difficult situation of all. Only someone like Jizi can occupy it. In such a situation his person may be humiliated, but his brightness cannot be extinguished.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4675:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4675:026 -->

### Top Six: First ascending to Heaven: illuminating the four regions. Later entering the earth: losing the rule.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4675:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4675:027 -->

**Original Meaning.** Illuminating the four regions is spoken of in terms of position.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4675:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4675:028 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** First ascending to Heaven means occupying a high and luminous position, from which illumination should extend throughout the four regions. Instead brightness is injured and becomes dark: this is later entering the earth and losing the Way of brightness. Losing the rule means losing that Way.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4675:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4675:029 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4675:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4675:030 -->

**Hu Bingwen:** The rule is principle that must not be overstepped. Losing the rule makes one a Zhou; following the rule makes one a King Wen.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4675:030 -->

<a id="eee-4676"></a>

## 37. Jiaren 家人 — The Family

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4676 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4676:001 -->

### Great Image: Wind comes forth from fire: The Family. Thus the noble person's words have substance and his conduct has constancy.[^j12-family-words]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4676:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4676:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** When the person is cultivated, the household is well governed.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4676:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4676:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The foundation of setting the household right is setting oneself right. In doing so, not one word or movement may be treated lightly. Observing wind coming forth from fire, the noble person knows that affairs proceed from within to without. Therefore his words must have substance and his conduct constancy. Substance means the reality of affairs; constancy means enduring measures and rules. Virtuous achievement manifest outside comes from careful speech and conduct within. With speech attended to and conduct cultivated, the person is correct and the household governed.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4676:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4676:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4676:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4676:005 -->

**Kong Yingda:** Substance means affairs. Words must be grounded in affairs: nothing the mouth says need be rejected. Conduct must be constant: nothing the person does need be rejected. Setting the household right is cultivated in what is near and small. Speech and conduct are the noble person's hinge and spring. Going forth from oneself, they reach others; beginning nearby, they transform what is distant. Hence speech and conduct are singled out for admonition.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4676:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4676:006 -->

**Yang Shi:** Loyal and trustworthy words have substance; earnest and reverent conduct has constancy.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4676:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4676:007 -->

**Hu Bingwen:** Wind comes forth from fire; a household's transformation comes forth from my speech and conduct. Both proceed from within to without, naturally permeating their surroundings and bringing transformation about.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4676:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4676:008 -->

**Yu Yan:** The Way of ordering the household begins with cultivating oneself. This is why wind coming forth from fire is the image of The Family. Knowing the wind's source, the noble person makes self-cultivation the foundation of household order, and speech and conduct the first concerns of self-cultivation. Words must have substance and be without falsity; conduct must have constancy and not change its standard. Substance means actuality: sincere and truthful speech has substance, insincere speech does not. Constancy means a lasting measure: enduring conduct has constancy, inconstant conduct does not.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4676:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4676:009 -->

### First Nine: Setting bounds within the household: its intentions have not yet changed.[^j12-family-bounds]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4676:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4676:010 -->

**Original Meaning.** Guard against deviation in advance, while intentions have not yet changed.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4676:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4676:011 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Set bounds at the beginning, before the household's intentions shift. Establishing bounds while correct intentions have not yet dispersed or changed avoids injuring affection or losing rightness. This is good handling of a household, and regret therefore vanishes. Governing only after intentions have changed causes much injury and brings regret.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4676:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4676:012 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4676:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4676:013 -->

**Su Shi:** Forget the bounds and intentions change. Establish them before that change, and regret vanishes.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4676:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4676:014 -->

**Yang Jian:** In governing the household, safeguards should be established at the beginning. Restrain its members through ritual propriety before their hearts and intentions change; then crooked intentions have no occasion to arise.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4676:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4676:015 -->

### Six in the Second Place: Its good fortune: compliance joined to deference.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4676:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4676:016 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The second, yielding yin in a centered and correct place, can follow compliantly and remain humble. Hence the woman's steadfastness brings good fortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4676:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4676:017 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** The second and fourth sixes are alike in compliance, which is the woman's correctness. The fourth occupies a higher position, so it is called compliance in its place. The second occupies a lower position, so it is called compliance joined to deference.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4676:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4676:018 -->

### Nine in the Third Place: A stern household: the Way has not been lost. Wife and children laughing heedlessly: the household's measure is lost.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4676:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4676:019 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Though stern, the household has not gone greatly astray from the Way of household government. But heedless laughter among wife and children means absence of ritual and rule, and loss of the household's measure. The household must then fall into disorder.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4676:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4676:020 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4676:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4676:021 -->

**Wang Bi:** Yang occupying yang is firm and stern. At the lower trigram's utmost, it is the head of a household. In conduct, excess of respect is preferable to carelessness; in the household, excess of strictness is preferable to irreverent disorder. Thus, although a stern household has regret and danger, it still attains the Way. Heedless laughter among wife and children instead loses its measure.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4676:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4676:022 -->

### Six in the Fourth Place: Enriching the household brings great good fortune: compliance occupies its place.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4676:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4676:023 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Deferential compliance occupies a correct position. Correctly placed and compliant, it can preserve its wealth. This is the great good fortune of enriching the household.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4676:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4676:024 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4676:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4676:025 -->

**Yu Yan:** The Evolution of Ritual says, “Devotion between father and son, concord between brothers, harmony between husband and wife: this is the household's abundance.” How could good fortune mean merely having much money? Occupy wealth compliantly, and fullness does not overflow. One can preserve the household and long maintain its prosperity. What good fortune could be greater?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4676:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4676:026 -->

### Nine in the Fifth Place: The king brings his household to fulfillment: they love one another.[^j12-family-mutual-love]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4676:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4676:027 -->

**Original Meaning.** Master Cheng says: the husband loves her assistance within, and the wife loves his example in ordering the household.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4676:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4676:028 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The Way of a king bringing his household to fulfillment is not merely making its members obey. Their hearts must be transformed and sincerely united. The husband loves her assistance within; the wife loves his example in ordering the household. This is mutual love. Was not King Wen's consort capable of this? If the person is cultivated and rules established but the household is not transformed, the Way of bringing the household to fulfillment has not been attained.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4676:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4676:029 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4676:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4676:030 -->

**Guo Yong:** Father as father, son as son, elder brother as elder brother, younger brother as younger brother, husband as husband, wife as wife: all sharing great accord without opposition. This is the meaning of mutual love.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4676:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4676:031 -->

**Gong Huan:** Mutual love means that in a household, fathers and sons, brothers, husbands and wives, elders and juniors all love one another. It is not confined to husband and wife.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4676:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4676:032 -->

### Top Nine: The good fortune of dignified authority: this means turning back upon oneself.[^j12-family-authority]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4676:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4676:033 -->

**Original Meaning.** It does not mean putting on a display of authority. Turn back upon oneself and govern oneself; others will then respect and follow.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4676:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4676:034 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The Way of household government rests upon correcting oneself, hence “turning back upon oneself.” The line statement says that governing a household requires dignified authority. Confucius further warns that one should first be strict with oneself. If authority is not first practiced upon oneself, others resent it and will not submit. Thus authority bringing good fortune means being able to turn back upon one's own person. As Mencius says, if one does not personally practice the Way, it will not prevail with wife and children.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4676:034 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4676:035 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4676:035 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4676:036 -->

**Zhu Zhen:** Authority is not sought outside: simply turn back upon oneself. Self-examination brings correctness; correctness brings sincerity; sincerity commands awe without anger. Later people do not understand that dignified authority means correcting oneself. Some remain incorrect yet prize angry displays of power. Father and son then injure one another, and submission recedes still further. How could there be good fortune?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4676:036 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4676:037 -->

**Guo Yong:** The Great Image explains words with substance and conduct with constancy; here the text again speaks of turning back upon oneself. What brings the household's Way from beginning to completion is simply self-cultivation.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4676:037 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4676:038 -->

**Zhao Rumei:** The lines speak of bounds at the beginning, sternness in the third, and authority at the top. The sage feared that later people would take this as excessive severity with insufficient affection and harmony. Therefore he expressly explains it as turning back upon oneself. Dignified authority does not mean creating awe through harshness, but simply seeking the cause within oneself.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4676:038 -->

<a id="eee-4677"></a>

## 38. Kui 睽 — Opposition

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4677 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4677:001 -->

### Great Image: Fire above, lake below: Opposition. Thus the noble person shares what is common while remaining distinct.[^j12-kui-difference]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4677:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4677:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** The two trigrams form one figure, yet their natures differ.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4677:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4677:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Fire above and lake below have contrary natures, and so form the image of separation and opposition. Observing it, the noble person knows what ought to remain distinct within great commonality. In their life in the world, sages and worthy people share fully in the constant principles of human relations. Yet they sometimes stand alone against what worldly convention takes for common ground. They share the constant nature bestowed on humanity, but differ from the errors of customary practice. Those unable to share in great commonality disorder the constants and violate principle; those unable to stand apart follow convention and practice what is wrong. The essential point is to share while retaining the capacity to differ. The Mean calls this harmonizing without drifting with the current.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4677:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4677:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4677:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4677:005 -->

**Xun Shuang:** Fire rises by nature, and a lake moistens downward; hence Opposition. Though the great destination is shared, lesser affairs should differ. The hundred offices have distinct duties, the four classes of people different occupations; civil and military means are employed together, authority and virtue work in contrasting ways. All return together to good government. Hence the noble person shares what is common while remaining distinct.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4677:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4677:006 -->
