**Xiang Anshi:** Commonality images Dui's joy; distinction images Li's clarity.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4677:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4677:007 -->

### First Nine: Seeing the bad person: to avoid blame.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4677:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4677:008 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** In a time of estrangement, people's feelings diverge, and one already suffers from being unable to achieve harmony. If one rejects people simply because they are bad, they will gather in hostility toward the noble person, and calamity and blame will arrive. Thus one must see them, to avoid resentment and blame. Without resentment and blame, there remains a Way toward union.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4677:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4677:009 -->

### Nine in the Second Place: Meeting the ruler in a lane: the Way has not been lost.[^j12-kui-lane]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4677:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4677:010 -->

**Original Meaning.** It is grounded in their proper correspondence, not in anything crooked.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4677:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4677:011 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** In a time of Opposition, when the ruler's heart is not yet in accord, the worthy minister below exhausts his strength and sincerity, seeking only to bring about trust and union. He moves the ruler through utmost sincerity, supports him with all his strength, clarifies right principles to extend his understanding, and removes obscuration and delusion to make his intention sincere. In such flexible ways he seeks agreement. Meeting does not mean bending the Way to flatter; the lane is not a crooked shortcut. Hence Confucius expressly says, “Meeting the ruler in a lane: the Way has not been lost.” *Wei* means “not necessarily”: the action need not mean losing the Way.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4677:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4677:012 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4677:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4677:013 -->

**Wang Shenzi:** When above and below are estranged, one cannot avoid adapting one's approach to seek union. Thus the text says that the Way has not been lost: there has been no departure from the correct Way.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4677:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4677:014 -->

### Six in the Third Place: Seeing the carriage dragged back: its position is not appropriate. No beginning, but an ending: meeting firmness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4677:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4677:015 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** A six in the third place is not correct. Incorrectness brings insecurity, and it also stands between two yang. Such difficulties arise from its inappropriate position. No beginning but an ending means that it will ultimately meet and unite with the top nine: this is meeting firmness. Union without correctness never endures without separation. Union through the correct Way, however, cannot remain forever estranged. Thus the worthy follow principle and proceed peacefully; the wise recognize the first signs and hold firmly.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4677:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4677:016 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4677:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4677:017 -->

**Hu Yuan:** No beginning but an ending through meeting firmness means that the top initially suspects it, but ultimately recognizes its sincerity and responds. The third six meets someone firm and clear.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4677:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4677:018 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** When a line has two comparisons and the Image singles out one, it selects the weightier. Here it selects the carriage dragged back because the line rides upon firmness. In Oppression's third line it selects sitting upon thorn-bushes for the same reason. In the Changes' conventions, the danger of riding upon firmness is especially severe.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4677:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4677:019 -->

### Nine in the Fourth Place: Mutual trust brings no blame: the intention can be carried out.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4677:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4677:020 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The first and fourth are both yang-firm noble people. In a time of estrangement, above and below meet with utmost sincerity, unite intentions, and combine strength. Their intentions can then be realized, not merely spared blame. The text says only “no blame”; Confucius further makes clear that their intention can be carried out to remedy the age's estrangement. With the noble person's yang-firm ability and mutually supporting utmost sincerity, what could not be accomplished? Only with a noble person can the intention be carried out.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4677:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4677:021 -->

### Six in the Fifth Place: One's kindred bites through the skin: going brings blessing.[^j12-kui-skin]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4677:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4677:022 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The line statement merely says that when one's kindred bites through the skin, one may go without blame. The Image unfolds the meaning further: even if the ruler's own ability is insufficient, trusting and employing a worthy helper allows that helper's Way to penetrate deeply into him. He can then accomplish something. This is going and receiving blessing.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4677:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4677:023 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4677:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4677:024 -->

**Xiang Anshi:** The second takes the fifth as ruler and finds an adaptable way to enter his confidence. Though a lane bends, it leads to the thoroughfare. Meeting the ruler in a lane seeks to put the Way into practice, not to act crookedly. The fifth takes the second as kindred and draws close. The second and fifth correspond through the middle Way. In a time of Opposition, the barrier between them is slight and union easy, like soft skin penetrated by a bite. There will be not only no blame but also blessing. The second's and fifth's yin and yang correspond correctly; hence these statements.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4677:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4677:025 -->

**He Kai:** Once one's kindred has bitten through the skin, going brings the blessing of union. The statement resolves the uncertainty.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4677:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4677:026 -->

### Top Nine: The good fortune of meeting rain: all doubts disappear.[^j12-kui-doubts]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4677:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4677:027 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Rain is yin and yang in harmony. Beginning in estrangement but ending in harmony brings good fortune. Harmony becomes possible because all doubts disappear. At the beginning, estrangement leaves nothing unsuspected: hence “all doubts.” Once extremity turns to union, they all disappear.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4677:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4677:028 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4677:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4677:029 -->

**Kong Yingda:** All doubts disappear when one goes to unite with the third, like the harmony of rain. The former suspicions of a pig, ghosts, and a drawn bow all dissolve together. Hence “all doubts disappear.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4677:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4677:030 -->

**Wang Anshi:** At Opposition's utmost, the top nine has a correspondent yet doubts it. In extreme estrangement, things may seem true and yet be false; even clarity remains suspicious. When suspicion grows excessive, one takes what is absent to be present and stops at nothing. How much more so without clarity! The top nine's firmness exceeds the mean, and its use of clarity goes too far. Thus it cannot avoid doubt at the beginning.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4677:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4677:031 -->

**Zhu Xi's Classified Conversations:** In establishing the line images, the sage must have had grounds; they were not composed out of nothing. We simply cannot now investigate those grounds. Only with Confucius do we find that the images themselves are no longer explained. For such things as the mud-covered pig and the carriage loaded with ghosts, he says only that all doubts disappear. This shows that the many preceding images are simply bewildering, doubtful circumstances. Later explanations are often forced.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4677:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4677:032 -->

**Zhao Rumei:** Strange events, feats of force, disorder, and spirits are what the sage did not discuss, yet this hexagram speaks of them in detail. Therefore the sage judges them to be doubt. When the heart doubts, such scenes appear; when the heart is clear, doubt disappears. For someone who understands this, books of marvels may be burned and arguments denying spirits may fall silent.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4677:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4677:033 -->

**Wang Shenzi:** Isolation arises from estrangement, and estrangement from doubt. Now that all doubts have disappeared, estrangement gives way to union, and union to harmony. Hence good fortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4677:033 -->

<a id="eee-4678"></a>

## 39. Jian 蹇 — Limping

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4678 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4678:001 -->

### Great Image: Water upon the mountain: Limping. Thus the noble person turns back upon himself and cultivates virtue.[^j12-jian-self-examination]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4678:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4678:002 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The mountain is steep and obstructing, and above it there is water. Kan's water images danger and entrapment. With obstructions above and below, there is Limping. The noble person observes the image of difficulty and turns back upon himself to cultivate virtue. On meeting obstacles, he necessarily seeks their cause within himself and increases his self-cultivation. Mencius says, “When one's actions fail to attain their aim, in every case seek the cause within oneself.” Thus in hardship he must examine whether some fault in himself has brought it about: this is turning back upon himself. Whatever is not yet good, he corrects; if his heart has nothing to reproach itself with, he redoubles his effort. This is cultivating his own virtue. The noble person simply cultivates virtue and awaits the time.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4678:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4678:003 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4678:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4678:004 -->

**Lü Dalin:** Water upon the mountain cannot flow freely: it has not found suitable ground, and so there is Limping. Water unable to find its proper course resembles the noble person's conduct failing to win a response from others. Failing to win that response, he simply seeks the cause within himself. Thus, if loving others brings no affection, he examines his benevolence; if governing others brings no order, he examines his wisdom; if treating others courteously brings no answer, he examines his reverence.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4678:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4678:005 -->

**Zhu Xi's Classified Conversations:** Pan Qianzhi's letter says that Limping resembles Oppression, and that committing one's life to fulfill one's intention is the same as turning back upon oneself to cultivate virtue. He does not realize that this is not so. The Image says, “A lake without water: Oppression.” At oppression's utmost, when nothing can be done, one can only commit one's life to fulfill one's intention. With Limping, however, further progress remains possible. Like a spring upon a mountain, its course winds through many obstacles, yet can still proceed. Thus people are taught to turn back upon themselves and cultivate virtue. Simply compare “a lake without water” with “water upon the mountain”: they are entirely different.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4678:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4678:006 -->

**Xiang Anshi:** Turning back upon oneself images Gen's back; cultivating virtue images Kan's labor.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4678:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4678:007 -->

### First Six: Going brings difficulty; coming brings praise: it is fitting to wait.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4678:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4678:008 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** At difficulty's beginning, advancement only increases the difficulty: the time does not yet permit advancing. One should therefore recognize the first signs and stop, waiting until action is timely before acting. All the lines regard going as difficult and coming as good. Does this leave no meaning of escaping difficulty? Answer: going while within difficulty is difficult; when difficulty ends, it changes. Thus the top line already contains the meaning of greatness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4678:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4678:009 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4678:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4678:010 -->

**Wang Shenzi:** Going into danger is less fitting than remaining in ease and awaiting the time.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4678:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4678:011 -->

**Gong Huan:** At the beginning of Keeping Still, danger is still distant. To stop immediately upon seeing danger is what the Judgment Commentary calls wisdom.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4678:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4678:012 -->

### Six in the Second Place: The king's servant meets difficulty upon difficulty: in the end, no reproach.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4678:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4678:013 -->

**Original Meaning.** Even if the affair is not accomplished, there is nothing to reproach.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4678:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4678:014 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Though hard pressed in a difficult time, the intention is to relieve the ruler's trouble. Even without success, in the end there is no fault to reproach. The sage values the intention's rightness and declares it free of reproach, thereby encouraging wholehearted loyalty.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4678:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4678:015 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4678:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4678:016 -->

**Hou Xingguo:** The second responds above to the fifth, which is within Kan: danger upon danger. Its intention is to correct and support the ruler, not to spare itself. Hence “the king's servant meets difficulty upon difficulty, not for his own sake.” Assisting the ruler in this way brings no reproach in the end.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4678:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4678:017 -->

### Nine in the Third Place: Going brings difficulty; coming is a return: those within rejoice in it.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4678:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4678:018 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Those within are the yin below. In a time of difficulty, yielding yin cannot stand independently; all therefore adhere to the third nine's yang and welcome it. A nine occupying the third finds its proper place amid difficulty. Winning the hearts of those below can secure peace. Thus coming is called a return, like the Spring and Autumn Annals' use of “return home.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4678:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4678:019 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4678:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4678:020 -->

**Kong Yingda:** Of the inner trigram's three lines, only the third nine is yang. Above the two yin, it is what those within rely upon. Hence they rejoice in it.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4678:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4678:021 -->

### Six in the Fourth Place: Going brings difficulty; coming brings union: in the proper place, there is substance.[^j12-jian-substance]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4678:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4678:022 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** In a difficult time, the fourth occupies a high position but comes back rather than going forward, sharing the intentions of those below. This is certainly enough to win the multitude. Moreover, yin occupies yin and thus attains its substance. Meeting those below with sincerity and substance, it can join with them and place itself beneath them. The second and third likewise each attain their substance, as does the first, yin occupying the lowest place. In shared adversity, meeting one another with substance assuredly brings union. Thus coming brings union because the position is occupied with substance. Amid difficulty, how could anything be accomplished without sincerity? The text calls the position not “correct” but “substantial” because relations between above and below rest upon sincerity. Each expression has its proper application.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4678:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4678:023 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4678:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4678:024 -->

**Xun Shuang:** Correctly placed and receiving yang above, it is therefore said that in the proper place there is substance.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4678:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4678:025 -->

**Shen Gai:** The fourth is appropriately placed and may advance, yet yielding yin cannot accomplish the task alone. Coming to receive the fifth and joining yang's substance, it finds the one it should assist.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4678:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4678:026 -->

**Jiang Bao:** Yin adjoins yang, and yang is substantial; hence the expression. Cheng's taking “substance” to mean sincerity is not right.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4678:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4678:027 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Xun, Shen, and Jiang are all correct. On this reading, however, “in the proper place” should be applied to the fifth nine: the occupant of the honored position has substantial virtue. This resembles the expression “the opponent is firm.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4678:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4678:028 -->

### Nine in the Fifth Place: Great difficulty; companions come: it meets the right measure.[^j12-jian-measure]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4678:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4678:029 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Companions are those of its own kind. The fifth has centered and correct virtue, as does the second. Even in great difficulty, they do not abandon what they preserve. Each, amid difficulty, responds to and assists the other through centered and correct integrity. When above and below are centered and correct yet cannot accomplish the task, it is because the minister's ability is insufficient. Have there been few who held to integrity and rightness yet lacked the ability to succeed? Li Gu and Wang Yun of Han, and Zhou Yi and Wang Dao of Jin, were such men.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4678:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4678:030 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4678:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4678:031 -->

**Kong Yingda:** Appropriately placed and centered, it does not alter its integrity. Those sharing its intention therefore come from afar. Hence companions come.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4678:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4678:032 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Limping's meaning lies in advancing or stopping appropriately; the lines' going and coming mean advancement and stopping. Although the fifth nine does not mention going or coming, the Commentary makes clear that it meets the right measure. Thus neither advancement nor stopping loses what is fitting, and difficulty can be overcome without recklessly courting it. Pei Du said that when the court handles matters appropriately, it can win their hearts. Is this not meeting the right measure?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4678:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4678:033 -->

### Top Six: Going brings difficulty; coming brings greatness: the intention is directed inward. Seeing a great person is beneficial: following the honored one.[^j12-jian-inward]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4678:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4678:034 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The top six responds to the third and follows the fifth: its intention is directed inward. Difficulty has reached its utmost and assistance is present; hence greatness and good fortune. Yielding yin at difficulty's utmost is closely adjacent to a ruler who is yang-firm, centered, and correct. Its intention naturally adheres to him to seek its own deliverance. Thus seeing a great person is beneficial: it follows the fifth nine's honored position. The words “following the honored one” are used lest people fail to understand that the great person refers to the fifth.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4678:034 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4678:035 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4678:035 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4678:036 -->

**Su Shi:** Both “inward” and “the honored one” refer to the fifth.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4678:036 -->

<a id="eee-4679"></a>

## 40. Jie 解 — Release

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4679 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4679:001 -->

### Great Image: Thunder and rain arise: Release. Thus the noble person pardons errors and mitigates punishments for offenses.[^j12-release-clemency]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4679:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4679:002 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Heaven and Earth open and disperse, producing thunder and rain; thus their arising constitutes Release. The wording differs from that of Clinging, where brightness arises twice. To pardon is to release; to mitigate is to deal leniently. Errors may be pardoned, but pardoning deliberate wrongdoing would not accord with rightness; it should therefore only be treated leniently. Observing thunder and rain arising in Release, the noble person embodies their generation and nourishment by bestowing benevolence and kindness, and embodies their loosening and dispersal by practicing leniency and release.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4679:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4679:003 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4679:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4679:004 -->

**Kong Yingda:** Pardoning means setting free; errors are inadvertent faults. Mitigation means leniency; offenses are deliberate transgressions. Lighter faults are pardoned and graver offenses treated leniently. Both express loosening and release.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4679:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4679:005 -->

**Zhao Rumei:** Thunder is Heaven's authority; rain is Heaven's bounty. Bounty within authority resembles pardon and mitigation within penal justice.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4679:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4679:006 -->

### First Six: Firmness and yieldingness meet: by rightness, no blame.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4679:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4679:007 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The first and fourth correspond: firmness and yieldingness meet and join. Their meeting attains what is fitting. After difficulty has been released, handling the situation with firmness and yieldingness appropriately brings no blame in principle.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4679:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4679:008 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4679:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4679:009 -->

**Cai Yuan:** Yieldingness at Release's beginning receives firmness above and responds to firmness. Attaining the fitting relation between firm and yielding, it must find release from difficulty. Hence “by rightness, no blame.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4679:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4679:010 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** The first, occupying the innermost and rearmost place, embodies coming back and returning, and so has no blame. Confucius feared that people would take this as doing absolutely nothing; therefore he supplements the meaning through following yang. Its position at the rear follows the same convention as Retreat's first line.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4679:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4679:011 -->

### Nine in the Second Place: Steadfastness brings good fortune: attaining the middle Way.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4679:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4679:012 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The good fortune of steadfastness means attaining the middle Way. Removing crookedness and evil so that centered uprightness may prevail is correct and fortunate.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4679:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4679:013 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Yellow is centeredness; the arrow is straightness. The minister's Way certainly rests upon uprightness. Yet without the mean, uprightness has often stirred up calamity through hatred of evil and efforts to remove the crooked. Attaining the middle Way directly explains obtaining the yellow arrow.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4679:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4679:014 -->

### Six in the Third Place: Carrying a burden while riding in a carriage: shameful indeed. Bringing armed attack upon oneself: whom else could one blame?[^j12-release-burden]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4679:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4679:015 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** A burden-bearer who also rides in a carriage is shameful. Occupying what is not rightfully one's place, with virtue unequal to the vessel one employs, one invites raiders and armed attack oneself. Whom could one blame? In the Appended Statements, the sage further explains how raiders are attracted, asking whether those who made the Changes understood thieves. Thieves arrive by exploiting an opening. Without an opening, how could they attack? Carrying burdens is the petty person's work; riding is the noble person's equipment. A petty person riding in the noble person's equipment cannot rest securely there, so thieves exploit the opening and seize it. A petty person occupying the noble person's position cannot bear it. Hence arrogance and presumption lead him to insult superiors and oppress those below. Thieves exploit these faults and attack him. Attacking means proclaiming his crimes; thieves are those who arrive with violent force. To have valuables but be careless about storing them is to instruct thieves to take them. For a woman to make her appearance alluring is to instruct the lustful to assault her. For a petty person to ride in the noble person's equipment is to invite thieves to seize it. All are described as bringing it upon oneself.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4679:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4679:016 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4679:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4679:017 -->

**Lei Si:** The petty person regards carrying a burden while riding as an honor; the noble person regards it as shameful. Hence it is disgraceful. Raiders on a small scale are thieves; on a large scale they are armies. Employing unsuitable people turns Release into Limping and raises armed conflict throughout the world.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4679:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4679:018 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Lei's explanation captures the intention both of this Commentary and of the Appended Statements. The former's bringing armed attack upon oneself and the latter's “thieves attack him” both concern those who possess states and domains.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4679:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4679:019 -->

### Nine in the Fourth Place: Release your big toe: the position is not yet appropriate.[^j12-release-toe]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4679:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4679:020 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Though the fourth is yang-firm, it occupies yin and may fall short of correctness. If it further associates intimately with petty people, it will certainly lose correctness. Thus it must release its big toe before noble people can come, for the position it occupies is not yet appropriate. Release means separating what was originally joined. Only after releasing the toe can companions trust: if petty people are allowed between oneself and noble companions, sincerity toward those companions is not yet complete.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4679:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4679:021 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4679:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4679:022 -->

**Zheng Ruxie:** The fourth places itself inappropriately, and petty people naturally attach themselves to it. It must release them before it can gain its companions' trust. Companions are the firm yang kind; the big toe is yin below.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4679:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4679:023 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Its virtue is neither centered nor correct, while it corresponds to the first and adjoins the third. Hence its position is not yet appropriate.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4679:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4679:024 -->

### Six in the Fifth Place: The noble person achieves release: petty people withdraw.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4679:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4679:025 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** What the noble person releases is the presence of petty people, causing them to withdraw. When petty people depart, the noble person's Way prevails. Hence good fortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4679:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4679:026 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4679:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4679:027 -->

**Wu Yueshen:** If the noble person can achieve release, petty people withdraw. If they have not withdrawn, he has not yet achieved release. Their withdrawal verifies his release. Though sincerity is not mentioned, its meaning is clear.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4679:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4679:028 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Following Zheng's explanation, one must say that if the noble person truly achieves release, even petty people trust him, change their hearts and conduct, and withdraw of themselves without waiting to be dismissed or suppressed.[^j12-release-withdrawal]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4679:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4679:029 -->

### Top Six: The duke uses his bow to shoot the falcon: to release what is rebellious.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4679:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4679:030 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** What remains unreleased at Release's end is grave rebellious disorder. Shooting it brings release; with release, the world is at peace.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4679:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4679:031 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4679:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4679:032 -->

**Wu Yueshen:** The world's difficulties are created by petty people: partisan attachments like the toe, crooked flattery like the foxes, predatory harm like the falcon. Release the toe, capture the foxes, shoot the falcon, and difficulty is released. Thus removing petty people is Release's essential meaning.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4679:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4679:033 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Before the fifth line, those being released are collectively called petty people. Here the word is “rebellious”: the distinction is between internal and external disorder. Under the lord of Yu, Gonggong and Huandou represented internal disorder, and the Three Miao external disorder.[^j12-release-rebellion]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4679:033 -->

<a id="eee-4680"></a>

## 41. Sun 損 — Decrease

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4680 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4680:001 -->

### Great Image: A lake beneath the mountain: Decrease. Thus the noble person checks anger and restrains desire.[^j12-decrease-anger]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4680:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4680:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** In the noble person's self-cultivation, nothing more urgently requires diminishing than these.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4680:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4680:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** A lake beneath the mountain communicates its energy upward to moisten what is above; deepening what lies below also increases the height above. Both image decreasing below. Observing Decrease, the noble person diminishes what is within himself. In self-cultivation, what should be diminished is anger and desire. Thus he disciplines his wrath and blocks the course of his desires.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4680:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4680:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4680:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4680:005 -->

**Yu Fan:** Dui is joy, hence checking anger; Gen is stopping, hence restraining desire.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4680:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4680:006 -->

**Kong Yingda:** Checking puts an end to what has already occurred; restraining closes the way to what is about to come. The two expressions complement one another, each supplying what the other leaves implicit.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4680:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4680:007 -->

**Yang Shi:** Decrease is the cultivation of virtue. What should be diminished is simply anger and desire. Thus the nine considerations begin with seeing, hearing, demeanor, and speech, and end with considering difficulty when angry and rightness when seeing something to gain.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4680:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4680:008 -->

**Zhu Xi's Classified Conversations:** Question: “Asked how desire can be restrained, Yichuan answered, ‘Thought.’ Does this mean that as soon as desire stirs, one should think of ritual propriety and rightness to overcome it?” Answer: “Yes.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4680:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4680:009 -->

**Wang Shenzi:** Harmonious joy leaves no anger; knowing where to stop leaves no desire. Hence this is the essential work of cultivating virtue.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4680:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4680:010 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Whenever a Great Image pairs its teaching with the two trigrams' virtues, the inner comes before the outer. Thus Yu's explanation should be followed. Increase's Image does likewise.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4680:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4680:011 -->

### First Nine: Cease one's affairs and go swiftly: joining intentions with the one above.[^j12-decrease-swiftly]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4680:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4680:012 -->

**Original Meaning.** *Shang* 尚 and 上 are interchangeable.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4680:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4680:013 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** *Shang* means above. What the time honors and employs is called *shang*, “esteemed.” What the first esteems is joining its intention with the one above. The fourth relies on the first, and the first benefits the fourth: this is joining intentions above.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4680:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4680:014 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** In the Changes' conventions, even though the first nine and fourth six correspond correctly, this does not ordinarily imply going to follow the fourth: one in a low position does not reach upward for support. Only Decrease's first line says to go swiftly, and the Commentary speaks of joining intentions above, because this is a time of decreasing below to increase above.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4680:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4680:015 -->

### Nine in the Second Place: Steadfastness is beneficial: taking the mean as one's intention.[^j12-decrease-mean]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4680:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4680:016 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** A nine in the second place is not formally correct; occupying Joy is not firmness. Yet attaining the mean is good. Preserve centered virtue, and how could there be anything not good? How could centeredness be incorrect, or centeredness contain excess? The second's benefit in steadfastness means taking the mean as its intention. With the intention fixed on the mean, it is correct of itself. Broadly, the mean is weightier than correctness: the mean entails correctness, but correctness does not necessarily attain the mean. Preserving the mean benefits the one above.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4680:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4680:017 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4680:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4680:018 -->

**Kong Yingda:** The second nine can remain and preserve steadfastness, benefiting the other without diminishing itself, precisely because it occupies the center and takes the mean as its intention. Thus decrease and increase attain their fitting measure.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4680:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4680:019 -->

**Wang Zongchuan:** Make mere compliance one's business, and much within oneself will be diminished. Preserving oneself through the Way is instead how one benefits the other. Hence the second nine benefits from steadfastness, taking the mean as its intention. With the mean as one's intention, nothing in oneself is lost, and the substance of benefiting the one above goes no further than this.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4680:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4680:020 -->

### Six in the Third Place: One person goes: with three, doubt arises.[^j12-decrease-three]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4680:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4680:021 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** One person going and obtaining one person is the gaining of a friend. If three go, there is doubt about whom to join. Principle calls for diminishing their number by one: removing what is superfluous.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4680:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4680:022 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Numbers beyond two may all be expressed generally as three; it need not mean precisely three people. Such are Ji Wenzi's thinking three times and Nan Rong's repeating three times.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4680:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4680:023 -->

### Six in the Fourth Place: Diminishing one's ailment: this too is cause for joy.[^j12-decrease-joy]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4680:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4680:024 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Diminishing what ails one is certainly cause for joy. “Too” is an introductory particle.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4680:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4680:025 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4680:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4680:026 -->

**Xiang Anshi:** Able to relinquish one's ailment, one diminishes oneself to receive assistance, allowing those who share one's intention to give loyal service. Is this not cause for joy?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4680:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4680:027 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** The Changes often says “there is joy,” but this Commentary says “this too is cause for joy.” Here the joy does not primarily belong to oneself. It belongs to the person whom one causes to come swiftly and benefit oneself. Thus the wording changes to “cause for joy”: it is spoken from the other's perspective.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4680:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4680:028 -->

### Six in the Fifth Place: Supreme good fortune: assistance comes from above.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4680:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4680:029 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Supreme good fortune is attained because one can draw fully upon everyone's insight and accord with the principles of Heaven and Earth. Thus blessing and assistance descend from Heaven above.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4680:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4680:030 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Assistance from above may be taken as directly explaining why turtle and milfoil divinations do not oppose it. Yet consider Increase's second line: after the precious tortoises that cannot be refused, it also speaks of the good fortune of making offerings to the Lord. The Lord presides over the hundred spirits. Thus assistance from above likewise means that Heaven's heart accepts the offering: neither humans nor spirits can oppose it.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4680:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4680:031 -->

### Top Nine: Without decreasing, increase the other: greatly fulfilling the intention.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4680:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4680:032 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Occupying the top, one does not diminish those below but instead benefits them. This is the noble person's great attainment, putting his intention into practice. The noble person's intention lies only in benefiting others.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4680:032 -->

<a id="eee-4681"></a>

## 42. Yi 益 — Increase

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4681 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4681:001 -->

### Great Image: Wind and thunder: Increase. Thus the noble person moves toward the good when he sees it, and corrects his faults when he has them.[^j12-increase-readings]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4681:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4681:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** Wind and thunder aid and increase one another's force. Moving toward goodness and correcting faults are the greatest forms of increase; their mutual benefit works in the same way.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4681:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4681:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Violent wind makes thunder swift; the thunder's agitation makes the wind fierce. The two things increase one another. Observing this image, the noble person seeks increase within himself. No Way of increase surpasses moving toward the good upon seeing it and correcting faults when they occur. Able to move toward the good, one can bring all the world's goodness to fulfillment; able to correct faults, one can become free of fault. Nothing benefits a person more greatly.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4681:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4681:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4681:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4681:005 -->

**Wang Bi:** Moving toward goodness and correcting faults: no increase is greater.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4681:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4681:006 -->

**Hu Bingwen:** Thunder and wind naturally have the force of mutual increase. Swift movement toward goodness should make faults ever fewer; decisiveness in correcting faults should make goodness ever purer. Thus these two practices also have the power of increasing one another.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4681:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4681:007 -->

**Jiang Tisheng:** Wind and thunder increase one another swiftly, without delay. The noble person models himself on them: seeing good, he moves toward it at once; knowing a fault, he corrects it quickly. He must not hesitate.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4681:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4681:008 -->

**He Kai:** Influence speaks of swiftness: the heart's virtue communicates through openness. Without decreasing, there is no openness. Check anger and restrain desire, diminishing them again and again, and openness is attained, restoring Influence. Constancy speaks of duration: the heart's virtue gathers firmly in substance. Without increasing, there is no substance. Move toward goodness and correct faults, increasing again and again, and fullness of substance is attained, completing Constancy.[^j12-increase-decrease]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4681:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4681:009 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Thunder stirs yang energy; the human heart's arousal and courage toward goodness resemble it. Wind disperses yin energy; the human heart's cleansing away of evil resembles it.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4681:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4681:010 -->

### First Nine: Supreme good fortune, no blame: those below do not ordinarily bear weighty affairs.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4681:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4681:011 -->

**Original Meaning.** Those below should not ordinarily undertake weighty affairs. Unless the result is as good as this, it is insufficient to avert blame.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4681:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4681:012 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Those below should not ordinarily handle weighty affairs, meaning great and important undertakings. Because the person above entrusts such work to one, undertaking it requires the ability to complete it and bring supreme good fortune; only then is there no blame. With supreme good fortune, the superior shows discernment in making the appointment, and oneself proves equal to the responsibility. Otherwise both above and below bear blame.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4681:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4681:013 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4681:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4681:014 -->

**Zheng Ruxie:** One who receives increase does not receive it for private use. Thus Decrease's top line benefits from going and obtains servants without keeping a private household, while Increase's first line benefits from undertaking great work. Great work should accomplish great benefit. Yet one below undertaking great benefit lacks elevated position and established public trust. Supreme good fortune is therefore required before there is no blame, because the position is not one for weighty affairs.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4681:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4681:015 -->

**Zhu Xi's Classified Conversations:** Benefiting from great work is explained by the Image as “those below do not bear weighty affairs.” From this it follows that those in subordinate positions should not undertake such affairs on their own: son in relation to father, minister to ruler, and staff to their chief must not overstep their allotted duties or office. Even where action is possible, it must be entirely good before there is freedom from fault. Hence the warning, “supreme good fortune, no blame.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4681:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4681:016 -->

### Six in the Second Place: Someone increases it: coming from outside.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4681:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4681:017 -->

**Original Meaning.** “Someone” expresses a multitude without identifying a particular source.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4681:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4681:018 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Centered and correct, open within, able to receive the world's goodness and preserve it firmly: whenever something beneficial is available, people come from outside to provide it. Someone asks whether “coming from outside” means the fifth. Answer: with the second's centered correctness and openness, who in the world would not wish to benefit it? The fifth, its proper correspondent, is certainly included.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4681:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4681:019 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4681:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4681:020 -->

**Kong Yingda:** Coming from outside makes clear that the benefactor arrives from outside, unbidden.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4681:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4681:021 -->

**Yang Jian:** Someone increasing it from outside resembles Decrease's fifth six receiving increase and assistance from above. Both mean that there was originally no intention of seeking benefit, yet benefit arrives of itself. Coming from outside means arriving without having been expected within the heart.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4681:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4681:022 -->

### Six in the Third Place: Increase through adverse affairs: holding it firmly as one's own.[^j12-increase-adversity]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4681:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4681:023 -->

**Original Meaning.** Increasing through adverse affairs seeks to trouble the heart and press thought to its limits, so that one firmly possesses the benefit.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4681:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4681:024 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The third six's increase can be employed only in adverse affairs because it holds the matter firmly as its own: it assumes sole responsibility. One below should receive directions from above, yet here acts on its own responsibility. This is permissible only in rescuing the people from calamity and relieving the age's urgent distress. It is an expedient for emergencies and extraordinary changes, and therefore brings no blame. In ordinary times it would not be permissible.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4681:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4681:025 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4681:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4681:026 -->

**Gong Huan:** Increasing through adverse affairs may be described as calamity coming from outside, but the benefit one receives is something inherently belonging to one's own share, not something arriving from outside.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4681:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4681:027 -->

### Six in the Fourth Place: Informing the duke and gaining his assent: an intention to benefit.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4681:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4681:028 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The line statement says only that attaining the middle course enables one to inform the duke and gain assent. The Image further explains that assent is obtained because one presents an intention to benefit the world. If that is truly the intention, the superior will certainly trust and follow it. One serving a ruler should fear not that the ruler will fail to follow, but that one's own intention is insincere.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4681:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4681:029 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4681:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4681:030 -->

**Gong Huan:** The fourth six informs the duke with the intention of benefiting the people. Thus its proposal is followed.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4681:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4681:031 -->

### Nine in the Fifth Place: A sincere, beneficent heart: no need to ask about it. Gratitude for my virtue: greatly fulfilling the intention.[^j12-increase-beneficence]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4681:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4681:032 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** When a ruler has an utterly sincere heart intent on benefiting the world, supreme good fortune needs no stating. Hence “no need to ask.” When the world sincerely cherishes my virtue as a benefit, the Way prevails greatly and the ruler's intention is fulfilled.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4681:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4681:033 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4681:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4681:034 -->

**Cui Jing:** In a time of diminishing what is above, one's sole concern is diminishing oneself. Though a sincere, beneficent heart reaches those below, one never speaks to display one's own achievement. Hence “a sincere, beneficent heart: do not ask.” Asking here is like speaking. Thus supreme good fortune is obtained, and those below trust and cherish one's virtue. Hence “with sincerity they hold my virtue dear.” Though the ruler does not speak, others cherish his virtue, and so his intention is greatly fulfilled.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4681:034 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4681:035 -->

**Zhang Zhenyuan:** Beneficence comes from the heart: what need to ask? Great fulfillment of intention does not lie simply in the people's gratitude toward me. It lies in my being sufficient to bring about conduct for which they feel gratitude.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4681:035 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4681:036 -->

### Top Nine: No one increases it: a one-sided expression. Someone strikes it: coming from outside.[^j12-increase-one-sided]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4681:036 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4681:037 -->

**Original Meaning.** “No one increases it” still speaks from the partial perspective of its desire for increase. To state the matter fully, there are also those who strike it.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4681:037 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4681:038 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Principle is the world's utmost impartiality; benefit is what all people alike desire. If one's heart is impartial and does not depart from correct principle, one shares benefit with others without encroaching upon them, and they too wish to give to one. But eagerness for profit and the blindness of selfishness lead one to seek increase by diminishing others. They then contend forcefully in return, so no one is willing to increase one, and some strike and seize from one. “No one increases it” condemns its partiality toward itself. Were it free of self-partiality and in accord with the public Way, others would benefit it: why would they strike it? Seeking benefit from others to an extreme makes everyone hate it and wish to attack. Hence those who strike come from outside. When someone does good, people respond even from a thousand miles away: the second six, centered and correct, empties itself, and its benefactors arrive from outside. When someone does wrong, people oppose it even from a thousand miles away: the top nine's extreme pursuit of increase brings attackers from outside. The Appended Statements says, “The noble person settles his person before acting, makes his heart easy before speaking, and establishes his relationships before asking. Cultivating these three, he remains whole. Moving from insecurity, he finds no support among the people; speaking from fear, he finds no response; asking without a relationship, he receives nothing. When no one gives to him, those who injure him arrive. The Changes says: ‘No one increases him; someone strikes him. Setting the heart without constancy: misfortune.’” The noble person's speech, action, and requests are complete and good only when each follows its proper Way. Otherwise he brings injury and misfortune upon himself.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4681:038 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4681:039 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4681:039 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4681:040 -->

**Hu Bingwen:** The second does not seek increase, yet someone increases it from outside. The top seeks increase, yet someone strikes it, also from outside. What brings them? The fifth's good fortune comes from sincerity within the heart; the top's misfortune comes from establishing the heart without constancy. The paths to good fortune and misfortune never fail to arise from the heart.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4681:040 -->

<a id="eee-4682"></a>

## 43. Guai 夬 — Breakthrough

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4682 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4682:001 -->

### Great Image: The lake rises to Heaven: Breakthrough. Thus the noble person distributes stipends to those below and, in abiding in virtue, remains wary.[^j12-breakthrough-wary]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4682:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4682:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** The lake rising to Heaven has the force of bursting and breaking through. Distributing stipends to those below expresses that release. “In abiding in virtue, remains wary” is not yet clear.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4682:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4682:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** A lake is gathered water, yet it rises to Heaven's utmost height; hence the image of Breakthrough. The noble person observes water breaking through above and pouring down to irrigate below, and distributes stipends downward: he extends the bounty of his resources to those below. Observing its bursting force, he also takes precautions in abiding in virtue. Abiding in virtue means resting securely in one's virtue. *Ze* means to regulate; *ji* means to guard. Thus one establishes restraints and prohibitions; with such safeguards, there is no bursting and dispersal. Wang Bi writes “make the prohibitions clear,” which also makes sense. The text does not say that a lake is in Heaven but that it rises to Heaven. Rising implies instability and the force of bursting; being there would be a settled expression.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4682:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4682:004 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** A lake rising to Heaven is what is called a flood reaching Heaven: it must burst forth. Wealth accumulated without distribution eventually leaves in disorderly ways, so the noble person distributes stipends to those below. A person who has no apprehension or restraint becomes full and overflows; therefore, in abiding in virtue, the noble person constantly preserves caution. The Rites says, “Accumulate and be able to distribute.” The Documents says, “Be reverent and wary, with no words in your person requiring correction.” With this, what danger of bursting could remain?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4682:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4682:005 -->

### First Nine: Going without the ability to prevail: blame.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4682:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4682:006 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Before acting, a person must assess whether the affair can be accomplished, and only then decide. There will then be no fault. If principle does not permit success yet one goes, the blame is evident. Fault in action always comes from excess in decisiveness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4682:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4682:007 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4682:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4682:008 -->

**Wang Shenzi:** Going itself is not the fault. The fault lies in failing to assess one's ability to prevail before going.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4682:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4682:009 -->

**Gu Jiajie:** In Breakthrough's Way, awareness of danger brings radiance. A heart set on victory must not be indulged. How much worse to go without the ability to prevail!

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4682:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4682:010 -->

### Nine in the Second Place: Armed attack, yet no anxiety: attaining the middle Way.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4682:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4682:011 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Armed attack at dusk or night is deeply frightening. Yet one may remain without anxiety because one has placed oneself well. Having attained the middle Way, knowing apprehension, and maintaining precautions, what need is there to worry? A nine in the second place is centered but not formally correct. Why is it entirely good? When yang removes yin and the noble person removes petty people while attaining the mean, how could there be anything incorrect? Knowing the time and recognizing the situation are the great method of studying the Changes.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4682:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4682:012 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4682:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4682:013 -->

**Master Zhang:** Able to attain the middle Way, it is firm without violence.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4682:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4682:014 -->

**Su Shi:** Able to remain still without forgetting vigilance, and to remain vigilant without employing force, it attains the middle Way. This resembles Great Strength's second nine, whose steadfastness brings good fortune. Thus both are praised for attaining the mean.[^j12-breakthrough-readings]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4682:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4682:015 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Armed attack without anxiety means not lightly resorting to arms. This is how the middle Way is attained.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4682:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4682:016 -->

### Nine in the Third Place: The noble person is resolutely decisive: in the end, no blame.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4682:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4682:017 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Entanglement in private attachments comes from lack of decision. The noble person joins himself to rightness and decides where decision is called for. Thus he does not ultimately incur blame.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4682:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4682:018 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4682:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4682:019 -->

**Huang Chunyao:** The ending is contrasted with the beginning. Though at first one seems drenched and meets resentment, in the end one resolutely removes the attachment and is without blame.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4682:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4682:020 -->

### Nine in the Fourth Place: Its movement falters: the position is not appropriate. Hearing words without believing them: hearing lacks clarity.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4682:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4682:021 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** A nine occupying yin is not appropriately placed. Yang in yieldingness loses firm decision and cannot advance forcefully; hence its movement falters. Only firmness enables clarity. Occupying yieldingness, it shifts and loses its correct nature: how could clarity remain? Hearing words without being able to believe them therefore results from unclear hearing.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4682:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4682:022 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** The fourth is still separated from yin. “The position is not appropriate” borrows the line's place to show that the fourth has not yet come to the responsibility in question, and that its wish to falter forward is unfitting.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4682:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4682:023 -->

### Nine in the Fifth Place: Taking the middle course brings no blame: the heart is not yet radiant.[^j12-breakthrough-inner-light]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4682:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4682:024 -->

**Original Meaning.** Cheng's Commentary gives the full explanation.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4682:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4682:025 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The text's “resolutely decisive” means that following the middle course brings no blame. The Image completes the explanation with “the heart is not yet radiant.” Only a correct heart and sincere intention can fulfill centered correctness and become substantial and radiant. The fifth's heart has an attachment. Recognizing that rightness forbids it, the fifth breaks it off. Outward conduct then does not lose centered correctness and may be free of blame, yet the middle Way is not radiant and great within. For once desire occupies the human heart, it departs from the Way. Confucius's teaching here is profound indeed.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4682:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4682:026 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4682:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4682:027 -->

**Master Zhang:** Yang is close to yin and cannot be free of entanglement. Thus its conduct must be corrected before blame can be avoided.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4682:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4682:028 -->

**Zhao Rumei:** Other hexagrams prize the middle course, but this line reaches only freedom from blame. Is this because it embodies Dui's pleasure and becomes submerged in attachment to the top? Hence there is not yet radiance within.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4682:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4682:029 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Master Zhang's explanation is entirely right. Because the heart is not yet radiant, the middle course is prized. The meaning is not that, although one takes the middle course, the heart still lacks radiance.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4682:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4682:030 -->

### Top Six: The misfortune of a cry that cannot help: in the end, it cannot endure.[^j12-breakthrough-cry]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4682:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4682:031 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The Way of yang-firm noble people advances and grows stronger. The petty person's Way has reached its limit and naturally disappears. How could it endure? Even wailing can accomplish nothing; hence “in the end, it cannot endure.” Earlier scholars, finding “sincere proclamation” and “vigilant call” within the hexagram, wished to read the word here in the departing tone, interpreting it as no need for further proclamations or commands. This is wrong. What harm is there in a single hexagram having two departing-tone uses and one level-tone use? Yet readers of the Changes generally doubt this. Someone asks: “The sage never necessarily cuts off even the gravely wicked. Is it permissible here to leave them no cry and declare inevitable misfortune?” Answer: “Breakthrough is the time when the petty person's Way disappears. Removing that Way does not require executing every petty person. Causing them to change and reform makes their Way disappear. The disappearance of that Way is their misfortune.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4682:031 -->

<a id="eee-4683"></a>

## 44. Gou 姤 — Coming to Meet

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4683 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4683:001 -->

### Great Image: Wind beneath Heaven: Coming to Meet. Thus the ruler issues commands and proclaims them to the four quarters.[^j12-gou-commands]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4683:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4683:002 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Wind moves beneath Heaven, reaching everywhere. A ruler observes its all-embracing reach and issues commands to proclaim them throughout the four quarters. Wind moving over the earth and wind beneath Heaven both image reaching all things. Yet movement over the earth, touching everything, makes Viewing: the image of traveling, observing, and inspecting. Movement beneath Heaven, extending throughout the four quarters, makes Coming to Meet: the image of issuing commands. The Images variously speak of former kings, the ruler, the noble person, or the great person. Former kings are named when institutions are established: founding states, making music, inspecting the regions, ordering the laws, closing the passes, nourishing things, and offering to the Lord. “The ruler” names what a sovereign does, as in fashioning and completing Heaven and Earth's Way or issuing commands to the four quarters. “Noble person” applies to those above and below alike; “great person” is a general designation for kings and dukes.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4683:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4683:003 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4683:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4683:004 -->

**Gong Huan:** Wind beneath Heaven in Coming to Meet resembles wind moving over the earth in Viewing. Thus the former speaks of issuing commands to the four quarters, the latter of inspecting regions, observing the people, and establishing instruction. Issuing and proclaiming proceed from above to below, imaging wind beneath Heaven. Inspecting and observing travel everywhere and take in all sides, imaging wind over the earth.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4683:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4683:005 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Penetration's reiteration of commands responds to accumulated abuses by rousing and correcting them. Coming to Meet's issuing of commands is the same: what is Xun among three-line figures is Coming to Meet among six-line figures. Issuing and reiterating commands remove concealed wrongdoing and accumulated abuses, modeling themselves on wind dispersing hidden yin.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4683:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4683:006 -->

### First Six: Bound to a metal brake: the yielding Way is drawn.[^j12-gou-drawing]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4683:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4683:007 -->

**Original Meaning.** “Drawn” means advancing. Because it advances, it is restrained.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4683:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4683:008 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Drawing means pulling forward into advancement. Yin has just arisen and advances gradually: the yielding Way is now being drawn onward. Binding it to the metal brake stops its advance. Prevent it from advancing, and it cannot diminish the correct Way. This is the good fortune of steadfastness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4683:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4683:009 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4683:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4683:010 -->

**Kong Yingda:** The yielding Way being drawn means that yielding yin must have something to draw and bind it.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4683:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4683:011 -->

**Zheng Ruxie:** This is the lean pig. Though its strength is slight, its intention is to leap and range about. Once its restlessness is understood, it must be restrained. Hence the yielding Way is drawn.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4683:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4683:012 -->

**Zhao Rumei:** Coming to Meet's first line speaks of binding and drawing because it disapproves of yin's growth and restrains it.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4683:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4683:013 -->

### Nine in the Second Place: Fish in the wrapping: by rightness, it does not reach the guests.[^j12-gou-fish]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4683:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4683:014 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** In the second's meeting with the first, no divided attachment outside should be allowed. It should be like fish held in a wrapping. Fish within the wrapping should not, in principle, extend to guests.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4683:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4683:015 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4683:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4683:016 -->

**Wu Yueshen:** Once the second nine has fish in its wrapping, it must fulfill its responsibility to guard and restrain. In terms of rightness, the fish must not be allowed to encounter the guests. Allowing that encounter without restraint would lose rightness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4683:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4683:017 -->

### Nine in the Third Place: Its movement falters: its movement has not yet been checked.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4683:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4683:018 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** At first its intention is to seek an encounter with the first, and so its movement is slow. “Not yet drawn” means not hastening its movement. Recognizing the danger and changing, it does not reach great blame.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4683:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4683:019 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4683:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4683:020 -->

**Guo Yong:** The danger of skinlessness and faltering comes from never having restrained itself, but moving recklessly. Thus it arrives at this condition.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4683:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4683:021 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Every use of “draw” in the Changes, from Small Restraint to this passage, should mean drawing back and restraining.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4683:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4683:022 -->

### Nine in the Fourth Place: The misfortune of having no fish: keeping the people distant.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4683:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4683:023 -->

**Original Meaning.** The people's leaving oneself amounts to oneself keeping them distant.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4683:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4683:024 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Those below depart because oneself has caused it. Keeping the people distant means oneself distancing them. The superior has done something to make them leave.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4683:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4683:025 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4683:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4683:026 -->

**Yu Ben:** This says that one fails in the Way of directing the people and has nothing with which to bind their hearts. Their leaving oneself results from oneself remaining distant from them.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4683:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4683:027 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Because the fourth nine corresponds to yin, it dislikes yin and wishes to distance itself, much as Breakthrough's third line displays strength in its cheekbones. Merely wishing to distance it, without being able to accommodate and restrain it, leaves the wrapping without fish. The noble person can restrain petty people precisely because he can accommodate them. Unable to accommodate them, he causes them to cut themselves off. Would not trying to restrain them by force then be difficult? The Documents says, “The people may be drawn near; they must not be treated as low.” This is the meaning.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4683:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4683:028 -->

### Nine in the Fifth Place: Keeping brilliance within: centered and correct. Something falls from Heaven: the intention does not abandon the mandate.[^j12-gou-mandate]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4683:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4683:029 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Keeping brilliance within means holding centered and correct virtue within. When virtue is substantial and full, it forms a pattern and shines. The mandate is Heaven's principle; abandoning means departing from it. With utmost sincerity and centered correctness, one humbles oneself to seek the worthy and preserves an intention conforming to Heaven's principle. Therefore something falls from Heaven: one will certainly obtain it.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4683:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4683:030 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4683:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4683:031 -->

**Su Shi:** Yin growing and diminishing yang is Heaven's mandate. Having the means to overcome it is the human will. The noble person does not abandon his will on account of the mandate. Thus, when the fifth nine's intention is firm, something must fall from Heaven. This means that when human effort reaches its utmost, Heaven cannot defeat it.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4683:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4683:032 -->

**Yang Qixin:** Yin and yang alternately prevailing is the natural movement of Heaven. Yet with every thought, one refuses to abandon Heaven's mandate. Governing through stillness is how one turns the course of transformation back.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4683:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4683:033 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** The Odes says, “When the mulberry sheds its leaves, they yellow and fall.” Thus something falling from Heaven means that the season arrives and the melon drops. Though this is inevitable under Heaven's mandate, it also results from the noble person's accumulated sincerity and cultivated virtue coinciding with it. Hence the intention does not abandon the mandate.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4683:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4683:034 -->

### Top Nine: Meeting with the horns: at the upper limit, humiliation.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4683:034 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4683:035 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** It occupies the uppermost limit, and firmness too is at its utmost. Thus exhaustion above brings humiliation. Seeking an encounter from a high position with extreme firmness—is this not difficult?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4683:035 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4683:036 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Although not encountering yin brings no blame, the noble person still regards inability to aid the time as shameful. This results from standing outside affairs, in an exhausted position.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4683:036 -->

<a id="eee-4684"></a>

## 45. Cui 萃 — Gathering

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4684 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4684:001 -->

### Great Image: The lake rises above the earth: Gathering. Thus the noble person puts weapons in order and guards against the unforeseen.[^j12-gathering-defense]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4684:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4684:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** Putting in order means repairing and assembling them.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4684:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4684:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** A lake above the earth images gathering. Observing it, the noble person puts weapons in order to prepare against the unforeseen. Wherever things gather, unanticipated affairs arise. A gathering of people brings contention; a gathering of possessions brings seizure. Broadly, gathering brings many complications. Thus this image calls for vigilance. Putting in order means selecting and repairing, removing what is defective or harmful. Repairing and assembling weapons prepares against the unforeseen.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4684:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4684:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4684:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4684:005 -->

**Wang Bi:** Gathering without safeguards allows ambitions to arise among the multitude.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4684:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4684:006 -->

**Zhu Xi's Classified Conversations:** Wherever things assemble and people become numerous, contention is likely, so preparations must be made in advance. Moreover, a lake ordinarily lies within the earth; here it rises above it. Abundant water brings danger of bursting through and rushing forth. Hence this image.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4684:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4684:007 -->

**Wang Shenzi:** Earth above the lake is Approach: natural banks gather the water. A lake rising above the earth is Gathering: embankments hold it together. Natural banks require no labor of dikes; artificial embankments bring the danger of bursting. Observing this image, the noble person establishes safeguards for the governed world, preparing weapons as a precaution against the unforeseen. To forget preparations for defense because government is peaceful would be like destroying an old embankment as useless. Could this be permissible?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4684:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4684:008 -->

### First Six: Now confused, now gathering: the intention is confused.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4684:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4684:009 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Its heart and intention are confused by its own kind, so it gathers with the company of yin. Unable to preserve its resolve firmly, it is confused by petty people and loses correctness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4684:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4684:010 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4684:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4684:011 -->

**Li Jian:** Without confusion in the intention, there would be no abandoning one's correspondent to gather in disorder.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4684:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4684:012 -->

### Six in the Second Place: Drawn onward, good fortune without blame: the center has not changed.[^j12-gathering-center]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4684:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4684:013 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** In Gathering's time, achieving union is fortunate; thus the fourth nine receives the gathering of above and below. Although the second and fifth correspond correctly, they occupy separate places with a barrier between: they have not yet united in a time of gathering. Drawing one another toward union therefore brings good fortune without blame, because centered and correct virtue has not yet changed. Once changed, they would no longer draw one another.
