Someone asks: “The second already possesses centered correctness; why does the Image say ‘has not yet changed,’ as though something were lacking?” Answer: “The assembled yin adjoin one another and are gathering with their kind. To remain among them without changing, waiting at a distance for one's proper correspondent, requires firm independence. With the second's yielding yin capacities, one can only hope that its centered and correct virtue will prevent change. Thus the Image keeps its meaning implicit in order to preserve the warning.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4684:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4684:014 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4684:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4684:015 -->

**Yang Wanli:** The center not changing means that the middle Way preserved by the second six does not change because it is drawn forward by the one above.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4684:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4684:016 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** The center not changing has the same meaning as Holding Together's second line not losing oneself. It is what the Mean calls not changing from what one was in adversity, and what Mencius calls not departing from the Way when one succeeds.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4684:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4684:017 -->

### Six in the Third Place: Going brings no blame: there is deference above.[^j12-gathering-above]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4684:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4684:018 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The top occupies the utmost of yielding pleasure. The third can go without blame because the top six receives it deferentially and compliantly.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4684:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4684:019 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4684:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4684:020 -->

**Yu Fan:** Moving to the fourth; hence deference above.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4684:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4684:021 -->

**Zheng Ruxie:** The two yin below have both gathered around yang. The third alone has no attachment, and so sighs and laments, with nothing beneficial. Yet in Gathering's time, those below wish to gather above, and those above likewise wish others to gather around them. If the third does not let its lack of a correspondent prevent it from going to join above, there may be slight humiliation but also no blame. “Above” does not mean the top six; it means the yang above.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4684:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4684:022 -->

### Nine in the Fourth Place: Great good fortune, no blame: the position is not appropriate.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4684:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4684:023 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Because the position is inappropriate, there is doubt whether its actions can be entirely good. Thus great good fortune must first be attained before there is no blame. Without complete goodness, how could there be great good fortune?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4684:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4684:024 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4684:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4684:025 -->

**Su Shi:** Possessing the power to gather others without occupying the proper position brings blame unless there is great good fortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4684:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4684:026 -->

**Guo Yong:** The fourth receives the gathering of those above and below but does not occupy the ruler's place. Thus its position is called inappropriate.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4684:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4684:027 -->

**Zheng Ruxie:** Its position is close to the ruler's, its virtue similar, and its reception of those below likewise similar. Unless its conduct is entirely good, the ruler will resent it. How could it be without blame? This is a warning. “The position is not appropriate” has more than one meaning. Here it means that firm yang presses close upon the ruler.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4684:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4684:028 -->

**Xiong Liangfu:** The fourth and fifth nines both govern gathering. The fifth governs gathering above, the fourth gathering below. Thus the fifth says that gathering has its position, while the fourth's Image calls its position inappropriate. Great good fortune is required for no blame because it adjoins the ruler above and, though a minister, images the ruler's gathering. This invites suspicion of blame.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4684:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4684:029 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Zheng is right that the meaning of an inappropriate position varies. Yet one must understand that formal line position is borrowed to explain whether virtue suits its time and station.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4684:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4684:030 -->

### Nine in the Fifth Place: Gathering has its position: the intention is not yet radiant.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4684:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4684:031 -->

**Original Meaning.** Lack of radiance means the absence of trust.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4684:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4684:032 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The Image cites only the line's first phrase. A ruler's intention must be to make sincerity and trust manifest throughout the world, responding freely to every influence, so that all living beings cherish him and come to him. If some still lack trust, his intention is not yet radiant and great.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4684:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4684:033 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4684:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4684:034 -->

**Gong Huan:** The fifth has the position, but merely having position leaves some people without trust. Thus its intention is not yet radiant.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4684:034 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4684:035 -->

**Hu Bingwen:** The fourth needs great good fortune before it is without blame, because its position is inappropriate. The fifth has the position, yet lacks trust and its intention is still not radiant. One who would receive the world's gathering must have the position, but having position, must also have virtue.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4684:035 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4684:036 -->

### Top Six: Sighs, tears, and a running nose: not at ease above.[^j12-gathering-tears]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4684:036 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4684:037 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The petty person's situation is constantly unfitting. Greedy and following desire, he cannot choose a secure place; when distress comes, he is thrown into confusion and knows not what to do. The six's tears and running nose come from uneasiness above. The noble person carefully chooses his place and will not occupy it without rightness. If misfortune brings danger and distress, he remains calmly at ease and does not let them burden his heart. The petty person does not choose security, continually occupying what is not his rightful place. Pressed into extremity, he panics and becomes agitated, even weeping with a running nose: a shameful condition. *Wei* means “not immediately,” like the colloquial “not readily.” One cannot readily be at ease above. Yin at the top, alone and without associates, is not in its proper place. How could it be at ease?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4684:037 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4684:038 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4684:038 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4684:039 -->

**Zhao Guangda:** This speaks of apprehension and not daring to settle complacently above. With an anxious heart and deep concern for trouble, how could one simply rest at ease?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4684:039 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4684:040 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Above is like outside. Though outside, one does not dare rest at ease. Like Shun farming Mount Li or the Duke of Zhou remaining in the eastern lands, one cries and laments until gathering with ruler and father has been achieved.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4684:040 -->

<a id="eee-4685"></a>

## 46. Sheng 升 — Pushing Upward

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4685 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4685:001 -->

### Great Image: Wood grows within the earth: Pushing Upward. Thus the noble person cultivates virtue in due order, accumulating the small to attain height and greatness.[^j12-ascent-virtue]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4685:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4685:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** Wang Su's text writes “careful” for “compliant.” I find that other books quoting this passage likewise often write “careful,” making the meaning especially clear. The ancient graphs were interchangeable, as explained under Meng in the Upper Part.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4685:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4685:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Wood grows within the earth, lengthening and rising upward: the image of Pushing Upward. The noble person observes it and cultivates virtue compliantly, accumulating what is minute until it reaches height and greatness. Compliance permits progress; opposition brings retreat. All things advance by following their proper course. Without accumulating goodness, one cannot establish a good name. Fullness of learning and achievement, and loftiness of the Way and virtue, all arise through accumulation. Accumulating the small to attain height and greatness is the meaning of Pushing Upward.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4685:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4685:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4685:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4685:005 -->

**Hu Bingwen:** If a growing tree fails to grow for a day, it withers. If advancing virtue is without care for a single breath, it retreats. Every thought and every affair must be carefully examined. Then virtue's accumulation from small beginnings to height and greatness will resemble the tree's ascent.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4685:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4685:006 -->

### First Six: Trusted ascent brings great good fortune: joining intentions with those above.[^j12-ascent-above]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4685:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4685:007 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** One joins intentions with the person above and ascends together. Above means the second nine. Ascending by following the second means sharing its intention. Trustingly following a worthy person of centered firmness brings great good fortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4685:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4685:008 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4685:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4685:009 -->

**Lü Dalin:** The first six is yielding below. In a time of ascent, yieldingness advances upward. Though at the lowest place, its intention is to rise together with the three yin. Accepted by all, it meets nothing unfavorable. Hence trusted ascent brings great good fortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4685:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4685:010 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Lü is correct to take those above as the upper trigram's three yin.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4685:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4685:011 -->

### Nine in the Second Place: Its sincerity: there is joy.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4685:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4685:012 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Serving above with sincerity gives the second more than merely blameless conduct as a minister. It can put centered firmness into practice and extend its bounty throughout the world: this gives joy. Whenever an Image speaks of blessing, it means that good fortune reaches other beings. When it speaks of joy, an affair is already good and has something further to rejoice in. Thus Great Restraint's “the young ox's horn-bar: supreme good fortune” is explained as giving joy. Restraining it while young is easy and also avoids the difficulty of forceful restraint: this is a cause for joy.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4685:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4685:013 -->

### Nine in the Third Place: Ascending into an empty settlement: nothing remains doubtful.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4685:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4685:014 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Entering a settlement without people, one's advancement meets no doubt or obstruction.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4685:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4685:015 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4685:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4685:016 -->

**Su Shi:** The third nine employs yang with yang, making its ascent decisive. Hence “ascending into an empty settlement: nothing remains doubtful.” Good fortune is not mentioned because whether this will bring calamity or blessing is not yet known. That depends upon the person.[^j12-ascent-third]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4685:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4685:017 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Qian's fourth line uses “perhaps” to express doubt, and thus there is no blame. Can decisiveness in advancing, with nothing doubtful, be approved without qualification? Su's explanation is good.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4685:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4685:018 -->

### Six in the Fourth Place: The king makes offerings on Mount Qi: service through compliance.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4685:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4685:019 -->

**Original Meaning.** Ascending through compliance gives the image of climbing a mountain to offer sacrifice.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4685:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4685:020 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** In a time of ascent, the fourth occupies a position near the ruler and obtains good fortune without blame because it possesses compliant virtue. Yieldingness within Kun is compliance at its utmost. King Wen's offering on Mount Qi likewise simply followed the time. Above, compliance toward those above; below, compliance toward those below; in oneself, compliant occupation of what rightness requires. Hence service through compliance.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4685:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4685:021 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Employing the worthy to make offerings to the spirits means serving them in accordance with the spirits' own hearts.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4685:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4685:022 -->

### Six in the Fifth Place: Steadfastness brings good fortune; ascending the steps: greatly fulfilling the intention.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4685:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4685:023 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Relying upon and employing worthy talent, while remaining steadfast and firm, one can ascend in this way to bring great order to the world. The intention can be greatly fulfilled. The ruler's ascent need fear only the absence of worthy assistance. With assistance, it is like ascending by steps.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4685:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4685:024 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4685:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4685:025 -->

**He Kai:** This is what the Judgment Commentary calls blessing and the intention's being carried out.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4685:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4685:026 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Ascending from the first line, ascent reaches its utmost here. Thus the first says “joining intentions above,” while this line says “greatly fulfilling the intention.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4685:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4685:027 -->

### Top Six: Ascending in darkness at the top: decrease, not abundance.[^j12-ascent-darkness]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4685:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4685:028 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Obscurely pursuing ascent to the utmost, reaching the top without knowing to stop, leaves only decline and disappearance. How could there be further increase? No abundance means no further addition. At ascent's limit, there is retreat but no advance.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4685:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4685:029 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4685:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4685:030 -->

**Hu Yuan:** The top six fails to understand the first signs of survival and ruin and reaches the upper position. It should certainly empty and diminish itself, not make itself grand and honored in pursuit of abundant prosperity.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4685:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4685:031 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Hu's explanation is good. Yet why does the text not say, “Unceasing steadfastness: decrease, not abundance,” but instead “ascending in darkness at the top”? The upper position shows that status and power are full and flourishing; therefore voluntarily diminishing oneself is what should constitute its steadfastness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4685:031 -->

<a id="eee-4686"></a>

## 47. Kun 困 — Oppression

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4686 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4686:001 -->

### Great Image: A lake without water: Oppression. Thus the noble person commits his life and fulfills his resolve.[^j12-oppression-life]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4686:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4686:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** Water leaks downward and the lake above dries up; hence a lake without water. Committing one's life is like surrendering it: holding it out to another without claiming it as one's own. Able to do this, one finds passage even in oppression.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4686:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4686:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** A lake without water images oppression and exhaustion. When the noble person meets extremity, has exhausted every means of prevention and deliberation, yet cannot escape, this is the mandate. He should entrust himself to that mandate and fulfill his resolve. Knowing what the mandate entails, he does not let obstruction and calamity move his heart, but simply practices what is right. Without understanding the mandate, he fears danger, panics in extremity, and loses what he preserves. How could he then fulfill his intention toward goodness?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4686:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4686:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4686:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4686:005 -->

**Wang Bi:** A lake without water means the water lies below it: the image of Oppression. One who bends his resolve under oppression is a petty person. The noble person remains steadfast in extremity. Could the Way be forgotten?[^j12-oppression-reading]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4686:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4686:006 -->

**Zheng Ruxie:** Knowing that something cannot be obtained by pursuit and allowing it to arrive of itself is entrusting oneself to the mandate. What belongs to the mandate cannot be sought at will; what belongs to one's resolve can be fulfilled. This is what is meant by following what one truly loves.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4686:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4686:007 -->

**Feng Dangke:** In the noble person's oppression, the mandate belongs to Heaven and is entrusted to it, while resolve belongs to oneself and is fulfilled. Being at ease amid oppression is entrusting the mandate; possessing something not oppressed amid oppression is fulfilling resolve. The petty person, by contrast, tries every means of escaping by luck. If he ultimately fails to escape, he has merely lost what he ought to preserve. Embody Kan's danger in entrusting the mandate, and Dui's joy in fulfilling resolve.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4686:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4686:008 -->

**He Kai:** Entrusting means committing to another's keeping. Without trust in one's mandate, one devises a hundred schemes concerning life and death, calamity and blessing. How could the resolve to remain steadfast then be fulfilled? Entrusting everything to the mandate prevents the mandate from dividing one's resolve. Indeed, one may thereby establish the mandate through resolve.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4686:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4686:009 -->

### First Six: Entering a dark valley: obscure and without clarity.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4686:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4686:010 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Obscurity without clarity means entering ever deeper darkness and trapping oneself in profound oppression. With clarity, one would not fall into the trap.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4686:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4686:011 -->

### Nine in the Second Place: Oppressed amid wine and food: within the center, there is blessing.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4686:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4686:012 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Though oppressed in what it wishes to do and unable yet to bestow benefit, preserving the virtue of centered firmness must bring passage and blessing. Even if the time does not yet allow outward success, preserving centered virtue lets the noble person's Way pass freely. That itself is blessing.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4686:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4686:013 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** The second possesses centered virtue and therefore can use wine and food in sacrificial offerings and receive blessing.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4686:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4686:014 -->

### Six in the Third Place: Resting upon thorn-bushes: riding upon firmness. Entering one's palace without seeing one's wife: inauspicious.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4686:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4686:015 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Resting upon thorn-bushes means riding upon the second nine's firmness. Its insecurity is like sitting on thorns. Inauspiciousness is the sign of what is not good; losing one's secure place is the effect of what is not good. Hence not seeing one's wife is inauspicious.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4686:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4686:016 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4686:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4686:017 -->

**Zheng Ruxie:** Advancing, it is blocked by the fourth: oppressed by stone. Retreating, it rides upon the second's firmness: resting on thorn-bushes. The top is its palace. Though the palace can be entered, yieldingness meets yieldingness, not its proper mate. No handling of oppression could be more inauspicious.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4686:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4686:018 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** When the line has several comparisons and the Commentary selects one, it selects the weightiest. Riding upon firmness is an especially weighty relation in the Changes. Thus Opposition's third line selects the carriage dragged back, and this line selects resting on thorns; both concern riding upon firmness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4686:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4686:019 -->

### Nine in the Fourth Place: Coming slowly: the intention is directed below. Though the position is inappropriate, it has an associate.[^j12-oppression-associate]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4686:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4686:020 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The fourth responds to the first but is separated from it by the second. Its intention seeks below, so it comes slowly. Though the inappropriate position is not good, it has its proper correspondent as an associate, and therefore an ending is achieved.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4686:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4686:021 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4686:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4686:022 -->

**Su Jun:** The fourth and fifth are both covered by the top six. Advancing only to be covered is hardly a time for the noble person to proceed directly. He must deepen and quiet himself to nourish obscurity, waiting calmly for the first opening. Thus the fifth says “gradually” and the fourth “slowly.” The intention is directed below: though the fourth's position is high, its heart is lowly. Yet the fourth and fifth unite in virtue, and the world's affairs are ultimately accomplished through patient slowness. Hence it has an associate and also an ending.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4686:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4686:023 -->

**He Kai:** The fifth, its close neighbor, is the fourth's associate.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4686:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4686:024 -->

### Nine in the Fifth Place: Nose and feet cut off: the intention is not yet fulfilled. Gradually there is release: through centered uprightness. Sacrificial offerings are beneficial: receiving blessing.[^j12-oppression-cutting]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4686:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4686:025 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** At first it is covered by yin and has no associates above or below. This is oppression with its intention unfulfilled. Gradual release comes through the Way of centered uprightness: obtaining the worthy below, it joins them in relieving oppression. The text does not say “centered and correct”; because it joins with the second, “upright” is fitting. Uprightness is somewhat less stringent in meaning than correctness. Exhaust one's sincere intention as in sacrifice in order to seek the world's worthy, and one can open a passage through the world's oppression and enjoy blessing.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4686:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4686:026 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4686:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4686:027 -->

**Lu Xisheng:** From oppression and exhaustion one reaches passage; virtue is distinguished and becomes clear. With the Way of centered correctness practiced, the intention is greatly fulfilled. Hence gradual release.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4686:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4686:028 -->

### Top Six: Entangled in creeping vines: the situation is not appropriate. Movement brings regret; repentance brings good fortune: taking action.[^j12-oppression-final]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4686:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4686:029 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Entangled in oppression and unable to change, one has not attained the Way. This is handling the situation inappropriately. Knowing that movement brings regret, one thereupon feels regret and leaves the condition, and can emerge from oppression. Thus action brings good fortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4686:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4686:030 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4686:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4686:031 -->

**Lu Xisheng:** Acting obtains good fortune; hence change brings passage.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4686:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4686:032 -->

**Tian Chou:** The other commentators all join “good fortune” and “taking action” into one phrase. This is wrong. “Movement brings regret; repentance brings good fortune” is one phrase; “taking action” is another. Movement and repentance bring good fortune because one can act and attain it. The words “taking action” explain the line's “going brings good fortune.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4686:032 -->

<a id="eee-4687"></a>

## 48. Jing 井 — The Well

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4687 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4687:001 -->

### Great Image: Water above wood: The Well. Thus the noble person cares for the people and encourages mutual assistance.[^j12-well-wood]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4687:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4687:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** Water above wood means moisture rising upward: the image of a well. Caring for the people is the ruler nourishing them; encouraging mutual assistance is enabling the people to nourish one another. Both draw on the well's nourishment.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4687:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4687:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Wood bears water and lifts it: the image of a vessel drawing water out of a well. The noble person observes the well's image and models himself on its virtue, caring for and drawing the people near, and encouraging the Way of mutual help. Caring for the people models the well's use; encouraging mutual assistance models its distribution.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4687:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4687:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4687:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4687:005 -->

**Master Zhang:** Nothing surpasses caring for the people and encouraging mutual assistance as a means of nourishing without exhaustion.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4687:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4687:006 -->

**Yang Hui:** Water's nature is to moisten below. Its ability to rise and moisten things above is the well's use.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4687:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4687:007 -->

**Zhu Xi's Classified Conversations:** Some explain the wood in “water above wood” as a drawing vessel. But later there is a jar, which is a pottery vessel. The phrase simply speaks of moisture rising upward to the tips of the tree. This is the image of well-water ascending.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4687:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4687:008 -->

**Further conversation:** As plants and trees grow, their moisture rises all the way to the treetops. This is the meaning of water above wood. For example, each morning sweet-flag leaves have water like pearls at their tips. This occurs even when they are kept in a closed room, so it is not dew. Question: “What does this have to do with a well?” Answer: “Water above wood resembles water originally at the bottom of a well being drawn upward to supply people's drinking. Hence the image is taken in this way.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4687:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4687:009 -->

**Li Xinchuan:** Encouraging mutual assistance means befriending, helping, and supporting one another.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4687:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4687:010 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** For the Great Image's water above wood, Zhu Xi's explanation must be preferred. For the Judgment Commentary's “entering beneath water and raising water,” Zheng's well-sweep explanation may stand alongside it. Caring for the people resembles Xun's wind spreading commands; encouraging mutual assistance resembles Kan's water pouring from one place to another.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4687:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4687:011 -->

### First Six: The well is muddy; no one drinks: it is below. No birds at the old well: abandoned by the time.[^j12-well-abandoned]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4687:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4687:012 -->

**Original Meaning.** This means that the time casts it aside.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4687:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4687:013 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Yin at the bottom of the well images mud. Without water and full of mud, it supplies no drink. If people do not drink, water is not drawn up and cannot reach birds; birds do not come either. This shows its inability to benefit things: the time casts it aside and leaves it unused. If it could at least reach birds, it would still accomplish some benefit. Here “abandon” is read in the rising tone, unlike the pronunciation of “lodging according to the time” in Qian.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4687:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4687:014 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4687:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4687:015 -->

**Kong Yingda:** Being below means being at the very bottom of the well; hence mud. Abandonment by the time means that neither people drink nor birds approach: everyone at that time casts it aside.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4687:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4687:016 -->

### Nine in the Second Place: The well's hollow shoots water upon the small fish: no associate.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4687:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4687:017 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** A well accomplishes its work by bringing water upward. The second's yang-firm ability could originally be put to useful service. But it remains below without corresponding assistance above, and so joins below and pours upon the small fish. With an associate above, it would be drawn up and accomplish the well's work.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4687:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4687:018 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4687:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4687:019 -->

**Gu Jiajie:** Having spring water but no associate is plainly different from having no spring and being cast aside by the time.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4687:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4687:020 -->

### Nine in the Third Place: The well is cleansed, but no one drinks: passersby grieve. Seeking an enlightened king: receiving blessing.[^j12-well-grief]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4687:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4687:021 -->

**Original Meaning.** Passersby grieve means that everyone traveling along the road feels sorrow over it.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4687:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4687:022 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The well is cleansed and put in order, yet no one drinks: a person has talent and understanding yet is not employed. Sorrow comes from being unable to put them into action. Grieving over that inability, how could one avoid seeking an opportunity? Thus one seeks an enlightened king and receives blessing: the intention is earnest in wishing to act.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4687:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4687:023 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4687:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4687:024 -->

**Zhao Rumei:** The well does not grieve because no one drinks, nor does the worthy person grieve over lack of recognition. Those whose hearts are sorrowful are the passersby. People coming to draw water seek an enlightened king on the worthy person's behalf. Is seeking an enlightened king forming a faction to obtain a stipend? No: recognition above would spread blessing among the living people, just as a well benefits others only when water is drawn out.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4687:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4687:025 -->

**Wang Shenzi:** If the cleansed well supplies no drink, even if it does not grieve for itself, passersby grieve for it. Even if one does not seek employment oneself, others seek it on one's behalf so that all may share the blessing.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4687:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4687:026 -->

### Six in the Fourth Place: The well is lined with masonry; no blame: repairing the well.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4687:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4687:027 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Lining with masonry means repairing the well. Though unable to enlarge its service to things, it can maintain itself without falling into disuse. Hence no blame: it can barely escape blame, nothing more. Firm yang would not ordinarily stop at this; for it, such a condition would merit blame.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4687:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4687:028 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4687:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4687:029 -->

**Yu Fan:** Repairing means putting in order. Building up the well with tiles or bricks is called lining it with masonry.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4687:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4687:030 -->

**Su Shi:** Repairing means cleansing. Yang is movement and substance; yin is stillness and openness. The spring makes the well a well: it moves and is substantial. The well is where the spring abides: it is still and open. The first six is lowest, hence mud; the top six is highest, hence drawing up. The fourth six occupies the space between without losing correctness, hence masonry lining. This keeps harmful things out and the well clean. The well depends upon it for cleanliness, and therefore there is no blame.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4687:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4687:031 -->

### Nine in the Fifth Place: Cold spring water to drink: centered and correct.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4687:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4687:032 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Cold spring water fit to drink is the well's Way at its best. The fifth nine's centered and correct virtue expresses this utmost goodness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4687:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4687:033 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** The Odes says, “The spring is exhausted, yet none says it comes from within.” Without centeredness, the source is not continually abundant and the water is not cold. It also says, “Cold is the spring below, soaking the clustered southernwood.” Without correctness, the flow does not reach those below and provides no drink.[^j12-well-spring]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4687:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4687:034 -->

### Top Six: Supreme good fortune at the top: great completion.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4687:034 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4687:035 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The good fortune of great goodness at the hexagram's top is the great completion of the well's Way. For a well, reaching the top is accomplishing its work.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4687:035 -->

<a id="eee-4688"></a>

## 49. Ge 革 — Revolution

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4688 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4688:001 -->

### Great Image: Fire within the lake: Revolution. Thus the noble person orders the calendar and makes the seasons clear.[^j12-revolution-calendar]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4688:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4688:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** The changing of the four seasons is a great instance of revolution.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4688:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4688:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Water and fire extinguishing one another make Revolution. Revolution means change. Observing the image of transformation, the noble person follows the changing courses of sun, moon, and stars to regulate calendrical reckoning and clarify the sequence of the seasons. Among the workings of change, none has greater consequences, clearer principle, or more manifest traces than the four seasons. Observing them and following their changes brings one's ordering into accord with Heaven and Earth.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4688:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4688:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4688:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4688:005 -->

**Yu Fan:** Calendrical images concern the sun, moon, and stars. Heaven and Earth change and the four seasons take shape; thus the noble person orders the calendar and makes the seasons clear.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4688:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4688:006 -->

**Zhu Xi's Classified Conversations:** Ordering the calendar and making the seasons clear does not mean that the calendar itself must be reformed. Rather, the transformations of the four seasons contain the principle of ordering the calendar and clarifying the seasons.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4688:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4688:007 -->

### First Nine: Bound fast with yellow oxhide: no action can yet be undertaken.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4688:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4688:008 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The first nine's time, position, and capacities all forbid action. Therefore it should make itself firm through centered compliance.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4688:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4688:009 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4688:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4688:010 -->

**Hu Yuan:** In the Way of reform, the day must first pass before change can be made. When the people do not yet trust on the very first day, can one suddenly impose change? One may only preserve centered compliance firmly; great action cannot yet be undertaken.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4688:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4688:011 -->

**Zheng Ruxie:** In a low position, one may reform without gaining followers. At reform's beginning, sudden change may not win trust. Firmly preserve centered compliance, following principle and adapting accordingly. Initiating action from oneself is not permissible. This is said at the beginning of Revolution to teach care at the outset.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4688:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4688:012 -->

### Six in the Second Place: After the day has passed, make the change: action brings what is admirable.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4688:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4688:013 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Making the change after the day has passed brings good fortune without blame in going, because action brings admirable blessing. One can reform the world's abuses and renew its affairs. To remain without acting would show no heart for remedying abuses and aiding the world, and would miss the time and incur blame.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4688:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4688:014 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4688:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4688:015 -->

**Yu Yan:** Going suddenly before change is called for only multiplies abuses. What would be admirable in that? One must go after the day has passed, when change is fitting; then action has admirable achievement. “Action” explains going, and “admirable” explains good fortune without blame.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4688:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4688:016 -->

### Nine in the Third Place: Words of reform agreed upon three times: where else would one go?[^j12-revolution-three]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4688:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4688:017 -->

**Original Meaning.** This says that the matter has been carefully examined.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4688:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4688:018 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Consulting the judgments of the multitude until agreement has been reached three times makes the affair entirely fitting. “Where else would one go?” is simply the familiar expression, “Where further?” Acting in this way follows principle and the time, rather than one's private desire to act. It must find what is fitting.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4688:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4688:019 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4688:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4688:020 -->

**Xu Ji:** At the first line change is not yet possible; at the second it is made; at the third the undertaking of reform is completed. Carefully examining an affair a second and a third time is enough. Where further would one go?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4688:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4688:021 -->

### Nine in the Fourth Place: The good fortune of changing the mandate: the intention is trusted.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4688:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4688:022 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Changing the mandate brings good fortune because above and below trust the intention. Once sincerity is complete, they trust. Reform rests upon the trust of those above and below. What is unfitting and insincere gains no trust. Even a fitting reform cannot proceed without trust; how much less an unfitting one!

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4688:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4688:023 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4688:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4688:024 -->

**Gong Huan:** Trust in the intention means sincerity. Reform rests upon sincerity. When trust is sufficient to reach human hearts, the mandate may be changed with good fortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4688:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4688:025 -->

### Nine in the Fifth Place: The great person changes like a tiger: its pattern shines brightly.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4688:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4688:026 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The principles of affairs are clear and manifest, like the tiger's brilliant and radiant markings. Could anyone under Heaven fail to trust?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4688:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4688:027 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4688:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4688:028 -->

**Yu Yan:** A tiger's markings are large and open. The Way of reform is completed, and affairs and principles are simple and clear, like the tiger's shining pattern.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4688:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4688:029 -->

### Top Six: The noble person changes like a leopard: its pattern is rich and dense. The petty person changes his face: compliantly following the ruler.[^j12-revolution-face]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4688:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4688:030 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The noble person follows the transforming influence and moves toward goodness, forming a rich and cultivated pattern manifest outwardly. Those of middling capacity and above all change. Even the unalterable petty person no longer dares indulge his evil. Changing his outward appearance to comply with the ruler's teachings and commands is changing the face. At this point the Way of reform is complete. The noble person tolerates the petty person's constrained assumption of goodness. To go further and keep correcting him would bring misfortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4688:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4688:031 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4688:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4688:032 -->

**Master Zhang:** Taking yieldingness as its virtue, it falls short of the fifth nine's centered firmness and brilliant clarity. Thus its patterned expression is merely rich and intricate, though it can make petty people alter their appearance and follow.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4688:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4688:033 -->

**Lü Dalin:** The top six and fifth nine both occupy the time when reform's Way is complete. The tiger's markings are long and large, with clear order; the leopard's are dense and abundant, forming spots. A shining pattern resembles fire's light, easily distinguished. A rich pattern resembles flourishing plants clustered together.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4688:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4688:034 -->

**Yu Yan:** At reform's end, the petty person turns decisively toward the Way and follows the ruler. None fails to rejoice inwardly and submit sincerely. Some say that the face changes but the heart does not. That is wrong.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4688:034 -->

<a id="eee-4689"></a>

## 50. Ding 鼎 — The Cauldron

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4689 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4689:001 -->

### Great Image: Fire above wood: The Cauldron. Thus the noble person makes his position correct and consolidates the mandate.[^j12-cauldron-mandate]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4689:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4689:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** The cauldron is a weighty vessel; hence the meaning of correcting position and consolidating the mandate. “Consolidate” is as in “the highest Way is not consolidated,” and in the tradition's expression “harmonizing above and below to receive Heaven's favor.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4689:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4689:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Fire above wood means wood entering fire: the image of cooking, and so The Cauldron. Observing it, the noble person corrects his position and consolidates the mandate. The cauldron is a vessel embodying a model. Its form is upright and correct; its body stable and weighty. Taking upright correctness as a model, one corrects the position one occupies. The noble person's place must be correct, down to not sitting upon an improperly placed mat and not standing unevenly or leaning. Taking stability and weight as a model, one consolidates one's commands, making them settled and weighty. Consolidation means gathering and coming to rest: stability and weight. The ordinary expression “firmly settled” has this meaning. Commands are named here, but all action should be stable and weighty.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4689:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4689:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4689:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4689:005 -->

**Fang Qiao:** The cauldron is a sacred vessel, supremely great and weighty. Correcting position and consolidating the mandate model its greatness and weight, not allowing them to shift.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4689:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4689:006 -->

**Li Yuanliang:** Fire above wood is not the cauldron itself but its use, just as water above wood is not the well itself but the well's work.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4689:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4689:007 -->

**Zheng Ruxie:** Revolution changes the mandate; The Cauldron settles it. Knowing Revolution without knowing The Cauldron only increases disorder throughout the world.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4689:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4689:008 -->

**Xiang Anshi:** Preserving the spirit and quieting vital energy are how a person consolidates the span of life. With no contrivance in the heart, preserving utmost correctness is how a ruler consolidates Heaven's mandate.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4689:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4689:009 -->

**Wang Shenzi:** The cauldron's form is upright and correct, its body settled and weighty. The noble person models its uprightness to correct his position, making it more secure with time. He models its settled weight to consolidate the mandate he has received, making it firmer with time.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4689:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4689:010 -->

**Hu Bingwen:** Only when the cauldron is upright can it hold firmly the substance it receives; only when the ruler's position is correct can he consolidate the mandate he receives.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4689:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4689:011 -->

### First Six: The cauldron's feet overturned: not yet contrary to the Way. Discharging what is foul is beneficial: following the honored one.[^j12-cauldron-overturning]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4689:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4689:012 -->

**Original Meaning.** A cauldron with its feet overturned contradicts its proper Way. Yet because this allows the foul to be discharged and the honored to be followed, it is not contrary. Following the honored means responding to the fourth and also taking in what is new.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4689:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4689:013 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** A cauldron overturned with its feet in the air contradicts its Way. Yet it is not necessarily contrary, because there are times for tipping out what is foul and bad. Removing the old and receiving the new, pouring away the bad and taking in the good, express following what is honored. Corresponding to the fourth is following the honored one above.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4689:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4689:014 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4689:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4689:015 -->

**Lu Xisheng:** The feet should support the cauldron. To overturn it is contrary; yet the foul is thereby discharged, so overturning is not contrary. Likewise, a concubine is lowly and should not ordinarily be honored, yet gains honor through her son. This is the Spring and Autumn principle that the mother is honored through the son.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4689:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4689:016 -->

**Zheng Ruxie:** The first, being below, is the cauldron's feet. They must be overturned to discharge the foul. Likewise, a concubine may follow above because of her son: when the son is honored, the mother is honored. Taking in the new requires abandoning evil for good and the lowly for the honored, seeking conformity with rightness. The first's response to the fourth is overturning the feet and following the honored one. A yielding line responding above must have this justification before it is permissible.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4689:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4689:017 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** The Commentary does not separately explain obtaining a concubine, but includes it within following the honored. The sage's words are concise yet complete in this way.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4689:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4689:018 -->

### Nine in the Second Place: The cauldron has substance: be careful where you go. My counterpart has an ailment: in the end, no reproach.[^j12-cauldron-counterpart]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4689:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4689:019 -->

**Original Meaning.** Having substance without being careful where one goes allows one's counterpart to approach and entrap one in evil.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4689:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4689:020 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Substance in the cauldron is talent and achievement in a person. One must be careful of the direction one takes. Without care, one too falls into what is not right. The second avoids intimacy with the first and follows its proper correspondent, the fifth six, above: this is care in where one goes. “My counterpart has an ailment” cites the preceding wording. The counterpart is what stands in relation to oneself: the first. Adjoining oneself without a correct correspondence is its ailment. Preserving oneself in correctness prevents it from approaching, and so there is no fault or reproach in the end.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4689:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4689:021 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4689:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4689:022 -->

**Master Zhang:** Yang occupying the center gives substance. To possess substance and contend with others would bring great loss. Thus care in where one goes is indispensable.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4689:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4689:023 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Reproach may mean one's own fault or another's resentment. Care in one's conduct leaves no fault to point to, even if one's counterpart has a harmful intention. The thought of resentment then disappears as well.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4689:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4689:024 -->

### Nine in the Third Place: The cauldron's ears are altered: the fitting relation is lost.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4689:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4689:025 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Initial estrangement from the cauldron's ears loses the fitting relation of mutual seeking. There is no correspondence with the fifth, so the Way of seeking union is lost. Lack of centeredness gives no image of shared intentions; hence its movement is blocked. Yet clarity above and talent below must ultimately unite harmoniously. Thus rain is about to come, and there is good fortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4689:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4689:026 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** When the Image Commentary speaks of rightness, it means the hexagram's meaning. Here losing the fitting relation does not mean that one's conduct is unjust. It means that the line images lack a corresponding relation.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4689:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4689:027 -->

### Nine in the Fourth Place: Spilling the duke's food: what becomes of trust?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4689:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4689:028 -->

**Original Meaning.** This speaks of losing trust.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4689:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4689:029 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** A great minister bearing responsibility for the world must bring it to peace and order. Only then does he avoid betraying the ruler's reliance, the people's expectations, and his own resolve to devote himself to the Way. Not failing what was expected is what is called trustworthiness. Otherwise he loses his office's duty and betrays the superior's commission. Could that be called trustworthy? Hence “what becomes of trust?”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4689:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4689:030 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4689:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4689:031 -->

**Yang Jian:** Occupying the great minister's position is promising the state the work of a great minister. When the reality does not match, the feet break and the food spills: the promise made to the state is broken.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4689:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4689:032 -->

### Six in the Fifth Place: The cauldron's yellow ears: taking the mean as substance.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4689:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4689:033 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The fifth six is good because it attains the mean: it takes centeredness as substantial virtue. Its clear hearing and sight, response to firmness, governance of the cauldron, and attainment of the cauldron's Way all arise from attaining the mean.[^j12-cauldron-reading]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4689:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4689:034 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4689:034 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4689:035 -->

**Lu Ji:** Attaining the center and receiving yang above: hence taking the mean as substance.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4689:035 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4689:036 -->

**Guo Yong:** Taking the mean as substance means that the fifth six, though yin and open, makes the virtue of yellow centeredness its substance. This resembles Kun's fifth six, with beauty within.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4689:036 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4689:037 -->

### Top Nine: The jade lifting-bar above: firmness and yieldingness are in measure.[^j12-cauldron-jade]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4689:037 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4689:038 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Firmness joined to warmth has measure. The top occupies the place of completed achievement and realized use; firmness and yieldingness meet the right measure. Thus there is great good fortune and nothing unfavorable. Both The Well and The Cauldron complete their work through bringing something out above. Why does The Cauldron not say “supreme good fortune”? Answer: the well's entire work and use lie in drawing water upward, together with the virtues of broad distribution and constancy. Hence supreme good fortune. The cauldron's work is cooking, and the top is its completion. Its virtue differs from the well's; because firmness and yieldingness are in measure, it obtains great good fortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4689:038 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4689:039 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4689:039 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4689:040 -->

**Xiong Liangfu:** The top occupies yieldingness with firmness. Hence firmness and yieldingness are in measure, and its virtue is compared to jade.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4689:040 -->

<a id="eee-4690"></a>

## 51. Zhen 震 — Shock

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4690 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4690:001 -->

### Great Image: Thunder repeated: Shock. Thus the noble person, in fear and apprehension, cultivates and examines himself.[^j12-shock-cultivation]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4690:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4690:002 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Repeated means coming again in succession. Above and below are both Zhen, so thunder is repeated. As thunder follows thunder, its authority grows more powerful. Observing this image of repeated thunder's awe, the noble person responds with apprehension, self-correction, and self-examination. Fearing Heaven's authority, he sets his person right, reflects on his faults, and corrects them. This applies not only to thunder: every frightening encounter should be met in this way.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4690:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4690:003 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4690:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4690:004 -->

**Xiang Anshi:** Apprehension followed by self-cultivation and examination is itself repetition. A person who feels apprehension has already been shaken. Adding cultivation and examination gives repetition within that response.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4690:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4690:005 -->

**Hu Bingwen:** Fear and apprehension arise in the heart; cultivation and examination appear in affairs. Cultivation is the work of mastering and correcting; examination is the effort of careful discernment.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4690:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4690:006 -->

### First Nine: Shock comes, fearful and watchful: fear brings blessing. Laughing words follow: afterward there is a rule.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4690:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4690:007 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** When shock arrives, apprehension and careful attention to every side prevent trouble. Thus fear can instead bring blessing. Apprehension leads to cultivation and examination, with no daring to depart from proper measures. Shock is followed by a rule. One can therefore preserve security and good fortune, and speak with laughter.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4690:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4690:008 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4690:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4690:009 -->

**Fan Zhongyan:** When the noble person's heart is apprehensive, he carefully attends to the beginnings of thought, so none of his intentions departs from the Way. When his person is apprehensive, advancement and withdrawal do not tread into danger, so none of his actions encounters calamity. Thus the first nine receives shock and brings blessing through care at the beginning.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4690:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4690:010 -->

### Six in the Second Place: Shock comes with danger: riding upon firmness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4690:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4690:011 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Meeting shock while riding upon firmness means that the other's severity makes oneself endangered. When Shock's firmness comes, how could it be resisted?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4690:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4690:012 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4690:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4690:013 -->

**Hu Bingwen:** Zhun's second six, Enthusiasm's fifth six, Biting Through's second six, Oppression's third six, and Shock's second six all speak of riding upon firmness. Only Oppression's third six rides upon Kan's middle line; all the others ride upon Zhen's first line.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4690:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4690:014 -->

### Six in the Third Place: Shock leaves one shaken and scattered: the position is not appropriate.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4690:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4690:015 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Fear makes one lose oneself and become scattered because one's situation is inappropriate. Neither centered nor correct, how could one be secure?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4690:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4690:016 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Shock should produce watchful apprehension, not scattered faintness. The third six stands between the two Shocks, precisely when vigorous effort should undertake action. Yet yielding yin without centeredness or correctness occupies it and becomes slack and dispersed. Hence the position is not appropriate.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4690:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4690:017 -->

### Nine in the Fourth Place: Shock sinks into mud: not yet radiant.[^j12-shock-radiance]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4690:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4690:018 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Yang is firm and Shock means movement. Firmness in movement should possess a Way of radiant passage. Yet it loses firm correctness and becomes trapped among repeated yin, sinking into mud. How could it be radiant? “Not yet radiant” shows that yang firmness was originally capable of vigorous movement. It is only through losing its virtue that it becomes mired.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4690:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4690:019 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** The fourth has firm virtue; it has not lost virtue. Lack of radiance means that its will and spirit cannot yet fulfill themselves and its actions are obstructed and thrown into disorder. This resembles Biting Through's fourth nine not yet being radiant. Both concern a situation that prevents fulfillment of the intention, unlike Joy's top line not being radiant.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4690:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4690:020 -->

### Six in the Fifth Place: Shock in going and coming; danger: action is perilous. Its task lies in the center: greatly without loss.[^j12-shock-center]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4690:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4690:021 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Going and coming are both dangerous; action involves peril. Since every movement is dangerous, the essential thing is not to lose one's task. That task means the mean. Not losing the mean enables self-preservation. Great freedom from loss means that not losing is what matters greatly.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4690:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4690:022 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4690:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4690:023 -->

**Master Zhang:** Having no loss and retaining one's task is like not losing what one possesses. Riding upon firmness brings danger; occupying the center prevents loss. Apprehension both when calamity arrives and when it does not preserves the task without loss.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4690:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4690:024 -->

**Guo Yong:** The second loses valuables when shock comes dangerously. Since the fifth meets danger in both going and coming, one might expect great loss. Yet, though its position is not formally correct, it employs the mean. Its affairs do not lose the middle Way; even in perilous action, it may be greatly without loss.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4690:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4690:025 -->

### Top Six: Shock leaves one trembling: nothing has been attained within. Though there is misfortune, no blame: taking warning from the neighbor.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4690:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4690:026 -->

**Original Meaning.** Within means within the heart.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4690:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4690:027 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Such fearful loss of composure comes from not attaining the middle Way: it goes beyond the mean. Were the mean attained, trembling would not reach this point. At the utmost, further advancement brings misfortune. But seeing the warning in one's neighbor and becoming apprehensive allows change before the extreme is reached, and so there is no blame. The top six is movement at its utmost, and shock at its utmost includes the meaning of change.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4690:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4690:028 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4690:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4690:029 -->

**Wu Cheng:** Taking warning from the neighbor means learning apprehension from the neighbor's warning.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4690:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4690:030 -->

**Gong Huan:** Nothing attained within means that at Shock's utmost, will and spirit are dispersed and the heart has no governing center.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4690:030 -->

<a id="eee-4691"></a>

## 52. Gen 艮 — Keeping Still

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4691 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4691:001 -->

### Great Image: Mountain beside mountain: Keeping Still. Thus the noble person's thought does not go beyond his proper place.[^j12-stillness-thought]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4691:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4691:002 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Above and below are both mountains; hence mountain beside mountain. Placing this together with that is doubling: the image of repeated Gen. The noble person observes Keeping Still and considers how to rest where stopping is proper, without going beyond his place. Place means the allotted situation one occupies. Every affair has its proper ground. Attain that ground, and one stops securely. Stopping when action is called for, delaying when speed is required, exceeding or falling short—all go beyond one's proper place. How much more overstepping one's share and occupying what is not one's own!

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4691:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4691:003 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4691:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4691:004 -->

**Master Dong:** Two thunders, winds, fires, waters, or lakes can all move toward and away from one another. Only two mountains stand together without such movement: this is the image of stillness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4691:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4691:005 -->

**Qiu Fuguo:** People easily go beyond their place because they fail to think. Thinking awakens understanding in the heart, enabling one to know where stopping is called for and attain that proper stopping-place.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4691:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4691:006 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** The other commentators take the phrase as thinking of not going beyond one's place, giving little weight to thought itself. Yet Influence says, “Steadfastness brings good fortune; regret vanishes. Restless coming and going; companions follow your thoughts,” and Confucius explains it through “what thinking, what deliberation?” Thus “thought” here must not be passed over. Distracted thought is stirred by desire; thought penetrating the subtle is deepened in principle. The Great Learning says, “After being at ease, one can deliberate.” This explains thought not going beyond its proper place.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4691:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4691:007 -->

### First Six: Keeping the toes still: correctness has not been lost.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4691:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4691:008 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Acting when one ought to stop is not correct. Stopping at the beginning prevents the loss of correctness. Arresting an affair at its outset is easy, before error has occurred.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4691:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4691:009 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4691:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4691:010 -->

**Yu Fan:** In movement it attains correctness; hence correctness has not been lost.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4691:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4691:011 -->

**Guo Yong:** The toes image the first line. Nothing precedes the toes in movement. Stopping before movement is easy; stopping after movement is difficult. The Commentary's “correctness has not been lost” means stopping before movement, before any loss of correctness has occurred.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4691:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4691:012 -->

### Six in the Second Place: Unable to rescue the one it follows: that one does not step back and listen.[^j12-stillness-following]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4691:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4691:013 -->

**Original Meaning.** The third stops above and refuses to step back and listen to the second.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4691:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4691:014 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** One cannot rescue but can only follow because the person above cannot humble himself and listen. Stepping back to listen means following the one below.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4691:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4691:015 -->

### Nine in the Third Place: Keeping the waist still: danger scorches the heart.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4691:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4691:016 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** This means stopping rigidly, unable to advance or retreat. Anxious thoughts of danger continually scorch the heart within.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4691:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4691:017 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4691:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4691:018 -->

**Zheng Ruxie:** Though the third stops and does not interact with things, its danger truly scorches the heart.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4691:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4691:019 -->

**He Kai:** Forceful repression is why danger scorches the heart. Keeping the waist still means such forcible control.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4691:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4691:020 -->

### Six in the Fourth Place: Keeping the body still: stilling it in one's own person.[^j12-stillness-body]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4691:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4691:021 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Unable to bring the world to its proper stopping-place, one can only still one's own person. How could this suffice for the great minister's position?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4691:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4691:022 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4691:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4691:023 -->

**Kong Yingda:** “Stilling it in one's own person”: person means body. This makes clear that one can keep the body quiet and still, without restless movement.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4691:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4691:024 -->

**Wang Yinglin:** Gen's fourth six says to keep the body still; the Image explains it through *gong*, one's person. A bowed back is *gong*: the back is seen, not the face. Zhu Xi's poem says, “Turn back upon the person, keeping the back still.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4691:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4691:025 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Stilling it in one's own person is simply keeping the body still, but with the word *zhu* replacing *qi*. That substitution shows that this is stilling it at the person, somewhat different from correcting the root and clearing the source so that stillness comes naturally. Wang's explanation is retained as an additional interpretation.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4691:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4691:026 -->

### Six in the Fifth Place: Keeping the jaws still: through centered correctness.[^j12-stillness-rhyme]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4691:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4691:027 -->

**Original Meaning.** “Correctness” is surplus text, as the rhyme makes clear.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4691:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4691:028 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The fifth's goodness is centeredness. Keeping the jaws still means stopping in the mean. Attaining the mean gives correctness. Keep the jaws still so that the mean is not lost, and correctness is attained.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4691:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4691:029 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4691:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4691:030 -->

**Yu Ben:** Words are not released recklessly; when released, they must accord with principle. Only someone possessing centered virtue can do this.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4691:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4691:031 -->

### Top Nine: The good fortune of substantial stillness: bringing the ending to fullness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4691:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4691:032 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** In all the world's affairs, preserving something to the end is what is difficult. One who is substantial in stillness brings it to completion. The top's good fortune comes from making its ending substantial.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4691:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4691:033 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4691:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4691:034 -->

**Wang Shenzi:** The more substantial the virtue, the more secure the stillness. This is a good completion of stillness, and its good fortune is evident.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4691:034 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4691:035 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Gen is where all things complete their endings and form their beginnings. Thus the top speaks of making the ending substantial. In the human heart, the concern is insufficient depth of nourishment, not insufficient brilliance of expression. Stored water grows fuller; banked fire grows stronger. Make the ending substantial, and every affair takes its beginning from this.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4691:035 -->

<a id="eee-4692"></a>

## 53. Jian 漸 — Gradual Progress

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4692 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4692:001 -->

### Great Image: Wood upon the mountain: Gradual Progress. Thus the noble person abides in worthy virtue and improves the customs.[^j12-gradual-virtue]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4692:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4692:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** Both must advance gradually. “Worthy” may be surplus, or something may be missing after “improves.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4692:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4692:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Wood upon a mountain owes its height to a foundation: the meaning of Gradual Progress. Observing it, the noble person abides in worthy and good virtue and transforms customs for the better. Progress in worthy virtue must be gradual. Only practice brings ease; one cannot skip the stages and arrive suddenly. This is true even within oneself. How, then, could instruction enter other people without gradual development? Customs cannot be changed in a morning or an evening. Thus improving them must be gradual.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4692:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4692:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4692:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4692:005 -->

**Master Yang:** Wood growing within the earth rises with the time; wood upon the mountain advances gradually.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4692:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4692:006 -->

**Feng Dangke:** Abiding means accumulating. Virtue accumulates gradually; customs improve gradually. The inner trigram, Gen, stops: abiding in virtue is stopping within. The outer trigram, Xun, enters: improving customs is entering what is outside. Embody Gen to abide in virtue and Xun to improve customs.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4692:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4692:007 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Wood growing within the earth is newly growing wood; wood upon the mountain is tall and large. Newly growing branches lengthen rapidly, changing between morning and evening. Once the tree is tall and large, growth from a hand's girth to an arm's embrace, from the reach of a hand to piercing the clouds, requires passing years of accumulation. This distinguishes Pushing Upward from Gradual Progress. Both abiding in virtue and improving customs require gradual development. Moreover, one must abide in worthy virtue before one can improve customs: this too is gradual progress.[^j12-gradual-tree]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4692:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4692:008 -->

### First Six: The young one's danger: by rightness, no blame.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4692:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4692:009 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Though the young person regards it as dangerous, in terms of rightness and principle there is truly no blame.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4692:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4692:010 -->

### Six in the Second Place: Eating and drinking at ease: not taking one's fill for nothing.[^j12-gradual-sustenance]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4692:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4692:011 -->
