**Original Meaning.** Taking one's fill for nothing is like the Odes' “eating without earning.” Obtaining sustenance through the Way means not merely filling oneself, and one rests at ease.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4692:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4692:012 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The line describes secure, peaceful progress through harmonious eating and drinking. Confucius feared that later readers would misunderstand and further explains: a noble person of centered correctness meets a ruler of centered correctness and gradually advances above, intending to put the Way into practice throughout the world. Eating and drinking at ease means the harmonious joy of fulfilling that intention, not merely an empty filling of the stomach. “For nothing” means empty.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4692:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4692:013 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4692:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4692:014 -->

**Gong Huan:** The second receives a stipend through centered correctness responding to the fifth. It is not comparable to those who hold office idly and eat without service. Thus it eats at ease and rejoices.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4692:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4692:015 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** The six lines take the wild goose as an image of advancement: from the water's edge to the mountain, from distant to near, from low to high. The bank is the most distant place, representing someone about to advance who does not advance heedlessly. Thus the Odes says, “Set it by the riverbank; that noble person does not eat without earning.” Although the second has advanced into timely service and progressed to the rock, it does not forget this principle. This is not changing from what one was in adversity upon attaining success.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4692:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4692:016 -->

### Nine in the Third Place: The husband goes and does not return: leaving his fellows. The wife conceives but does not bring forth: losing the Way. Resisting raiders is beneficial: preserving one another through compliance.[^j12-gradual-household]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4692:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4692:017 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** A husband going without returning loses the correctness of gradual progress. Following desire and losing correctness separates him from his fellows and is shameful. The other lines are all good; alone losing correctness separates this line from their company. The wife's conception does not follow the proper Way, and so she does not bring forth. The benefit of resisting raiders means preserving one another through the compliant Way. When a noble person adjoins a petty person and preserves correctness, he does more than keep himself whole: he also prevents the petty person from falling into what is not right. This is preserving one another through compliance. Blocking the other's evil is therefore called resisting raiders.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4692:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4692:018 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4692:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4692:019 -->

**Yang Jian:** The husband going without returning means that the top nine does not respond: leaving his fellows. The wife conceiving without bringing forth means that the third nine loses what makes it a wife. The third lacks centeredness and images loss of the Way; hence misfortune. What is not correct can harm me and is therefore called a raider. Concern that the third may lose the Way, befriend raiders, and fail to resist them prompts the teaching to resist. Then I do not lose correct compliance, and husband and wife can preserve one another.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4692:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4692:020 -->

**Xiong Liangfu:** In preserving one another through compliance, “compliant” and “careful” are interchangeable. It simply means carefully protecting one another.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4692:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4692:021 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Yang's explanation captures both the line's meaning and the wording. In serving, even if the superior does not communicate with him, the noble person must fulfill his own Way. Thus the Duke of Zhou says, “Such affection, such diligence; such care in rearing the young.” Lack of recognition does not justify abandoning this devoted effort. Wang Zhongyan said, “How admirable was the Duke of Zhou's service to Zhou! He would surely make ruler and ministers secure together, preventing calamity and disorder.” Is this not preserving one another through compliance?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4692:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4692:022 -->

### Six in the Fourth Place: Perhaps finding a level branch: compliance joined to deference.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4692:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4692:023 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The level branch is a secure place. The Way of seeking security lies only in compliance and deference. With rightness compliant and correct, and one's station humble and deferential, where could security be absent? Such compliance and deference in the fourth find the level branch.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4692:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4692:024 -->

### Nine in the Fifth Place: In the end, nothing can overcome it; good fortune: obtaining what is desired.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4692:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4692:025 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Ruler and minister meet through centered correctness, and their Way should prevail. Though something intervenes between them, how could it ultimately prevail against them? In time, what they desire must be attained. This is Gradual Progress's good fortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4692:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4692:026 -->

### Top Nine: Its feathers may serve as an adornment and model; good fortune: it cannot be thrown into disorder.[^j12-gradual-feathers]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4692:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4692:027 -->

**Original Meaning.** Progressing ever higher, it does not thereby become useless. Its intention stands loftily apart. How could it be thrown into disorder?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4692:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4692:028 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The noble person's advancement moves from below to above and from obscurity to prominence. Every half-step and momentary action follows an order. Without losing that order, good fortune is everywhere attainable. Thus, though the nine reaches the utmost height, it does not lose good fortune. It can serve as an exemplary rule because it possesses an order that cannot be disturbed.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4692:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4692:029 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4692:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4692:030 -->

**Hu Bingwen:** The second occupies a useful position, benefits another's state and household, and does not eat for nothing. The top stands where ordinary use is absent, yet can still serve as an example and is not useless. The second's intention is not warmth and a full stomach; the top's intention stands loftily apart and cannot be disturbed. Officials should draw upon this in their service and retirement.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4692:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4692:031 -->

**Zhang Zhenyuan:** Lofty and pure intentions are not burdened by achievement, reputation, wealth, or rank. Thus the intention may serve as a rule. If it could be thrown into disorder, how could it serve as an example?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4692:031 -->

<a id="eee-4693"></a>

## 54. Guimei 歸妹 — The Marrying Maiden

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4693 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4693:001 -->

### Great Image: Thunder above the lake: The Marrying Maiden. Thus the noble person considers how to preserve the ending and understands what brings decay.[^j12-maiden-ending]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4693:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4693:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** Thunder moves and the lake follows: the image of The Marrying Maiden. Observing that the union is not correct, the noble person knows that its ending will bring decay. Extended to things and affairs, the principle applies everywhere.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4693:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4693:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Thunder shakes above, and the lake moves after it. Yang moves above; yin rejoices and follows: the image of a woman following a man, hence The Marrying Maiden. Observing men and women joining, and generation continuing in succession, the noble person seeks to preserve the ending and knows that decay is possible. Preserving the ending means continuing generation and making its transmission enduring. Knowing decay means recognizing that things break down and establishing a Way of succession. A woman's marriage allows generation, and so contains the meaning of an enduring ending. Moreover, the Way of husband and wife should be constant and carried through to the end. They must know the possibility of decay and guard carefully against it. Decay means estrangement and separation. The Marrying Maiden moves through pleasure, unlike Constancy's deferential movement or Gradual Progress's stillness joined to deference. The young woman's pleasure is emotion moved by attraction. Such movement loses correctness and is not the correct, enduring Way of husband and wife. In time it must decay. Knowing this, one should consider how to preserve the ending. All those in the world who turn against one another have failed to preserve it. This applies not only to husband and wife. Every affair has an ending and a possibility of decay; every affair has a Way of continuation and duration. Observing The Marrying Maiden, one should therefore attend to the warning about preserving the ending.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4693:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4693:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4693:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4693:005 -->

**Cui Jing:** The Marrying Maiden concerns human beginnings and endings. At the beginning, going brings misfortune; at the end, nothing is beneficial. Thus the noble person takes preserving the ending and knowing decay as his warning.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4693:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4693:006 -->

**Wu Yueshen:** Preserving the ending and knowing decay means thinking far ahead to the ending and recognizing the possibility of breakdown. In the Odes' “The Peasant,” failure to consider his reversal is why she is ultimately abandoned.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4693:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4693:007 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Thunder above the lake should not be imaged as the lake following thunder, but as the lake influencing thunder. It takes the prior stirring of yin energy as the meaning of The Marrying Maiden.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4693:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4693:008 -->

### First Nine: Marrying the maiden as an attendant wife: through constancy. The lame can walk; good fortune: assisting and supporting.[^j12-maiden-attendant]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4693:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4693:009 -->

**Original Meaning.** Constancy means possessing enduring virtue.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4693:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4693:010 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The Marrying Maiden moves through pleasure and is not the enduring Way of husband and wife. Yet the nine is firm yang and possesses worthy, steadfast virtue. Though lowly as an attendant wife, it can act with constancy. Below, it cannot undertake matters independently, like a lame person who can still walk. Going brings good fortune because it can assist and support. Helping her mistress is the attendant wife's good fortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4693:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4693:011 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4693:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4693:012 -->

**Zheng Ruxie:** The first is the youngest daughter, lowly and below. Marrying as an accompanying attendant is its constant role. An attendant wife cannot independently accomplish the work of assistance within the household; even with virtue, she is like the lame walking. Though insufficient to undertake the whole journey, the lame person can still walk. Likewise, by assisting the principal wife, she can support the work. Going in this way rests within her allotted role, and so is fortunate.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4693:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4693:013 -->

**Yu Yan:** Assisting and supporting means helping the principal wife as they serve the husband together.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4693:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4693:014 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Constancy is mentioned because a woman initiating her own marriage is not the prescribed norm; only the attendant wife who accompanies the principal bride proceeds according to her regular role.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4693:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4693:015 -->

### Nine in the Second Place: The secluded person's steadfastness is beneficial: the constant Way has not changed.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4693:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4693:016 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Preserving secluded steadfastness does not lose the constant, correct Way of husband and wife. Worldly people take familiar indulgence as the norm and regard steadfast quiet as a departure from it. They do not know that this is precisely the enduring Way.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4693:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4693:017 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4693:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4693:018 -->

**Yu Yan:** Zhun's second six says “returning to the constant Way.” Pledging herself is a woman's prescribed norm; not pledging herself is exceptional. Doing so after ten years returns to that norm. Here the text says the constant Way has not changed. Marriage is the woman's norm; the second nine's unwillingness to marry seems a change from it. Yet quiet self-preservation is also the constancy of woman's virtue, and so the constant Way has not been changed.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4693:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4693:019 -->

**Lai Zhide:** Once joined with him, she does not change for the remainder of life. This is the constant Way prescribed for a wife. Preserving the secluded person's steadfastness therefore does not change that constancy.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4693:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4693:020 -->

### Six in the Third Place: The marrying maiden must wait: the situation is not appropriate.[^j12-maiden-waiting]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4693:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4693:021 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Not appropriate means that her position, virtue, and manner of seeking marriage are all inappropriate. Thus no one takes her, and she must wait.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4693:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4693:022 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4693:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4693:023 -->

**Zhu Zhen:** The third six occupies an inappropriate place: its virtue is not correct. Yieldingness stands above firmness: its conduct is heedless. Governing Joy and marrying through pleasure, it moves without ritual propriety. With no correspondent above, no one receives it. Thus it becomes lowly, and the situation is called inappropriate. No one marries her; she returns to marry as an attendant wife.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4693:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4693:024 -->

### Nine in the Fourth Place: The intention behind delaying the appointed time: waiting before going.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4693:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4693:025 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The delay comes from herself, not from the other. A worthy woman is one whom people wish to marry. The delay therefore comes from her intention to wait: she waits for a worthy match before going.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4693:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4693:026 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4693:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4693:027 -->

**Kong Yingda:** Marriage should occur at the fitting time. Passing that time and marrying late expresses the bride's intention to wait for something before going.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4693:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4693:028 -->

**Yu Yan:** The line says that the time is delayed, while the Commentary directly describes her intention. This shows that the delay belongs to her own choice, not heedless compliance with another. Waiting before going is not being rejected by others. Going means marrying, as in the Odes' “Spring Water”: “A woman goes forth.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4693:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4693:029 -->

### Six in the Fifth Place: Di Yi marries off the maiden; her sleeves are not as fine as her attendant wife's. Her position is in the center: going forth in nobility.[^j12-maiden-sleeves]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4693:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4693:030 -->

**Original Meaning.** She proceeds with the nobility of centered virtue and therefore does not prize adornment.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4693:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4693:031 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** In the Way of Di Yi's marrying off the maiden, her sleeves are not as fine as her attendant wife's because ritual propriety is prized over adornment. The fifth, yielding and centered in a high and honored position, practices the middle Way from a position of nobility. Yielding compliance, self-lowering, and preferring propriety to adornment constitute that Way.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4693:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4693:032 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4693:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4693:033 -->

**Wang Shenzi:** The first two phrases cite the line statement, and the next two explain it. The fifth occupies the honored position but employs the mean; thus, from the highest nobility, she practices diligence, frugality, humility, and deference.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4693:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4693:034 -->

### Top Six: No substance: bearing an empty basket.[^j12-maiden-basket]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4693:034 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4693:035 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** A basket without contents is empty. Can an empty basket be offered in sacrifice? This says that sacrificial service cannot be performed. If the woman cannot carry out sacrificial service, separation is all that remains. This is a marriage that does not endure to its ending.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4693:035 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4693:036 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4693:036 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4693:037 -->

**Wang Zongchuan:** The empty basket without substance is singled out because the top six represents the woman.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4693:037 -->

<a id="eee-4694"></a>

## 55. Feng 豐 — Abundance

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4694 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:001 -->

### Great Image: Thunder and lightning arrive together: Abundance. Thus the noble person decides cases and carries out punishments.[^j12-abundance-justice]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** This takes the image of authority and illumination proceeding together.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Thunder and lightning arriving together mean clarity and shaking acting together. The two trigrams unite, hence both arrive. Clarity and movement assist one another and form the image of Abundance. Li is clarity, the image of illuminating and examining; Zhen is movement, the image of authoritative decision. Deciding cases requires illuminating the actual circumstances: only clarity can attain truth. Carrying out punishment imposes authority upon the crooked and wicked: only decision can accomplish it. Thus the noble person observes thunder and lightning's clarity and movement to decide cases and carry out punishments. Biting Through speaks of former kings ordering the laws, while Abundance speaks of the noble person deciding cases. Clarity above joined to awesome shaking is the king's work, hence establishing punishments and laws. Clarity below joined to awesome shaking is the noble person's application, hence deciding cases and carrying out punishments. Why does The Wanderer have clarity above yet speak of the noble person? It concerns caution in punishment and not prolonging imprisonment, which every noble person should practice.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:005 -->

**Kong Yingda:** Deciding cases requires discovering what is true and false in their circumstances. Employing punishment requires attaining the proper measure of severity and leniency. Movement without clarity produces excess and abuse. Thus the noble person models himself on this hexagram in deciding cases and carrying out punishments.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:006 -->

**Su Shi:** The tradition says, “Establish punishments and imposing prisons to resemble Heaven's shaking and brilliance.” Thus whenever thunder and lightning meet in the Changes, penal affairs are necessarily mentioned: the image is clarity joined to movement. When Li and Gen meet, the text says not to decide cases rashly and not to prolong imprisonment, taking clarity joined to stopping.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:007 -->

**Zhu Zhen:** Lightning's clear illumination is for deciding cases; thunder's awesome force is for carrying out punishment.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:008 -->

**Zhu Xi's Classified Conversations:** Question: “Are thunder and lightning in Biting Through and Abundance the same?” Answer: “In Biting Through, clarity is above. One understands the principles of affairs and first establishes the law; no one has yet offended its authority, and it awaits future application. Thus the text says to make punishments clear and put the laws in order. In Abundance, authority is above and clarity below. This is the time of applying the law, when one must clearly see all the turns and complications of circumstances below. Otherwise, authority moving above must make mistakes. Hence deciding cases and carrying out punishments.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:009 -->

### First Nine: Though evenly matched, no blame. Going beyond the match brings calamity.[^j12-abundance-equality]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:010 -->

**Original Meaning.** This warns the diviner not to seek superiority over the partner. It also develops a meaning beyond the line statement itself.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:011 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The sage responds fittingly to the time and follows principle in each affair. Ordinarily, equal power means that neither party submits to the other. Yet there are equals who assist and seek one another, as with the first and fourth. Hence no blame despite equality. When matching another in strength, one must humble oneself to seek the other and combine efforts in the work. Harboring a private wish to put oneself first and stand above the other must bring trouble. Hence going beyond the match brings calamity. To be equal yet put oneself first is to go beyond the match. Once one seeks to prevail, common purpose is impossible.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:012 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:013 -->

**Liu Mu:** *Xun* is the completion of a numerical cycle, like the sun at noon. No blame means that the first has not yet reached the middle and may still advance. Advance beyond the middle, however, and calamity follows. Hence the Image says that going beyond *xun* brings calamity. The line does not mention abundance because the first has not yet reached fullness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:014 -->

**Hu Yuan:** Even in a time of flourishing abundance, there may be no blame. But going beyond fullness must bring the calamity of collapse.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:015 -->

**Yu Yan:** The line says that even at *xun* there is no blame; its Commentary says that going beyond *xun* brings calamity. It warns against excess, agreeing with the Judgment Commentary's discussion of Heaven, Earth, sun, and moon.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:016 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Going beyond *xun* and meeting calamity has the same meaning as the sun declining after noon and the moon waning after fullness. The Classic means that those sharing virtue and assisting one another may be without blame even at fullness. How much more at abundance's beginning, before noon has been reached! The Commentary means that precisely now is the time to make provision. Go even slightly beyond the middle, and calamity will arrive. The meanings complement one another.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:017 -->

### Six in the Second Place: Sincerity brings awakening: trust awakens the intention.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:018 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Sincerity bringing awakening means that one's own sincerity and trustworthiness awaken the heart and intention above. If awakening is possible, good fortune is evident. Even yielding obscurity has a Way by which it can be awakened.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:019 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:020 -->

**Zhao Rumei:** Trouble arises from suspicion prompted by circumstances; awakening through sincerity arises from trust within the intention.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:021 -->

**Wang Shenzi:** The second is open within and therefore sincere; the fifth is also open within and therefore can be awakened. This speaks of mutual influence through sincerity.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:022 -->

### Nine in the Third Place: Thickening its covering: great affairs cannot be undertaken. Breaking its right arm: in the end, no use can be made of it.[^j12-abundance-obscurity]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:023 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The third responds to the top. That correspondent has no position, and yielding yin has neither power nor influence while occupying the end. Could they jointly accomplish great affairs? With nothing to rely upon, it is like a broken right arm: in the end, it cannot be put to use.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:024 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:025 -->

**Pan Shizao:** Though the second six meets a time of thick screens, the fifth is appropriately placed and centered, and great affairs remain possible. Thus the second's sincerity that awakens can be employed. The third nine's correspondent, the top six, has no brightness that can be awakened. Not employing what cannot be employed is the wisdom that preserves oneself.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:026 -->

### Nine in the Fourth Place: Thickening its screen: the position is not appropriate. Seeing the Dipper at noon: obscure and without clarity. Meeting its equal lord brings good fortune: taking action.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:027 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The position is inappropriate because one occupies a high station without centeredness or correctness, remaining dark and unable to bring abundance. Seeing the Dipper at noon means obscurity without illumination: the ruler is yielding yin and the minister neither centered nor correct. Meeting its equal lord brings good fortune through action: firm yang meeting firm yang is fortunate conduct. Going down to the first is called taking action. Seeking below brings good fortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:028 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:029 -->

**Xiang Anshi:** The second six identifies the fifth six as its screen and Dipper. Thus going enters darkness and meets suspicion. The fourth nine's screen and Dipper both refer to itself; hence action meets clarity and obtains good fortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:030 -->

**Wu Cheng:** Thickening the screen and seeing the Dipper are already fully stated in the second six's oracle, yet its Image does not explain them. Only the fourth nine's Image develops them in detail, because both images arise through the fourth. The fourth is the screen, so the second sees the Dipper. The two lines share the image, but its principal source lies in the fourth.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:031 -->

### Six in the Fifth Place: Its good fortune: there is blessing.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:032 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Its good fortune means that blessing can reach the whole world. Though the ruler is yielding and obscure, employing worthy talent can bring blessing to the world. The only concern is inability to do so.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:033 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:034 -->

**He Kai:** The ruler takes the world's lasting abundance as his blessing. Blessing concerns the world, and so brings good fortune. Praise is already included in the mention of blessing.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:034 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:035 -->

### Top Six: Enlarging its house: soaring to Heaven's edge. Peering through its door, stillness and no one there: concealment of its own making.[^j12-abundance-house]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:035 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:036 -->

**Original Meaning.** Concealment means screening and obstructing.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:036 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:037 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The six occupies abundance's extreme, standing high and exalting itself as though soaring at Heaven's edge: height and greatness taken to excess. Peering through its door and finding no one means that, though at abundance's height, it actually occupies a place without office. Because it is dark and makes itself grand, people all abandon it, hiding and avoiding it rather than drawing near.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:037 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:038 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:038 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:039 -->

**Shi Jie:** First conspicuous and grand, then hidden away: in both the sage warns against excessive flourishing. Ziyun says, “What blazes is extinguished; what thunders is cut off. Observe thunder and fire in their fullness and substance: Heaven withdraws the sound, Earth conceals the heat. At the houses of the high and brilliant, spirits peer within.” This fits the meaning precisely.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:039 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:040 -->

**Master Zhang:** A great house and a screened household mean extreme self-obscuration: greatness exhausted and one's dwelling lost. At the highest limit, it does not communicate below, and it also occupies movement's end. Hence soaring to Heaven's edge.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:040 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4694:041 -->

**Zhu Xi's Classified Conversations:** Enlarging the house and soaring to Heaven's edge resembles the expression “like a pheasant in flight.” The house is so high and great that it reaches Heaven's edge, yet this merely makes the self-obstruction more extensive.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4694:041 -->

<a id="eee-4695"></a>

## 56. Lü 旅 — The Wanderer

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4695 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4695:001 -->

### Great Image: Fire upon the mountain: The Wanderer. Thus the noble person is clear and cautious in applying punishments, without prolonging imprisonment.[^j12-wanderer-justice]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4695:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4695:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** Caution in punishment resembles the mountain; not lingering resembles fire.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4695:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4695:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Fire high above illuminates everything. Observing its illumination, the noble person is clear and cautious in punishment. Clarity must not be relied upon presumptuously; hence the warning to be cautious. Clarity joined to stopping also images caution. Observing fire moving without remaining, he does not prolong imprisonment. Prisons are established only from necessity. People enter them because of offenses; how could they be detained there indefinitely?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4695:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4695:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4695:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4695:005 -->

**Kong Yingda:** Fire upon the mountain follows the grass as it moves, with no tendency to remain long: the image of The Wanderer. The two trigrams are Gen's stopping and Li's clarity. The noble person models them in clear examination and careful punishment, without delaying cases and imprisonment.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4695:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4695:006 -->

**Xiang Anshi:** The mountain is not a place where fire remains. A wildfire spreads along its course and merely passes through. Thus the hexagram is called The Wanderer, and its Image teaches against prolonging imprisonment.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4695:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4695:007 -->

**Zhao Rumei:** Fire used for heating dwells in a stove; for smelting, in a furnace. Upon a mountain it is a passing wildfire, like a temporary sojourn: hence The Wanderer. Li's openness gives clarity; Gen's stopping gives caution. The noble person embodies these in clear, cautious punishment without prolonged imprisonment. Prison is a place where people sojourn; not prolonging imprisonment means not making them remain there long. Clarity is prized in punishment, but the clear-sighted are not necessarily cautious, and the cautious may prolong imprisonment. Even with clarity and caution, endless delay without decision turns clarity into darkness and caution into harm.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4695:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4695:008 -->

**Zhang Qingzi:** Clarity allows no concealment of the circumstances; caution prevents excessive punishment. Once clarity and caution have been fully exercised, decision follows. The sage takes the image from The Wanderer precisely because he fears prolonged imprisonment.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4695:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4695:009 -->

### First Six: Petty and trifling in the sojourn: the intention is cramped, bringing calamity.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4695:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4695:010 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Cramped and pressed in heart and intention, one increasingly brings calamity upon oneself. When calamity and fault are paired, their meanings differ; standing alone, calamity simply means trouble and harm.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4695:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4695:011 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4695:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4695:012 -->

**Gu Jiajie:** The line statement disapproves of the conduct; the Image despises the intention.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4695:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4695:013 -->

**Yang Qixin:** Exhaustion here is not material distress. It means cramped, mean, and narrow-minded confinement.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4695:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4695:014 -->

### Six in the Second Place: Obtaining a young servant's loyalty: in the end, no reproach.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4695:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4695:015 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** A traveler depends upon servants. Once their steadfast loyalty is obtained, there is no reproach or regret in the end.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4695:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4695:016 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4695:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4695:017 -->

**Wang Bi:** Once the servant is obtained, lodging and resources are also preserved without loss. Hence no reproach in the end.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4695:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4695:018 -->

### Nine in the Third Place: The traveler's lodging burns: already an injury. Treating those below as strangers: by rightness, loss follows.[^j12-wanderer-servants]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4695:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4695:019 -->

**Original Meaning.** In a time of sojourning, treating those below in this way must, in principle, lead to loss.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4695:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4695:020 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The traveler loses his lodging by fire and is already distressed and injured. Treating those below in this way while sojourning must lead to loss. Exalting oneself through excessive firmness in one's treatment of subordinates loses their steadfast loyalty: their hearts are lost. For a traveler to lose his servants' hearts is dangerous.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4695:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4695:021 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4695:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4695:022 -->

**Guo Yong:** The third nine is firm without the mean and so cannot be secure. A traveler losing his secure place is already cause for sorrow. With harsh and violent capacities, he treats his servants as temporary strangers and naturally loses people's hearts. Is the relation of passing strangers a proper Way of treating others? The noble person simply makes his own conduct generous and never treats those below as strangers.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4695:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4695:023 -->

**Wang Zongchuan:** Having already suffered the burning of his lodging, he further loses his servants. This is the fault of violent severity. A traveler has few intimates; those with whom he lives morning and evening are only his servants. How could he regard them as strangers? If the third nine treats those below as passing strangers, the servants too will treat their superior as a stranger. How could they remain long? Hence loss follows in principle.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4695:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4695:024 -->

**Huang Chunyao:** Those below are the servants. Treating them as travelers means regarding them as strangers. The burning of the lodging already removes the person's shelter, leaving injury and insecurity. How much worse to lose the servants as well! Yet this is not the servants' lack of goodness. In a sojourn, treating subordinates harshly and without kindness, just like passing strangers, naturally fails to win their hearts and efforts. Loss follows in principle. Whom could one blame?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4695:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4695:025 -->

### Nine in the Fourth Place: The traveler finds a stopping-place: not yet attaining the position. Obtaining resources and an axe: the heart is not yet content.[^j12-wanderer-position]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4695:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4695:026 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The fourth ordinarily has an appropriate position through nearness to the ruler. In The Wanderer, however, the fifth is not interpreted as a ruler; thus the fourth has not attained its position. Someone asks: “Does this mean that a nine in the fourth, being incorrect, incurs blame?” Answer: “Firmness in a yielding place is appropriate to the traveler.” With firm and clear ability, the nine wishes to obtain the time and carry out its intention. Thus, though resources and an axe are good for a traveler, its heart is not yet satisfied.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4695:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4695:027 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4695:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4695:028 -->

**Huang Chunyao:** Resources and an axe are means of guarding against trouble. Obtaining them gives no more than self-protection, and so the heart is not yet content.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4695:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4695:029 -->

### Six in the Fifth Place: In the end, praise and appointment: reaching upward.[^j12-wanderer-recognition]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4695:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4695:030 -->

**Original Meaning.** Reaching upward means that its praise and appointment become known above.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4695:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4695:031 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** With cultivated clarity and yielding compliance, above and below associate with it. Reaching means joining. Able to receive those above compliantly, it gains their association: they reach down to it. Standing above and winning those below, it is reached from below. In a sojourn, association with both above and below brings praise and appointment. The Wanderer is a time of distress before a secure place is found. Praise and appointment in the end mean that these will eventually be attained. Once they are attained, one is no longer a wanderer. Distress and few close relationships make the wanderer; one need not literally be abroad.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4695:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4695:032 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4695:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4695:033 -->

**Hu Yuan:** The fifth six is what is described as yieldingness attaining the center outside and complying with firmness. Its yielding, compliant, centered, and correct virtue is trusted by the top nine, and an appointment to honor and distinction reaches it.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4695:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4695:034 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** The fifth six has position and the top nine does not. There is no need to make the fifth's honor depend on the top nine. Occupying high position is itself reaching upward. Though this line is not treated as the ruler's place, it chiefly concerns officers and scholars carrying presentation gifts and obtaining standing and office. Thus reaching upward means that its station and reputation are already high.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4695:034 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4695:035 -->

### Top Nine: A traveler above: by rightness, burning follows. Losing the ox through heedlessness: in the end, it does not hear.[^j12-wanderer-hearing]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4695:035 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4695:036 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** A traveler who occupies the top and treats himself as exalted cannot preserve his dwelling. The situation must lead to the nest's burning. Taking extreme firmness and self-exaltation for fulfillment, he laughs, unaware that restless heedlessness has cost him compliant virtue. In the end he does not hear: he never becomes aware of it himself. Had he become aware, he would not reach the extreme and then wail. Yang firmness without the mean at the utmost already images arrogant height and restless movement; fire flaming upward makes it still worse.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4695:036 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4695:037 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4695:037 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4695:038 -->

**Master Zhang:** Yang at the uppermost limit is the traveler grown arrogant and unrestrained. Losing yielding compliance's correctness is called losing the ox through heedlessness. Angry and offending others, he may face danger and misfortune, but who will tell him? Hence, in the end, he does not hear.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4695:038 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4695:039 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Guo, Wang, and Huang explain the third nine's treating those below as travelers well. Yet the corresponding meaning of being a traveler above has not been explained. Occupying the superior's place in the manner of a traveler means regarding one's position as merely a temporary lodging. Its lack of reverence and caution is evident. Hence burning follows in principle.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4695:039 -->

<a id="eee-4696"></a>

## 57. Xun 巽 — Penetration

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4696 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4696:001 -->

### Great Image: Wind following wind: Penetration. Thus the noble person reiterates commands and carries affairs into action.[^j12-penetration-command]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4696:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4696:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** Following means succession.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4696:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4696:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Two winds repeated are wind following wind. Following means succeeding one another. The noble person observes repeated Xun's succession and compliance, and reiterates commands while carrying out public affairs. Following and repetition both mean compliance above and below. The superior issues commands in accordance with those below; those below comply with the superior and follow. Compliance on both sides is repeated Xun. Commands and public affairs that follow principle accord with the people's hearts, and the people follow compliantly.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4696:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4696:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4696:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4696:005 -->

**Xun Shuang:** Xun represents commands. Two Xun follow one another, hence reiteration. Laws and teachings have a hundred aspects, but carrying commands into effect is foremost. Thus carrying affairs into action.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4696:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4696:006 -->

**Hu Yuan:** Above and below in Xun are both Xun, like wind entering things, reaching everywhere and finding passage everywhere. Hence wind following wind is Penetration. The noble person models it in reiterating commands and putting affairs into practice throughout the world, reaching everywhere and encountering compliance everywhere.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4696:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4696:007 -->

**Guo Yong:** The noble person's virtue is wind. When he possesses wind's virtue and none below fails to follow, the meaning of repeated Xun is complete. In the Changes, Xun governs instruction and commands, like the Odes' use of wind. Thus Viewing speaks of inspecting regions, observing the people, and establishing instruction; Coming to Meet speaks of issuing commands to the four quarters. Both chiefly concern Xun.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4696:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4696:008 -->

**Qiu Fuguo:** Reiterating commands conveys admonition before affairs are undertaken. Carrying affairs into action fulfills those words after commands have been reiterated.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4696:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4696:009 -->

**Yu Yan:** First explain and admonish, then repeat the explanation earnestly, so that people hear and trust. Only afterward make it visible in action. The people then follow as swiftly as the wind. Broadly, commands must be issued with the intention that they be carried out. Without execution, they are empty formalities.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4696:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4696:010 -->

### First Six: Advancing and retreating: the intention is doubtful. The warrior's steadfastness is beneficial: the intention is brought to order.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4696:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4696:011 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Advancing and retreating without knowing where to settle arise from doubt and apprehension in the intention. Employing the warrior's firm steadfastness to establish one's resolve brings that intention to order. Order means cultivating and establishing it.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4696:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4696:012 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4696:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4696:013 -->

**Zhao Rumei:** Order contrasts with doubt. An intention doubtful and undecided cannot settle on advance or retreat. An intention ordered and undisturbed is decisive in action.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4696:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4696:014 -->

**Huang Chunyao:** Seeing both courses as possible without deciding is doubt; being settled and undisturbed is order.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4696:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4696:015 -->

### Nine in the Second Place: The good fortune of manifold efforts: attaining the mean.[^j12-penetration-many]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4696:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4696:016 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The second occupies yieldingness below, imaging excessive deference. Yet it can employ many manifold means of communicating its sincere intention because it attains the mean. Yang occupying the center images substance within. When the center is sincere and substantial, people naturally trust that sincerity. It is neither flattery nor fearful submission. Hence good fortune without blame.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4696:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4696:017 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4696:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4696:018 -->

**Guo Yong:** The second has the virtue of centered firmness and can practice the Way of Penetration. This is why it is without blame.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4696:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4696:019 -->

**He Kai:** Reiterating commands and carrying affairs into action involve manifold efforts that attain the mean.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4696:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4696:020 -->

### Nine in the Third Place: The humiliation of repeated deference: the intention is exhausted.[^j12-penetration-repetition]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4696:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4696:021 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The third's capacities and constitution are not originally capable of deference, yet what stands above requires deference from it. Receiving repeated firmness above while treading upon firmness below, its circumstances prevent the intention from being carried out. Thus it repeatedly fails and repeatedly defers. Its intention is distressed and exhausted: humiliation indeed.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4696:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4696:022 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4696:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4696:023 -->

**Su Jun:** The third nine's repeated deference is not forced practice that fails, but habitual practice carried to excess. Repeating deference leaves insufficient spirit for vigorous effort. The intention too is exhausted, with nowhere to recover itself.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4696:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4696:024 -->

**Zhang Zhenyuan:** Doubt in the intention can be remedied by bringing it to order. Once the intention is exhausted, only humiliation remains.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4696:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4696:025 -->

### Six in the Fourth Place: The hunt yields three kinds: accomplishment.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4696:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4696:026 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Deference toward above and below resembles the hunt yielding three kinds and providing for both above and below. It completes the work of Penetration.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4696:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4696:027 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4696:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4696:028 -->

**Kong Yingda:** Accomplishment means that the hunt obtains a catch, comparing this with accomplishment in carrying out commands.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4696:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4696:029 -->

### Nine in the Fifth Place: Its good fortune: the position is properly centered.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4696:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4696:030 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The fifth nine's good fortune comes from being properly centered. Attaining this Way brings good fortune and makes regret disappear. Proper centeredness means neither excess nor deficiency, but precisely attaining the mean. In handling yielding deference and issuing commands, only the mean is good. Losing it brings regret.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4696:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4696:031 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4696:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4696:032 -->

**Qiu Fuguo:** A nine in the fifth occupies centered correctness. Hence steadfastness brings good fortune, and it governs the reiteration of commands.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4696:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4696:033 -->

### Top Nine: Penetration beneath the bed: exhaustion at the top. Losing resources and the axe: misfortune is certain.[^j12-penetration-final]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4696:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4696:034 -->

**Original Meaning.** “Misfortune is certain” means that misfortune must follow.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4696:034 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4696:035 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Penetration beneath the bed means excessive deference. At the hexagram's top, deference reaches exhaustion and extremity. Occupying the top yet deferring so excessively that one loses oneself—can this be correct? It is a Way of misfortune. Deference is originally good conduct, so the text first questions whether this can be correct, then declares it misfortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4696:035 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4696:036 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4696:036 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4696:037 -->

**Yang Qixin:** Penetration beneath the bed occupies deference's extreme. The world's affairs are completed only through decision. Now, losing resources and the axe means losing the means of decision. Without decision, one fails; misfortune can be expected with certainty.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4696:037 -->

<a id="eee-4697"></a>

## 58. Dui 兌 — Joy

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4697 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4697:001 -->

### Great Image: Lakes joined together: Joy. Thus the noble person discusses and practices learning with friends.[^j12-joy-learning]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4697:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4697:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** Two lakes joined together nourish and benefit one another. Discussion and practice among friends have this image.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4697:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4697:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Joined lakes are two lakes attached to one another. They moisten one another and supply mutual benefit. Thus the noble person observes the image and discusses and practices learning with friends. Friends discussing and practicing benefit one another. Earlier scholars say that no pleasure under Heaven surpasses discussion and practice among friends. This is certainly a great pleasure, but the image of mutual benefit must be made clear.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4697:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4697:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4697:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4697:005 -->

**Yu Fan:** Learning gathers understanding, and questioning distinguishes it. Dui's two mouths face one another; hence friends discussing and practicing.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4697:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4697:006 -->

**Kong Yingda:** Those sharing a teacher are companions; those sharing an intention are friends. Friends gathering together to discuss and practice the Way and rightness have no greater abundance of mutual joy than this.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4697:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4697:007 -->

**Master Cheng:** The world's pleasures must not be pursued to the extreme. Only discussion and practice among friends cause no harm even in abundant enjoyment. Dui's lakes have the quality of mutually nourishing one another.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4697:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4697:008 -->

**Su Shi:** This takes the joy that does not drift into excess.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4697:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4697:009 -->

**Zhu Zhen:** Discuss what one knows; practice what one does.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4697:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4697:010 -->

**Cai Yuan:** Discussion images Dui; practice images repeated Dui.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4697:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4697:011 -->

**Yu Yan:** Discussion clarifies what is not yet understood. Much discussion makes principles and rightness clear. Practice makes familiar what is not yet well learned. Long practice matures its embodiment in conduct. Thus friends discussing and practicing benefit one another like two joined lakes. Studying alone without friends leaves one isolated, narrow, and poorly informed. Hence the Analects regards learning left undiscussed as a concern, learning practiced at fitting times as a pleasure, and friends arriving from afar as a joy.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4697:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4697:012 -->

### First Nine: The good fortune of harmonious joy: conduct is not yet doubtful.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4697:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4697:013 -->

**Original Meaning.** At the hexagram's beginning, its joy is correct and nothing doubtful has yet arisen.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4697:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4697:014 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Seeking something through harmony entangles one in crooked flattery. The first follows the time and handles matters compliantly, with nothing attaching its heart and no ulterior undertaking. There is harmony alone, and so good fortune. Yet the Image, because it occupies Joy below without centeredness and correctness, says that conduct is not yet doubtful. Its conduct has not yet given cause for doubt: no fault has appeared. With centered correctness, this qualification would not be needed. Joy rests upon centered correctness. The line states its meaning directly; the Image unfolds it to the full.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4697:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4697:015 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4697:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4697:016 -->

**Cai Yuan:** The first is not yet drawn toward yin, so its conduct has no doubt or perplexity. The fourth, adjoining the third, has the doubts of deliberating over joy.[^j12-joy-readings]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4697:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4697:017 -->

**Xu Ji:** Doubt means attachment to yin. Of the four yang, only the first has no attachment to yin, hence no doubt yet.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4697:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4697:018 -->

**Zheng Weiyue:** Firm yang at Joy's beginning does not adjoin yin. Thus it proceeds in trust in its heart and in principle. Its outward conduct is not in conflict with the heart. With an attached correspondent, it could no longer decide for itself.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4697:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4697:019 -->

### Nine in the Second Place: The good fortune of sincere joy: the intention is trustworthy.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4697:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4697:020 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** What the heart preserves is intention. The second's firm substance occupies the center, so sincerity and trust are preserved within. With an intention grounded in sincerity and trust, how could it please petty people and lose itself? Hence good fortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4697:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4697:021 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4697:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4697:022 -->

**He Kai:** The first is far from the third: not only is its intention trustworthy, its conduct has not entered anything doubtful. The second is close to the third: though its conduct cannot avoid seeming doubtful, its intention can be trusted.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4697:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4697:023 -->

### Six in the Third Place: The misfortune of coming to please: the position is not appropriate.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4697:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4697:024 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Placing oneself without centeredness or correctness, lacking an associate yet recklessly seeking pleasure, brings misfortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4697:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4697:025 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4697:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4697:026 -->

**Xiong Liangfu:** The third six's position is inappropriate. Between the two Dui, the lower joy is ending and the upper joy arrives: joy upon joy, without correctness. The top six's drawing others into joy is its counterpart.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4697:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4697:027 -->

### Nine in the Fourth Place: Its joy: there is blessing.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4697:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4697:028 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Joy means that if it preserves correctness and the ruler is pleased with it, it may carry out its firm yang Way and extend blessing to others.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4697:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4697:029 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4697:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4697:030 -->

**Guo Yong:** In Joy's time, at the meeting of above and below, it does not recklessly follow pleasure but knows what to choose. Firmly preserving itself, it can complete joy's gladness. This joy is not confined to one person; in the end, blessing reaches others.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4697:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4697:031 -->

### Nine in the Fifth Place: Trusting what strips away: the position is rightly held.[^j12-joy-position]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4697:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4697:032 -->

**Original Meaning.** This is the same as Treading's fifth nine.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4697:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4697:033 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The warning against trusting what strips away is given because the position the fifth occupies rightly calls for warning. Closely adjoining yielding yin, there is a Way of mutual pleasure. Hence the warning concerns trusting it.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4697:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4697:034 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4697:034 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4697:035 -->

**Wang Shenzi:** It means occupying precisely the honored position. If it trusts the yielding persuasions above, yang will certainly be diminished and stripped away.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4697:035 -->

#### Top Six — surviving commentary

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4697:036 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Joy has already reached its utmost, yet one draws it out and prolongs it. Though the heart's desire for pleasure does not cease, the principles of the affair have already been exceeded, and there is in truth nothing left to enjoy. Affairs in their flourishing have radiance; forcing that radiance to continue after its utmost leaves profound emptiness of meaning. How could there be light? *Wei* expresses “not necessarily,” a frequent usage in the Images. It cannot necessarily possess radiance: this means it cannot be radiant.[^j12-joy-missing]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4697:036 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4697:037 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4697:037 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4697:038 -->

**Yang Qixin:** Both coming to please and drawing others into joy concern petty people. The noble person should not receive them when they come, nor go when they draw him. Noble people draw one another through the Way and virtue, and their Way is radiant. Drawing merely toward pleasure leaves the heart's methods obscure and conduct crooked. How could it be radiant?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4697:038 -->

<a id="eee-4698"></a>

## 59. Huan 渙 — Dispersion

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4698 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4698:001 -->

### Great Image: Wind moves over water: Dispersion. Thus the former kings offered sacrifice to the Lord and established ancestral temples.[^j12-dispersion-ritual]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4698:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4698:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** Both serve to gather what has dispersed.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4698:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4698:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Wind moving over water images dispersal. Observing it, the former kings sought to remedy the world's dispersion, reaching as far as sacrifice to the Lord and the establishment of temples. Nothing gathers human hearts like ancestral temples. The gratitude of sacrifice comes from the heart; thus sacrifice to the Lord and establishing temples are what people's hearts turn toward. No Way of binding hearts and gathering what is scattered is greater.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4698:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4698:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4698:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4698:005 -->

**Master Cheng:** Gathering and Dispersion both concern sacrifice to the Lord and establishing temples. The gathering of the spirit takes visible form here. Because it disperses, these are established to collect it again.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4698:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4698:006 -->

**Lü Dalin:** Wind over water must raise waves. In a time of shaking, disturbance, dispersal, and unrest, the ruler seeks to reunite what is scattered. Nothing is better than returning to the root. Sacrifice to the Lord and establishing temples make clear the roots of Heaven and humanity.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4698:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4698:007 -->

### First Six: Its good fortune: compliance.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4698:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4698:008 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The first has good fortune because it compliantly follows the person of centered firmness. Employing rescue when dispersion has only begun also follows the time.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4698:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4698:009 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4698:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4698:010 -->

**Guo Yong:** The first six is difficulty's beginning. Rescuing at that beginning never fails to accomplish the crossing. Recognize the world's affairs early, and they follow easily into order. Thus the Commentary says, “The first six's good fortune: compliance.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4698:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4698:011 -->

### Nine in the Second Place: Amid dispersion, hastening to its support: obtaining the wish.[^j12-dispersion-self]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4698:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4698:012 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** In a time of dispersion, union provides security. The second, within danger, hastens to the first in search of security. Relying on it as on a support makes regret vanish and obtains the wish.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4698:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4698:013 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4698:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4698:014 -->

**Wang Zongchuan:** In Dispersion's time, yang firmness comes to occupy the second, a position of quiet security. Hence the image of hastening to its support. Only through quiet security can one unite all the world's movements. The fifth establishes the king's dwelling above; the second hastens to its support below. Each finds a secure place, and this is how the world's dispersion can be gathered.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4698:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4698:015 -->

### Six in the Third Place: Dispersing one's person: the intention is directed outward.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4698:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4698:016 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Its intention responds to the top, and so lies outside. Responding to the top allows its person to escape dispersion without regret. When regret vanishes, it originally existed and is removed; with no regret, it was originally absent.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4698:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4698:017 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4698:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4698:018 -->

**Huang Chunyao:** Outside means the world. Only through dispersing one's own person can one remedy the world's dispersion; only because one's intention lies with the world's dispersion can there be this dispersal of one's person.[^j12-dispersion-readings]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4698:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4698:019 -->

### Six in the Fourth Place: Dispersing its group; supreme good fortune: radiant greatness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4698:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4698:020 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Supreme good fortune means that the achievement and virtue are radiant and great. Why are supreme good fortune and radiant greatness assigned to the fourth rather than the fifth? The two lines are discussed together. The fourth explains implementation, the fifth completed achievement: the distinction between minister and ruler.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4698:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4698:021 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4698:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4698:022 -->

**Lai Zhide:** Those who establish private factions all have darkened and narrow hearts. With not a hair's breadth of private partiality, one is radiant, upright, and great, and can naturally disperse one's group. Hence radiant greatness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4698:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4698:023 -->

### Nine in the Fifth Place: The king's dwelling; no blame: the correct position.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4698:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4698:024 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The king's dwelling means the correct position, the ruler's honored station. Acting as the fifth does makes occupation of that honored station fitting and free of blame.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4698:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4698:025 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4698:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4698:026 -->

**Xiong Liangfu:** When the world is dispersed, the ruler must issue commands and occupy the correct position above, so that people's hearts know where to turn and the world becomes one. Hence “the king's dwelling; no blame,” and the Image says “the correct position.” This is the same meaning as Gathering's possessing the position. The Original Meaning explains “dispersing the king's dwelling” as dispersing his accumulated stores. Yet in a time of dispersion, someone must govern the work of dispersal and reunification. One should follow the Small Image's explanation through the correct position.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4698:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4698:027 -->

### Top Nine: Dispersing its blood: keeping harm distant.[^j12-dispersion-blood]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4698:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4698:028 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** If the Image simply read “dispersing its blood,” its construction would be the same as “holding back its rich moisture” in Zhun. Yet that is not the intended meaning. A word meaning “depart” is missing after “blood.” “Blood departs; apprehension leaves” means that keeping harm distant brings no blame.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4698:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4698:029 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4698:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4698:030 -->

**Xiang Anshi:** In the top nine's line statement, “blood” and “emerge” rhyme. Both belong to three-character clauses; “blood” is not joined to the following “depart.” Small Restraint's “blood departs; apprehension leaves” is different. Here blood has already been dispersed and need not depart again. Moreover, the wording and meaning of *ti*, apprehension, and *ti*, distance, differ. The Small Image says “keeping harm distant,” making the meaning of distance entirely clear. It cannot be replaced by apprehension. Of all the lines, only the top nine is farthest from danger; hence its wording.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4698:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4698:031 -->

**Further explanation:** Disperse sweat to remove stagnation and blockage; disperse blood to keep injury distant.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4698:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4698:032 -->

**Chen Youwen:** Kan is the blood trigram. *Ti* means distant. The Small Image's keeping harm distant explains that word through distance. Although the top corresponds to the third, it stands far above Dispersion. Thus it disperses its blood, leaves that connection behind, and goes far away. Leaving the harm of Kan's danger brings no blame.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4698:032 -->

<a id="eee-4699"></a>

## 60. Jie 節 — Limitation

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4699 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4699:001 -->

### Great Image: Water above the lake: Limitation. Thus the noble person establishes measures and standards, and deliberates upon virtue and conduct.[^j12-limitation-measures]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4699:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4699:002 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** A lake's capacity for water is limited. Beyond that capacity, it fills and overflows. This is a limit, and so the hexagram is Limitation. Observing its image, the noble person establishes measures and standards. The size, weight, height, and degree of ornament or substance of all things have their measures and standards: these impose limits. Measures concern quantity; standards concern prescribed rules. Deliberating upon virtue and conduct means that what is preserved within is virtue and what appears outside is conduct. One's virtue and conduct should be examined so that they meet the right measure. Deliberation means assessing and weighing in order to find that measure.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4699:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4699:003 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4699:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4699:004 -->

**Hou Xingguo:** Water above the lake is limited by embankments.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4699:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4699:005 -->

**Zhang Jun:** Establishing measures and standards takes account of others; deliberating upon virtue and conduct begins with oneself. The Rites says, “The noble person's deliberation on the Way begins with himself; his establishment of laws takes account of the people.” If what one cannot practice oneself, or what cannot rightly be practiced, is forced upon others, who will follow? One word exhausts the Way of Limitation: the mean. The mean must begin with oneself.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4699:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4699:006 -->

**Zhu Zhen:** The lake's capacity for water is certainly limited. Empty, it admits water; full, it releases it. Water takes the lake as its measure.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4699:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4699:007 -->

**Guo Yong:** A lake without water is deficient; water above the lake is surplus. Deficiency brings Oppression; surplus calls for Limitation. This is the constant principle. In human limitation, establishing measures and standards regulates what is outside, while deliberating upon virtue and conduct regulates what is within. In the state, the household, and one's own person, the regulation of inner and outer is the same.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4699:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4699:008 -->

**Supplement — Kong Yingda:** Measures and standards concern the relative extent of ritual distinctions and appointments among high and low. Virtue and conduct concern the varying capacities that make people fit for responsibilities. The noble person models Limitation in setting the gradations of ritual measure, ensuring that all have bounds, and in assessing people's virtue and conduct for appointment, ensuring that every employment is fitting.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4699:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4699:009 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** The other scholars take virtue and conduct as one's own. Kong alone takes them as those of other people. This fits the word “deliberate” especially closely and also captures the care required in guarding ranks and stipends and treating titles and insignia with caution.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4699:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4699:010 -->

### First Nine: Not leaving the inner courtyard: knowing passage and obstruction.[^j12-limitation-courtyards]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4699:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4699:011 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** At Limitation's beginning, the line warns one to preserve oneself carefully; hence not leaving the inner courtyard brings no blame. The Image fears that people will cling to the words, and so further explains that even while carefully remaining within the courtyard, one must know whether the time offers passage or obstruction. With passage, act; with obstruction, stop. When rightness requires going out, go out. Weisheng's fidelity kept him from leaving when the water rose: he did not know passage and obstruction. Thus the noble person is steadfast without being rigidly literal in trust. The Appended Statements explains this solely through speech because what a person limits is speech and conduct. Limitation in speech implies conduct as well, and speech comes first.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4699:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4699:012 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4699:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4699:013 -->

**Wang Shenzi:** The time has passage and obstruction. With passage, act; with obstruction, stop. One who stops when stopping is required is the noble person who knows both. The Appended Statements explains this specifically through discreet speech because the line belongs to Dui.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4699:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4699:014 -->

**Wu Yueshen:** Limitation includes both passage and obstruction, just as Keeping Still includes both movement and stopping. The first nine's remaining in the inner courtyard knows obstruction, yet passage is also mentioned to show that it is not exclusively stopping. The second misses the time to an extreme because it knows only obstruction, not passage.
