<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4699:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4699:015 -->

### Nine in the Second Place: Not leaving the outer courtyard brings misfortune: missing the time to the extreme.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4699:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4699:016 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Unable to follow the fifth nine's firm, centered, and correct Way and complete Limitation's work, it instead remains attached to yielding yin in a private intimacy. This is missing the time to the extreme, and so misfortune. Missing the time means missing what is fitting.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4699:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4699:017 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4699:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4699:018 -->

**Su Shi:** When water first arrives, the lake should hold it back, not let it pass. Once it has arrived fully, it should let it pass, not hold it back. Hence the first nine's remaining in the inner courtyard brings no blame, because this is the time for stopping. The second nine's remaining in the outer courtyard brings misfortune, because this is the time for passage. Failing to allow passage now misses the time to the extreme.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4699:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4699:019 -->

**Guo Yong:** The first is a place where action should not yet be undertaken. The second, however, occupies a place for action with centered firmness. Their Ways cannot be the same. Thus the first's remaining within knows obstruction, while the second's remaining within fails to know passage. Knowing obstruction brings no blame; failing to know passage brings the misfortune of missing the time.[^j12-limitation-readings]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4699:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4699:020 -->

### Six in the Third Place: Sighing over lack of limitation: whom else could one blame?[^j12-limitation-blame]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4699:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4699:021 -->

**Original Meaning.** This “no blame” differs from that in the other lines. It means having no one to whom the blame can be assigned.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4699:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4699:022 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Limitation could avert error, but one fails to limit oneself and comes to a cause for sighing. Whom could one blame?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4699:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4699:023 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4699:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4699:024 -->

**Shen Yiguan:** Wang Jiefu and Cheng Shasui say that being able to sigh in remorse and govern oneself also brings no blame. This sighing is like “sorrowful sighs,” and “whom else could one blame?” is like the Image of going out of the gate to join others.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4699:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4699:025 -->

**He Kai:** The line statements of the hexagrams say “no blame” ninety-nine times, mostly as words concerning correction of faults. Release's third-line Commentary also says “whom else could one blame?” Yet although its wording is the same, that line statement itself has no words “no blame.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4699:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4699:026 -->

### Six in the Fourth Place: The success of peaceful limitation: receiving the Way above.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4699:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4699:027 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** More than one feature allows the fourth to remain peacefully within limits. The Image singles out the weightiest: receiving the fifth nine's firm, centered, and correct Way as its measure is sufficient for success. Its other good qualities do not depart from centered correctness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4699:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4699:028 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4699:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4699:029 -->

**Qian Yiben:** Passage through centered correctness lies in the fifth. The fourth receives it at close hand, taking not mere stopping as accomplishment but free passage as the Way. Hence receiving the Way above.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4699:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4699:030 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Limitation says success because the fifth nine provides passage through centered correctness. Why, then, is success stated at the fourth? The fifth is the water's source, the fourth its flow. Water finds passage in flowing: the fourth receives the source above and distributes it.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4699:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4699:031 -->

### Nine in the Fifth Place: The good fortune of sweet limitation: occupying the center.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4699:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4699:032 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** It occupies the honored position and also attains the middle Way, hence good fortune and accomplishment. Limitation prizes the mean. Attaining the mean brings correctness, but correctness does not fully encompass the mean.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4699:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4699:033 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4699:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4699:034 -->

**Yu Yan:** Limitation prizes the mean. Failing to limit when limitation is required brings the third six's sighs over lack of limitation. Excessive limitation brings the top six's misfortune of bitter limitation. Only the fifth nine's sweet limitation has good fortune, because it occupies the center and limits appropriately, without excess or deficiency.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4699:034 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4699:035 -->

### Top Six: Bitter limitation; persistence brings misfortune: its Way is exhausted.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4699:035 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4699:036 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Once limitation is bitter, holding to it steadfastly brings misfortune. The Way of Limitation has reached exhaustion and extremity.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4699:036 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4699:037 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4699:037 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4699:038 -->

**Wu Yueshen:** The line says that persistence in bitter limitation brings misfortune; the Judgment says that bitter limitation must not be persisted in. Precisely because persistence brings misfortune, it must not be maintained. Thus both Judgment and Image Commentaries explain it as the exhaustion of its Way.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4699:038 -->

<a id="eee-4700"></a>

## 61. Zhongfu 中孚 — Inner Sincerity

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4700 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4700:001 -->

### Great Image: Wind above the lake: Inner Sincerity. Thus the noble person reviews cases and stays executions.[^j12-sincerity-justice]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4700:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4700:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** Wind influences and water receives: the image of Inner Sincerity. Reviewing cases and staying executions express its intention.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4700:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4700:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Wind above the lake reaches and stirs what is within it. Water's body is open, so wind can enter. The human heart is open, so things can influence it. Wind stirring the lake resembles things stirring what is within; hence Inner Sincerity. The noble person observes this image to review cases and stay executions. In reviewing cases, he exhausts his conscientiousness; in deciding death sentences, he carries compassion to its utmost. Thus his sincere intention constantly seeks leniency. Staying means dealing generously. There is no affair in which he fails to exercise complete conscientiousness, but reviewing cases and staying executions are among the greatest.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4700:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4700:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4700:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4700:005 -->

**Yang Wanli:** Wind has no form yet stirs what lies hidden; sincerity has no visible image yet moves people and things. No influence of Inner Sincerity is greater than loving life rather than taking it. Reviewing cases seeks grounds for release within a case that would condemn; staying execution seeks grounds for life within a sentence of death.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4700:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4700:006 -->

**Xiang Anshi:** Before a case is decided, review it. Even after decision, delay its execution. Only then is everything required by the human heart fulfilled. The king hears it, the minister of punishments hears it, and the three chief ministers hear it: this is reviewing cases. After ten days the responsible officers hear it, after twenty days they hear it again, and after three months it is submitted above: this is staying execution. Thus the case is completed with trust and submitted with trust. Having exhausted what belongs to oneself, one leaves no grievance in others.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4700:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4700:007 -->

**Xu Ji:** Five Great Images discuss penal affairs. Biting Through and Abundance possess Li's clarity and Zhen's authority. Adornment follows Biting Through, and The Wanderer follows Abundance: Li's clarity remains, while Zhen in each turns into Gen. Clarity is prized at all times, but authority sometimes must stop. Inner Sincerity as a whole resembles Li, and its interlocking trigrams include Zhen and Gen; moreover, Dui supplies deliberation and Xun delay. The sage draws teaching from the images. His generous conscientiousness and compassion appear in this care over punishment.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4700:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4700:008 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** When wind enters things, it does not only bend plants on level ground or sound through rocky valleys and hollows. Even beneath accumulated water and layers of yin, frozen ice loosens and dissolves through it. Thus it images utmost sincerity reaching everywhere. Cases among the people resemble layers of yin in the earth. Only when the ruler's examination of the world's hidden circumstances reaches the reviewing of cases and staying of execution does his utmost sincerity leave nothing unpenetrated.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4700:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4700:009 -->

### First Nine: Forethought brings good fortune: the intention has not changed.[^j12-sincerity-forethought]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4700:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4700:010 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** At trust's beginning, before the intention has settled upon anything, one considers what deserves trust and can find correctness. Hence good fortune. The intention has not yet moved or changed. Once it follows something, it has changed, and deliberation may fail to attain correctness. At the beginning, the text explains how to seek what should be trusted.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4700:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4700:011 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** The intention not changing means that the substantial heart is not lost. Once the intention changes, there is something else.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4700:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4700:012 -->

### Nine in the Second Place: Its young answer it: the heart's inward wish.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4700:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4700:013 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The heart's inward wish means what sincere intention desires. Thus it communicates and responds.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4700:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4700:014 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4700:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4700:015 -->

**Zhu Zhen:** This is what Xunzi calls those sharing the same character joining, and those of the same kind responding.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4700:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4700:016 -->

**Cheng Jingcheng:** The crane's call issues from within. Its young's answering likewise responds from the heart. Hence the heart's inward wish. A wish issuing from within is sincerity at its utmost.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4700:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4700:017 -->

### Six in the Third Place: Now beating the drum, now ceasing: the position is not appropriate.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4700:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4700:018 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Occupying an inappropriate position, it has no governing principle and simply follows whatever it trusts. If its situation were correct, what it trusted would have a proper direction.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4700:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4700:019 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4700:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4700:020 -->

**Yu Yan:** The third six occupies an inappropriate position and has no master in the heart. Hence it alternately beats the drum and stops, without stability. The first nine is not like this.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4700:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4700:021 -->

### Six in the Fourth Place: The paired horse is lost: severing its kind to go upward.[^j12-sincerity-horse]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4700:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4700:022 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** It severs its own kind and follows the fifth above. Its kind means its correspondent.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4700:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4700:023 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4700:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4700:024 -->

**Hu Bingwen:** Kun finds blessing in losing companions; Inner Sincerity's fourth finds no blame in severing its kind.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4700:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4700:025 -->

**Zhao of Yuquan:** The fourth possesses yielding correctness. Thus it can sever the party and kind of the first and place its trust in the fifth above. This is losing the paired horse.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4700:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4700:026 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** The third and fourth are both what the hexagram calls openness within. They equally occupy the interior to form that image, and both have correspondents as partners or counterparts. Yet the third's heart is bound to its counterpart while the fourth's intention severs its partner, because the third is incorrect and the fourth correct. Moreover, the fourth six's receiving the fifth nine is often fortunate, while the third six's responding to the top nine is often unfortunate. Such are the Changes' conventions.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4700:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4700:027 -->

### Nine in the Fifth Place: Sincerity binds firmly: the position is rightly held.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4700:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4700:028 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The fifth occupies the ruler's honored position. Through the Way of centered correctness, it can make the world's trust firm as a binding. Only then is it equal to its position. The ruler's Way should be like this.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4700:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4700:029 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4700:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4700:030 -->

**Kong Yingda:** Precisely because it occupies the honored position, it is warned to bind others through trust. Only then is it without blame.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4700:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4700:031 -->

### Top Nine: A winged cry rises to Heaven: how could it endure?[^j12-sincerity-winged]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4700:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4700:032 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Preserving trust to exhaustion without knowing how to change—how could this endure? Holding rigidly without free passage brings misfortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4700:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4700:033 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4700:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4700:034 -->

**Kong Yingda:** Empty sound without substance: how could it last?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4700:034 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4700:035 -->

**Hou Xingguo:** At the exhausted top, the position is lost and trust does not come from within. Sound without substance, empty brilliance displayed outwardly: this is the winged cry rising to Heaven. How could such an empty cry endure?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4700:035 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4700:036 -->

**Hu Yuan:** The top nine merely decorates itself outwardly with empty reputation, without conduct of pure sincerity and substantial earnestness. Continuing in this way makes misfortune grow with time. Thus the sage warns, “How could it endure?” He desires people to correct faults and return to sincerity, taking truthful substance as the root.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4700:036 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4700:037 -->

**Xiang Anshi:** The top nine is at Xun's utmost and restless, neither correct nor centered. Insufficient within, it seeks trust outside. When reputation exceeds actuality, its drying up may be awaited at any moment. The longer it continues, the worse the misfortune. How could it endure?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4700:037 -->

<a id="eee-4701"></a>

## 62. Xiaoguo 小過 — Small Excess

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4701 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4701:001 -->

### Great Image: Thunder upon the mountain: Small Excess. Thus the noble person exceeds in reverence in conduct, in grief in mourning, and in frugality in expenditure.[^j12-small-excess-image]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4701:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4701:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** Thunder upon the mountain has a sound exceeding the ordinary in a small degree. These three excesses are all excesses in small matters. One may exceed in the small, not the great; one may exceed a little, not excessively. These are the Judgment's small undertakings and preference for what is low.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4701:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4701:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Thunder shaking upon the mountain has a sound beyond the ordinary, hence Small Excess. The world's affairs sometimes call for going beyond the usual measure, but not too far. Thus Small Excess. The noble person observes its image and makes an effort in those matters that should exceed: reverence in conduct, grief in mourning, and frugality in expenditure. Exceeding when excess is called for is precisely what is fitting. Exceeding when it is not called for is error.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4701:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4701:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4701:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4701:005 -->

**Kong Yingda:** Petty people's errors incline toward carelessness and extravagance. Thus the noble person corrects them through excess of reverence in conduct, grief in mourning, and frugality in expenditure.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4701:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4701:006 -->

**Master Zhang:** Exceeding in reverence, grief, and frugality all belong to preferring what is low.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4701:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4701:007 -->

**Chao Yuezhi:** In an age with a Mo'ao who raised his feet proudly, Zheng Kaofu corrected the example by walking close to the wall. In an age with Master Zai's proposal to shorten mourning, Gao Chai corrected it with tears of blood. In an age with Guan Zhong's Three Returns and ceremonial cupstand, Master Yan corrected it with a worn fur robe. Though not the middle course itself, such conduct could correct the time and reform its customs.[^j12-small-excess-allusions]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4701:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4701:008 -->

**Zhao Yansu:** Reverence, grief, and frugality more often fall short. Only by exceeding can they be brought back to the mean.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4701:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4701:009 -->

**Yang Qixin:** Excess of reverence, grief, and frugality may indeed be conduct surpassing the age and standing apart from convention. Yet the excess lies in gathering inward and placing oneself low. Thus it can be called only Small Excess, not Great Excess.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4701:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4701:010 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** When thunder comes forth from the earth, its sound is just opening and expanding, and is great. By the time it reaches the mountain, the sound gradually draws in and becomes faint. Thus wind and thunder may rage on level ground without being noticed high upon a mountain. This is Small Excess's meaning.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4701:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4701:011 -->

### First Six: The flying bird brings misfortune: nothing can be done.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4701:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4701:012 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Its swift excess is like a bird's rapid flight. How could there be time to rescue or stop it? Misfortune is fitting. Nothing can be done means that no effort can be brought to bear.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4701:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4701:013 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4701:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4701:014 -->

**He Kai:** Bringing misfortune means delivering oneself into it. When the calamity is one's own making, what can be done?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4701:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4701:015 -->

### Six in the Second Place: Not reaching the ruler: the minister must not overstep.[^j12-small-excess-minister]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4701:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4701:016 -->

**Original Meaning.** It does not reach the ruler but returns to meet the minister because the minister must not be passed over.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4701:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4701:017 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** In a time of exceeding, affairs all go beyond their ordinary measure. Thus, concerning advancement, the text warns against reaching the ruler. The minister must not overstep: this is the minister's allotted place.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4701:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4701:018 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4701:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4701:019 -->

**Hu Bingwen:** Small matters may sometimes exceed, but a minister in relation to the ruler must not overstep.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4701:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4701:020 -->

### Nine in the Third Place: Following, someone may strike it down: how great the misfortune!

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4701:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4701:021 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** When yin exceeds, it must harm yang; when the petty person's Way flourishes, it must harm the noble person. Extraordinary precautions are therefore necessary. Inadequate precautions leave one exposed to their attack. Hence “how great the misfortune,” expressing its severity.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4701:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4701:022 -->

### Nine in the Fourth Place: Not exceeding, one meets it: the position is not appropriate. Going brings danger and requires caution: in the end, this cannot endure.[^j12-small-excess-fourth]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4701:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4701:023 -->

**Original Meaning.** The line's meaning is not clear. This too should be left unresolved.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4701:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4701:024 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The position is inappropriate because it occupies yieldingness. In a time of excess, the fourth nine does not exceed through firmness but instead occupies yieldingness. It thereby finds what is fitting, hence “meets it”: meeting the appropriate measure. A nine in the fourth is not appropriately placed; occupying yieldingness, however, is what makes it meet the fitting measure. When yin exceeds, yang can only withdraw and protect itself. How could it ultimately grow and flourish? Thus going brings danger and requires caution. “Grow” is read in the rising tone. Reading it in the level tone would greatly miss the Changes' intention. Comparing Breakthrough and Splitting Apart makes this clear. Its written form is the same as in Breakthrough's Image, but its pronunciation differs.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4701:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4701:025 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4701:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4701:026 -->

**Qian Yiben:** The third and fourth both lose their positions, so the inappropriate position is expressly explained. The third's precautions and the fourth's meeting both favor what is low. The third's following and being struck, and the fourth's going and requiring caution, both disfavor rising.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4701:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4701:027 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** The inappropriate position is what the Judgment calls firmness losing position and failing to attain the mean. Precisely because firmness loses position and the mean, the line is warned to go beyond the ordinary in meeting what is required. Otherwise there is danger. How could it long cling to this without knowing change?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4701:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4701:028 -->

### Six in the Fifth Place: Dense clouds without rain: already above.[^j12-small-excess-clouds]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4701:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4701:029 -->

**Original Meaning.** Already above means too high.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4701:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4701:030 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Yang descends and yin rises; when they join, harmony produces rain. Yin is already above. However dense the clouds, how could rain form? Yin exceeding cannot accomplish great things.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4701:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4701:031 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4701:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4701:032 -->

**Gong Huan:** Small Restraint says of dense clouds without rain that they are still going onward: yin cannot restrain yang, and yang still advances. Small Excess says they are already above: yin exceeds yang and has already risen. One concerns yang's excess, the other yin's. Both image lack of harmony between yin and yang, and therefore no rain can form.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4701:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4701:033 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Gong is right that dense clouds without rain in both hexagrams image discord between yin and yang. Yet Small Restraint's still going onward also means yin energy rising. It is the same as this line's already being above, not a different explanation. The distinction is that Small Restraint's meaning concerns one below: what still rises should accumulate deeply until rain comes of itself. This line concerns one above: what is already high should communicate downward before rain can come.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4701:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4701:034 -->

### Top Six: Not meeting it, one passes beyond: already overreaching.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4701:034 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4701:035 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** At excess's end, it does not meet principle but passes beyond it. Excess has already reached an overreaching extreme, and misfortune is fitting.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4701:035 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4701:036 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4701:036 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4701:037 -->

**Kong Yingda:** This explains why it fails to meet and passes beyond: it already occupies the place of overreaching extremity.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4701:037 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4701:038 -->

**Zhao Rumei:** Already above is not yet the utmost; already overreaching is the utmost.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4701:038 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4701:039 -->

**Yu Yan:** The fifth six says already above, meaning it has already exceeded. The top six goes further still, hence already overreaching.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4701:039 -->

<a id="eee-4702"></a>

## 63. Jiji 既濟 — After Completion

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4702 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4702:001 -->

### Great Image: Water above fire: After Completion. Thus the noble person considers trouble and guards against it in advance.[^j12-completion-precaution]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4702:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4702:002 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Water and fire join, each attaining its use: this is After Completion. In such a time, the concern is the arising of trouble and harm. Thus one considers them and takes precautions in advance, preventing their arrival. Whenever a completed and ordered world has fallen into calamity and disorder, it is because trouble was not considered and guarded against beforehand.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4702:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4702:003 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4702:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4702:004 -->

**Wang Shenzi:** After Completion is not itself a time of trouble, yet trouble often arises afterward. If the noble person considers this and prepares against it, he can preserve the initial good fortune without fearing disorder at the end.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4702:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4702:005 -->

**Gong Huan:** Water above and fire below serve one another. Yet if water bursts through, fire goes out; if fire blazes, water dries up. Within their interaction lies the hidden possibility of mutual harm. Thus the noble person considers trouble and guards against it in advance. Effective defense depends on preparation; preparation depends on thought.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4702:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4702:006 -->

### First Nine: Dragging back the wheels: by rightness, no blame.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4702:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4702:007 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** At completion's beginning, being able to halt advancement prevents reaching the extreme. The meaning is naturally free of blame.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4702:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4702:008 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4702:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4702:009 -->

**Xu Zaihan:** At the beginning of crossing, the first drags back the wheels used to traverse danger. Control remains with oneself, so in principle the crossing need not fail. Thus the tail may be wet without blame. The Image therefore places its emphasis on dragging back the wheels.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4702:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4702:010 -->

### Six in the Second Place: Regaining it in seven days: through the middle Way.[^j12-completion-seven]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4702:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4702:011 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Though the Way of centered correctness is not employed by the time, it cannot remain forever without application. Thus the lost carriage screen should be regained in seven days. This means preserving one's centeredness until it necessarily finds application at another time. Not losing the mean gives correctness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4702:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4702:012 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4702:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4702:013 -->

**He Kai:** The second occupies the lower trigram's center. Through centeredness it influences centeredness and attains its proper correspondent. Thus in the end they must trust one another.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4702:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4702:014 -->

### Nine in the Third Place: Conquering them in three years: exhaustion.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4702:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4702:015 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Exhaustion is mentioned to show the affair's utmost difficulty. With Gaozong's heart, such action is permissible. Without it, greed and anger bring calamity upon the people.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4702:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4702:016 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Exhaustion shows that achievement is not easily attained. It is like attacking and removing illness with potent medicine: the original vital energy is depleted and injured as well. Without a means of rest and nourishment to restore that energy, the treatment becomes the root of another grave illness.[^j12-completion-exhaustion]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4702:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4702:017 -->

### Six in the Fourth Place: Vigilance all day: there is cause for apprehension.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4702:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4702:018 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Watchfulness and apprehension throughout the day continually anticipate approaching trouble. After completion, care should be like this.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4702:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4702:019 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4702:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4702:020 -->

**Li Jian:** Vigilance all day means that the heart preparing against trouble must never be forgotten.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4702:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4702:021 -->

### Nine in the Fifth Place: The eastern neighbor's slaughtered ox does not equal the western neighbor's timely offering. Truly receiving the blessing: great good fortune arrives.[^j12-completion-offering]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4702:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4702:022 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The fifth's ability and virtue are not without goodness, but they do not equal the second's time. The second is below, with a time for further advancement. Thus centered correctness joined to sincerity brings the great arrival of good fortune: this is receiving blessing. Good fortune arriving greatly is great in the circumstances of After Completion. It is the small success and initial good fortune mentioned in the Judgment.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4702:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4702:023 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4702:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4702:024 -->

**Zhu Zhen:** Lavish offerings not equaling scant ones is a matter of timing. The fifth is already at completion, with no further advancement; fullness should give way to emptiness. Hence it does not equal the western neighbor's simple *yue* sacrifice. Nothing reaches the utmost without reversing, and completion has reached its utmost. Preserving it through centered correctness merely keeps reversal from arriving yet.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4702:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4702:025 -->

**Wang Shenzi:** This means that a ruler after completion must handle matters as though they were still incomplete. Only then does he truly receive blessing. Otherwise, even at the height of abundance, the Way of completion declines.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4702:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4702:026 -->

**Zhang Qingzi:** After completion, the chief concern is excessive flourishing. In sacrifice at such a time, lavishness does not equal restraint. Thus the eastern neighbor does not equal the western, and an ox does not equal a simple *yue* sacrifice. Sacrifice at the appropriate time, however modest, is truly sufficient to receive blessing. The great arrival of good fortune is evident.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4702:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4702:027 -->

### Top Six: Wetting the head; danger: how could this endure?[^j12-completion-ending]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4702:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4702:028 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** At completion's utmost, danger reaches the wetting of the head. How could it last long?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4702:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4702:029 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4702:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4702:030 -->

**Hu Yuan:** At After Completion's end, reversal leads to Before Completion and even to the head's submersion. One should therefore awaken decisively and change with apprehension. How could one remain long in this condition?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4702:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4702:031 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Danger is not yet misfortune; it is a perilous condition. Recognize that danger and turn back, and the head need not be submerged. Whenever the Changes says “how could it last?” or “how could it endure?”, from Zhun's top line to this one, it urges apprehension, reform, and awakening rather than bewildered immersion.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4702:031 -->

<a id="eee-4703"></a>

## 64. Weiji 未濟 — Before Completion

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4703 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4703:001 -->

### Great Image: Fire above water: Before Completion. Thus the noble person carefully distinguishes things and places them in their proper quarters.[^j12-before-completion-order]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4703:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4703:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** Water and fire are different things, each occupying its own place. Thus the noble person observes the image and carefully distinguishes them.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4703:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4703:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Water and fire do not interact or assist one another in use; hence Before Completion. Fire above water is not in its proper place. Observing this image of inappropriate placement, the noble person handles affairs and things carefully, distinguishing where each belongs and placing each in its proper quarter. This is resting at the proper place.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4703:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4703:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4703:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4703:005 -->

**Zhu Zhen:** Fire above and water below each occupy their own places: this is Before Completion. The noble person observes this and carefully distinguishes the myriad things. Only after distinction can there be interaction. Only with incompletion can there be completion. Thus Before Completion contains the image of After Completion.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4703:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4703:006 -->

**He Kai:** Carefully distinguishing things means that things divide into groups. Carefully assigning quarters means that directions gather according to kind.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4703:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4703:007 -->

### First Six: Wetting the tail: it too does not know the limit.[^j12-before-completion-limit]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4703:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4703:008 -->

**Original Meaning.** The word “limit” is not clear. Examining the rhymes above and below, it does not agree with them either. Perhaps it should be “reverence.” For now, leave it unresolved.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4703:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4703:009 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Advancing without assessing one's ability and strength, until the tail is wet, is ignorance at its utmost.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4703:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4703:010 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4703:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4703:011 -->

**Zhang Zhenyuan:** An affair must begin with reverent care before its work can be well completed at the end. The first wets its tail not because the time makes action impossible, but because the heart does not know reverence and caution.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4703:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4703:012 -->

### Nine in the Second Place: Steadfastness brings good fortune: through the mean, it acts correctly.[^j12-before-completion-center]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4703:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4703:013 -->

**Original Meaning.** A nine in the second place is originally not correct. Through centeredness, however, it attains correctness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4703:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4703:014 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The second nine attains correctness and good fortune because dragging back the wheels attains the middle Way, and this is correct.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4703:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4703:015 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Master Cheng says that correctness does not necessarily attain the mean, but the mean is never incorrect. A second nine and fifth six are both formally incorrect, yet they often have the good fortune of steadfastness because they are centered. This Image Commentary explains the matter most clearly.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4703:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4703:016 -->

### Six in the Third Place: Before completion, going forward brings misfortune: the position is not appropriate.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4703:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4703:017 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The third meets misfortune in advancing because its position is inappropriate: yielding yin without centeredness or correctness lacks the ability to traverse danger. If it could cross danger to follow its correspondent, however, that would be beneficial.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4703:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4703:018 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4703:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4703:019 -->

**Wu Cheng:** All Before Completion's lines occupy inappropriate positions, yet this is stated only of the third six because incompletion arises through that line.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4703:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4703:020 -->

**Yu Yan:** All six lines are in inappropriate positions, yet only the third six is expressly described this way because its ability is weak while it occupies the top of the lower trigram.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4703:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4703:021 -->

### Nine in the Fourth Place: Steadfastness brings good fortune; regret vanishes: the intention is carried out.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4703:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4703:022 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** With the fourth's ability suited to the time, adding steadfast firmness allows the intention to be carried out, bringing good fortune and making regret vanish. The campaign against the Guifang is steadfastness at its utmost.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4703:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4703:023 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4703:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4703:024 -->

**Yu Yan:** Since the third six is called not yet completed, the fourth nine reaches completion. Thus its intention is carried out.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4703:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4703:025 -->

### Six in the Fifth Place: The noble person's light: its spreading radiance brings good fortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4703:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4703:026 -->

**Original Meaning.** Radiance is light spreading outward.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4703:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4703:027 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Abundant light has radiance: its outward diffusion. The noble person accumulates fullness until his light is abundant and radiates outward. This is goodness at its utmost, and so good fortune is stated again.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4703:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4703:028 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4703:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4703:029 -->

**Zhang Zhenyuan:** Speaking of radiance after light displays its abundance. The good fortune of steadfastness lies in the fifth; the good fortune of spreading radiance lies throughout the world.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4703:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4703:030 -->

### Top Nine: Drinking wine until the head is wet: it too does not know restraint.[^j12-before-completion-ending]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4703:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4703:031 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Drinking wine until the head is wet is an extreme lack of restraint. It reaches this point because it cannot rest at ease in rightness and the mandate. With such ease, it would not lose its constant measure.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4703:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4703:032 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4703:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4703:033 -->

**Kong Yingda:** This explains how drinking wine leads to the trouble of a wet head: it comes from not knowing where to stop and restrain oneself.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4703:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4703:034 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** After Completion's top is what the Judgment calls disorder at the end; Before Completion's top is what its Judgment calls nearly completing the crossing. Because the tail belongs to the first line's image, the top does not use wetting the tail. It only warns against wetting the head and losing restraint. This is still the meaning of failing to continue through to the end.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4703:034 -->

## Translator’s Endnotes

The base is the repository’s [Chinese Juan Twelve](../source/juan-12.md). “Parallel transcription” refers to the *Siku Quanshu* text at [Wikisource, fixed revision 759593](https://zh.wikisource.org/w/index.php?title=御纂周易折中_(四庫全書本)/卷12&oldid=759593), consulted September 26, 2026. Selected historical page images were also inspected where specified. This is selective comparison, not a systematic collation of historical leaves; agreement between transcriptions does not establish their independence. The Chinese source is unchanged. Attested corrections, repunctuation, contextual interpretation, historical conjectures, and unresolved readings are distinguished below. “Editorial Judgment” always denotes the imperial compilers, not the translator.

[^j12-layer]: This is 象下傳, the lower Commentary on the Images, covering hexagrams 31–64. A Great Image draws instruction from the two trigram images; a Small Image comments on a line statement. These are not new oracles. Line labels in the English are editorial aids. The compilation attributes this commentarial layer to Confucius; that attribution is retained as the source’s account, not independently established here. The prefatory and preliminary juans remain outside this translation.

[^j12-xian-openness]: 虛 is openness or emptiness of the heart, contrasted with a heart already occupied by 私, private partiality. 無心 here is not unconsciousness or absence of discernment. Cheng’s rejection of receiving only what agrees with oneself is stronger than a recommendation to collect congenial advice. He Kai expressly retains thought and intention while rejecting their narrowing into private attachment. The English preserves that distinction rather than replacing the teaching with either indiscriminate assent or modern self-assertion. The Three Powers, 三才, are Heaven, Earth, and humanity.

[^j12-xian-readings]: Two attested transcription repairs affect the English. In Chen Chen’s Great Image gloss, the base has 先人者為主; the parallel has 先入者為主, “what entered first becomes the master,” the familiar explanation of preconception. In Li Dingzuo’s fifth-line quotation, 志未 is corrected to the parallel’s 志末, matching both the primary Image and his explanation of 末 as the top. Conversely, the base’s intelligible 君子體之, “the noble person embodies it,” is retained against the parallel transcription’s apparent 君子禮之 error. The Chinese file itself is unchanged.

[^j12-xian-also]: 亦, “too,” joins successive Small Images. Cheng uses it as evidence for reading the Image statements as a connected text, originally separate from the line oracles. His observation does not make the third line equivalent in value to the first two: Zhu emphasizes that firm yang at the top of Gen should be capable of stillness. In this project, six and nine designate yin and yang; the lines are counted upward.

[^j12-xian-back]: 脢 is the flesh along the back, apart from the heart’s active concerns. 末 can mean the peripheral or insignificant, but several collected commentators take it concretely as the top line. Zhu reads failure to reach others; Cheng reads a warning against shallow private attachments; the collected glosses relate that warning specifically to the top six. “At the extremity” leaves these competing developments available instead of suppressing one. The underlying line oracle is translated in Juan Five.

[^j12-heng-direction]: 方 is a settled direction, proper place, or principle of conduct, not a command to remain at one geographical address. The collected explanations insist that constancy persists through change. The compilers reject two familiar allusions as inadequate explanations of these particular images: Shun remaining unconfused in violent weather and Confucius changing demeanor at thunder. Their point is interpretive specificity, not denial of those traditional accounts.

[^j12-heng-depth]: 浚 means to deepen or dredge. The physical image becomes a warning against demanding mature constancy before its conditions have developed. 蘇氏濬 is Su Jun; the graph in his name happens also to mean depth. His reference to 助長 recalls Mencius’s warning against forcing growth. It is not a recommendation to abandon sustained effort.

[^j12-heng-woman]: The distinction between the woman’s fidelity to one and the man’s judgment according to 義 belongs to the source’s historical gender hierarchy. Cheng construes 從婦 as following the woman’s prescribed mode of conduct, whereas Xiang explicitly construes the husband’s following his wife. Both readings remain visible. The translation neither disguises them as gender-neutral advice nor asks the modern reader to adopt that hierarchy.

[^j12-dun-distance]: 不惡而嚴 is not a license for cruelty. The commentators distinguish firm moral distance from anger that provokes resentment. 張子 is Zhang Zai. His pronunciation gloss reads 惡 as wù, “to hate.” Yang Shi and the compilers differ from the opening structural explanation: they make the mountain itself a model of inaccessible dignity rather than simply identifying it with petty people. The English keeps both constructions.

[^j12-dun-readings]: The parallel transcription supports 韓侂胄, Han Tuozhou, for the base’s 韓侘胄; 如執之以牛革, “as though bound with oxhide,” for 扣執之以牛革; and 未可, “cannot yet,” for 末可 in the third-line editorial judgment. That judgment’s concluding allusion is corrupt in the base, 小貞言; the parallel has 小貞吉. The English conveys its reference to the Judgment’s teaching about limited steadfastness without manufacturing a new exact oracle quotation. The anecdote of Zhu Xi’s proposed impeachment is the compilers’ report, not independently verified here.

[^j12-dun-binding]: The speakers disagree about whose resolve is being secured and what it is resolved to do. Cheng stresses mutual commitment above and below; Hou stresses remaining to assist the ruler; Cai stresses the individual’s unbribable resolve; Kong stresses preventing another’s departure. Kong’s paragraph is already labeled 附錄 in the Chinese base: “Supplement” here is a source label, not a translator’s addition. 殷之父師 refers to Jizi in the traditional account.

[^j12-dun-entanglement]: 臣妾 denotes subordinated household dependents, including male servants and female dependents or concubines. The earlier line translation and this commentary retain the historical relation rather than recasting it as ordinary friendship. 疾憊 develops the image of disabling encumbrance. The compilers connect the limitation on “great affairs” with discretion in public speech and action, not with permanent incapacity.

[^j12-strength-self-mastery]: 禮, rendered “ritual propriety” or “propriety,” is the historically embodied order of fitting conduct, not merely good manners. 克己復禮 recalls Analects 12.1. The commentary deliberately places moral self-mastery above spectacular martial courage. That comparison is not softened into an endorsement of force.

[^j12-strength-wang]: 罔 is disputed. The primary Image follows the negative construction argued by Xiang, Yang, Gong, and Yu: the noble person does not employ force in the petty person’s manner. Zhu and Cheng instead understand disregard of obstacles and explain a fault in the noble person as well. Their incompatible readings are fully translated. The change from two 用 graphs in the line oracle to one in this Image is Yu Yan’s own textual argument. This is an interpretive choice, not correction of the Chinese witness.

[^j12-strength-punctuation]: In the fourth-line Cheng commentary, both transcriptions have 藩決開而不羸困其力也. The punctuated base separates 不羸 from 困其力, which can misleadingly make exhaustion the result. The English reads 羸困 together under 不: the hedge does not entangle or exhaust the strength. This is repunctuation supported by the continuous wording, not a claim to have found an additional negative.

[^j12-strength-position]: The fifth-line 易 is read through the commentaries’ yielding ease, not automatically as the alternative “boundary” reading discussed in Juan Five. The compilers distinguish formal line correctness from what a position calls for in this particular time. Thus 位不當 is not invariably a verdict of moral wrongness. The ram remains a concrete image, subsequently applied to obstinate force.

[^j12-jin-light]: 自昭明德 develops the same vocabulary as the Great Learning’s 明明德. “Luminous virtue” is not fame or favorable publicity: Yu Yan distinguishes an inherent brightness from its obscuration and manifestation. The source’s claim that desire obscures that brightness remains a philosophical claim of the commentators, not an added psychological theory.

[^j12-jin-commission]: Zhu and Cheng understand 命 here as an official commission; the compilers also relate it to the mandate or favorable turn of the time discussed under Approach. Spacious patience therefore does not excuse neglect of duties already assumed. Cheng’s final 亦容有為之兆者 is shared by the consulted transcriptions; “there may also be signs that something can still be done” preserves its qualification without claiming a recovered reading. The source names the next commentator only 劉氏; no given name has been supplied.

[^j12-jin-ancestress]: 王母 can suggest a royal mother in this context, but He Kai explicitly cites the kinship gloss “father’s deceased mother,” paternal grandmother or ancestress. His particular interpretation is retained. Positional correspondence here does not require opposite line polarities: both the second and fifth are yin, a fact the earlier commentary uses to discuss lack of recognition.

[^j12-jin-settlement]: 伐邑 retains the concrete language of attacking one’s own settlement. Cheng and the compilers extend it to correcting oneself, but differ over whether “not yet radiant” describes the imperfection of excessive self-severity or identifies the prior condition requiring correction. The English does not replace the primary military image with a purely psychological paraphrase.

[^j12-mingyi-governing]: 用晦而明 is translated to preserve Lin Xiyuan’s distinction: a ruler need not become ignorant in order to refrain from exhausting every private matter by scrutiny. He Kai gives a somewhat different account, clarity arising through obscurity. The crown pendants, ear-floss, and architectural screens are the commentators’ ritual images of restraint. They have not been replaced with a modern theory of surveillance or administration.

[^j12-mingyi-sustenance]: The primary phrase 義不食 can mean that rightness requires not eating or not accepting another’s sustenance. Zhu and Cheng allow hardship in withdrawal; Wang Shenzi explicitly makes refusal of sustenance a reason to depart. The cited Boyi and Grand Duke belong to traditional accounts of withdrawal from unworthy rule. Their mention is preserved without expanding it into a reconstructed historical episode.

[^j12-mingyi-left-belly]: Cheng reads the fourth line as a corrupt intimate who gains a dark ruler’s heart and perpetuates his delusion. This differs substantially from readings of the line oracle in terms of understanding a ruler and departing. No conciliatory paraphrase has been inserted: the disagreement belongs to the compilation. See the corresponding line discussion in Juan Five.

[^j12-mingyi-jizi]: Jizi is the traditional exemplum of preserving inward brightness under the last Shang ruler, Zhou. Cheng contrasts him with Yang Xiong, making an adverse historical judgment that the translation reports without endorsing or independently adjudicating. In Hu Bingwen’s final gloss, “a Zhou” means that Shang ruler, not the Zhou dynasty. The contrast is between losing and preserving the rule, 則.

[^j12-family-words]: 物 in 言有物 means substance grounded in actual affairs, not ornamental eloquence. Kong’s 口無擇言 and 身無擇行 are rendered as speech and action that need not be rejected or corrected; they do not mean speaking or acting indiscriminately. The image carries influence from an inward source to outward effects, which explains the priority of self-cultivation.

[^j12-family-bounds]: The base punctuates 正志未流散，變動而閑之, potentially suggesting that bounds are imposed after intentions change. The continuous parallel wording is the same. The English instead reads 未 over 流散變動: while correct intentions have not dispersed or changed, establish bounds. This is repunctuation, not an attested addition, and follows the beginning and ending of Cheng’s own paragraph.

[^j12-family-mutual-love]: 刑家 here means serving as a model for or ordering the household, not physically punishing it. Cheng initially discusses husband and wife; Gong expressly extends mutual love to the whole household. The final condition—rules alone do not amount to the household’s fulfillment—has been preserved. The Evolution of Ritual, 禮運, is a chapter of the Book of Rites.

[^j12-family-authority]: The source itself qualifies its earlier language of sternness. Zhu, Cheng, and the collected commentators locate legitimate 威 in correcting oneself, and Zhao explicitly warns against reading the chapter as severity without affection. This qualification is part of the Chinese text, not an added modern apology. The historical gender and household hierarchy nevertheless remains visible.

[^j12-kui-difference]: 同而異 is not uniformity, nor difference cultivated for its own sake. Cheng distinguishes the common principles of human relations from mistaken social convention; Xun adds differentiated public functions. The four classes, 四民, are the traditional occupational groupings of scholars or officers, farmers, artisans, and merchants. The natural divergence of fire and lake remains the image supporting these applications.

[^j12-kui-lane]: In Cheng’s second-line gloss the base has 未非此也; the parallel reads 未非必也. The latter supports the explanation of 未 as “not necessarily,” rather than simply the temporal “not yet.” In Xiang’s fifth-line gloss, 噬之則人 is corrected to the parallel’s 噬之則入, “a bite penetrates it.” These are attested repairs. The fourth-line Cheng paragraph calls the line’s “no blame” 卦辭; the English says “the text,” since the immediate reference is to the line wording, not the hexagram Judgment.

[^j12-kui-skin]: The flesh or skin image is retained while the commentators explain an easily overcome barrier and a minister’s influence entering a ruler’s heart. 宗, “kindred,” does not have to name a literal relative in their interpretation. They connect the second and fifth through their proper correspondence, in contrast with the same-polarity partnership of the first and fourth.

[^j12-kui-doubts]: The pig, ghosts, and drawn bow belong to the underlying line oracle; the Small Image gathers them under “all doubts disappear.” Zhu explicitly says that the original grounds of some images are no longer recoverable. Zhao’s final pairing is also deliberate: he dismisses both obsessive marvel-stories and the need for arguments denying spirits. The translation preserves this historical interpretive stance without turning it into either a modern clinical diagnosis or proof about spirits.

[^j12-jian-self-examination]: The source does not say that all adversity proves personal wrongdoing. Cheng expressly distinguishes correcting an actual fault from redoubling cultivation when the heart has no reproach. Zhu further insists that Limping’s opportunity for progress differs from the extremity represented by Oppression. His contrast should not be flattened into a general injunction to passivity or self-blame.

[^j12-jian-substance]: 實 can denote sincerity or substantial virtue, but also the solid yang line. Cheng applies the sentence to sincere relations among correctly placed lines; Xun, Shen, and Jiang identify yang as the substance; the compilers assign 當位 to the fifth, not the fourth. The primary English leaves room for the dispute, which the commentary states explicitly. The base’s stop after 宜著 in the editorial judgment has been removed in the English syntax: 宜著九五說 means that the phrase should be applied to the fifth nine, not that something merely “ought to be manifest.” This is repunctuation of wording also present in the parallel.

[^j12-jian-measure]: 中節 can describe centered integrity or fitting the right measure. Cheng emphasizes loyal constancy even without success; the compilers emphasize timing and proportion in advancing or stopping. Their historical judgments on the named Han and Jin ministers are retained as their judgments. The parallel supports 朝廷, “the court,” for the base’s 朝延 in the Pei Du quotation.

[^j12-jian-inward]: For the top line, “inward” need not mean only the inner trigram. Cheng combines correspondence with the third and following the fifth; Su identifies both expressions solely with the fifth. In Cheng’s explanation, the English follows the parallel’s 以求自濟, “to seek its own deliverance,” against the base’s 以來自濟. This is an attested correction, not a change to the stored Chinese.

[^j12-release-clemency]: 赦過宥罪 is deliberately differentiated. Cheng and Kong take 過 as inadvertent fault, which may be pardoned, and 罪 as deliberate offense, which receives mitigation rather than unqualified pardon. The translation does not collapse their distinctions into a general injunction to forgive. This is historical ethical and governmental commentary, not present-day legal guidance.

[^j12-release-burden]: The burden-bearer and carriage are concrete images of a mismatch between office and virtue, developed within the source’s hierarchical society. Cheng extends them through a blame-attributing analogy that includes a woman’s appearance and sexual assault. That historical statement is translated without deletion; responsibility for sexual violence belongs to the perpetrator, and the analogy does not justify it. The parallel supports 教誨, “instruct,” for the base’s 教海. The compilers themselves stress the political application to rulers rather than treating the paragraph solely as advice about private possessions.

[^j12-release-toe]: 而 in 解而拇 is read as the second-person pronoun, “your,” as in the underlying line. The toe represents a low attachment, but the interpreters differ over the particular yin relationships. The compilers specify the first by correspondence and the third by adjacency. The primary image is not replaced with its moral interpretation.

[^j12-release-withdrawal]: The editorial phrase “following Zheng’s explanation” appears in both consulted transcriptions, although no Zheng gloss is supplied immediately under the fifth line in either. It may refer to the broader line-commentary tradition rather than a missing local paragraph. No unattested supplementary text has been invented. The compilers’ proposal is importantly different from forcible removal: petty people respond to credible release by changing and withdrawing themselves.

[^j12-release-rebellion]: The lord of Yu is Shun in the traditional account. 共驩 abbreviates Gonggong and Huandou; 三苗 is the Three Miao. These examples express the compilers’ distinction between internal and external disorder. They are retained as historical allusions, not presented as independently reconstructed events or a modern classification of peoples.

[^j12-decrease-anger]: 懲忿窒欲 keeps two actions distinct: checking anger already stirred and preventing desire from finding an opening. Kong explains their mutually completing syntax, not an absolute division of two unrelated disciplines. The “nine considerations” allude to the Analects. Yichuan is Cheng Yi. The compilers here explicitly connect the Great Image’s two instructions to the inner and outer trigram virtues; that local explanation is retained alongside their broader methodological account in Juan Eleven.

[^j12-decrease-swiftly]: 已事 can mean ceasing or setting aside one’s own affairs; the commentaries emphasize prompt assistance upward. 尚 is both associated graphically with 上, “above,” and explained by Cheng through what is esteemed. That wordplay is stated rather than silently reduced to a single English sense. The compilers treat this as an exception justified by Decrease’s particular time, not a general recommendation to seek patronage.

[^j12-decrease-mean]: Cheng distinguishes formal positional correctness from the ethical mean. A yang line in the second, an even place, is not formally correct, yet its centered virtue can make its conduct correct. His claim that the mean is weightier than correctness therefore must not be read as permission to abandon rightness. “Benefit without diminishing oneself” means preserving the Way rather than gratifying a superior at the cost of integrity.

[^j12-decrease-three]: The compilers do not require exactly three people: 自二以上 is read in this context as plurality extending beyond the pair, expressed by “three.” Their examples are conventional repeated actions, Ji Wenzi’s deliberation and Nan Rong’s recitation. This is their nonliteral numerical interpretation, not a change in the transmitted numeral or a divination algorithm.

[^j12-decrease-joy]: 疾 retains the image of an ailment while the commentary treats a remediable personal fault. The compilers further distinguish the beneficiary’s joy from the helper’s joy: the changed wording 可喜 is taken as the other person’s response. The text is not prescribing treatment for a physical illness. In the fifth line, assistance from Heaven and the Lord’s acceptance remain explicitly religious terms, not merely metaphors for personal confidence.

[^j12-increase-readings]: The Great Image’s base text has 有過則敗, “when there is a fault, fail.” The parallel has 有過則改, “when there is a fault, correct it,” agreeing with every explanation that follows; 改 is adopted in the English. In Cheng’s final quotation, 莫易之 is likewise corrected to the parallel’s 莫益之. His opening is repunctuated as 理者天下之至公，利者眾人所同欲: “Principle is the world’s utmost impartiality; benefit is what all people alike desire.” These repairs affect the English only.

[^j12-increase-decrease]: He Kai’s 咸言速, “Influence speaks of swiftness,” is shared by both consulted transcriptions and is retained, not silently changed to 感, “influence.” He pairs prompt responsiveness through openness with Constancy’s enduring substance, then relates Decrease and Increase to them. This is an interpretive comparison across four hexagrams, not an identification of their distinct Images.

[^j12-increase-adversity]: 固有之 receives three distinct readings: Zhu treats adversity as a means of making moral benefit firmly one’s own; Cheng treats it as assuming responsibility without prior authorization, permissible in an emergency; Gong treats the benefit as inherently one’s own rather than externally bestowed. The primary English keeps possession and firmness audible, while the prose preserves their disagreement. No claim that suffering inevitably improves a person has been added.

[^j12-increase-beneficence]: 惠 can denote giving benefit and recognizing benefit with gratitude. The fifth-line wording turns between the ruler’s beneficent heart and others’ response to his virtue. Cui further reads 問 through “speaking,” emphasizing the ruler’s refusal to advertise merit. His opening reference to diminishing above belongs to Increase’s 損上益下 principle and has not been mistaken for an accidental change of hexagram.

[^j12-increase-one-sided]: Zhu takes 偏辭 as an incomplete statement: lack of benefit is only part of the outcome, since attack also follows. Cheng instead treats it as criticism of self-partiality; 非其偏己之辭也 is read with 非 as “condemn,” not as a negative denying the criticism. The concluding claims that fortune and misfortune arise from the heart are the commentators’ ethical argument, not a translator’s assertion that victims cause every harm they suffer.

[^j12-breakthrough-wary]: 居德則忌 remains difficult: Zhu explicitly writes 未詳, “not yet clear.” Cheng reads 則 as regulation and 忌 as preventive prohibition, and reports Wang Bi’s variant 明忌. The compilers read abiding in virtue with continuing caution. The primary English follows their intelligible construction without suppressing Zhu’s uncertainty or presenting Cheng’s reported variant as independently collated. 施祿 concerns distributing stipends or material bounty downward, not merely sharing personal insight.

[^j12-breakthrough-readings]: In Su Shi’s second-line gloss, the base’s 大壯九二貞占 is corrected to the parallel’s 大壯九二貞吉: Great Strength’s second nine has the good fortune of steadfastness, not a separate expression “steadfast divination.” The fourth-line commentators differ substantially: Cheng criticizes loss of firmness, whereas the compilers stress that the line has not yet reached the responsibility it would assume. Neither is silently assimilated to the other.

[^j12-breakthrough-inner-light]: The fifth-line dispute concerns the direction of explanation. Cheng describes outwardly correct decision despite an incompletely purified heart; the compilers make the inner lack of radiance the reason for prescribing the middle course. 中 thus moves between a centered course and what is within the heart. “The heart” in the primary English makes the latter meaning readable; the alternative is fully present in the commentary.

[^j12-breakthrough-cry]: 號 is distinguished by pronunciation and sense: hào, proclamation or command, versus háo, crying or wailing. Cheng argues for the latter here, against interpreting the phrase as “no further commands are needed.” His final answer is also essential: eliminating the petty person’s Way may mean reforming the person, not killing him. The English preserves both the harsh prognostic language and this express qualification.

[^j12-gou-commands]: 后 means the ruler or sovereign here, not “queen.” Cheng explicitly distinguishes the agents named in different Great Images. The compilers’ comparison of Xun with Coming to Meet concerns the first yin entering beneath yang; it does not claim that the two six-line hexagrams have identical structures. Their political use of “hidden yin” is retained as part of the historical interpretive vocabulary.

[^j12-gou-drawing]: 牽 is disputed. Zhu and Cheng first explain being drawn onward, hence the need for restraint; Kong and the later commentators explain binding or checking. At the third line, Cheng reads not hastening, Guo reads failure to restrain, and the compilers insist on restraint throughout. “The yielding Way is drawn” keeps the initial wording open; the third-line primary English follows the compilers’ construction. 柅 is conventionally read here as a stopping device or brake; the exact implement is not established by this selective comparison.

[^j12-gou-fish]: The fish and wrapping are retained as physical images, while the commentators apply them to controlling the initial yin’s further attachments. 賓 denotes guests or outsiders to the relation. The fourth-line compilers then qualify simple exclusion: accommodation is needed before restraint is possible. This tension between containment and alienation belongs to the text, not to a modern harmonizing interpretation.

[^j12-gou-mandate]: 命 is interpreted differently within the collection. Cheng identifies it with Heaven’s principle; Su contrasts the natural growth of yin with a human resolve capable of overcoming it; the compilers emphasize the timely fall of the melon and cultivated virtue coinciding with that timing. Su’s strong claim about human effort is retained as his claim, not presented as the consensus. The melon is supplied by the underlying line oracle, not newly introduced by the translator.

[^j12-gathering-defense]: 除戎器 means putting military equipment in order, not abolishing weapons. Both Zhu and Cheng explain repair and preparation, and Wang Shenzi distinguishes natural banks from artificial dikes. The word “unforeseen,” 不虞, concerns risks not yet anticipated; the Image is not an instruction to initiate conflict.

[^j12-gathering-center]: In Cheng’s second-line paragraph, the parallel supports 象含其意以存戒, “the Image keeps its meaning implicit to preserve the warning,” against the base’s 象舍其義以存戒. Yang Wanli’s quotation 中末變 is corrected to the parallel’s 中未變. Cheng emphasizes the risk that yieldingness may change; Yang and the compilers emphasize preserving the same principle after advancement. These are related but not identical readings of 未. In Cheng’s final-line gloss, the parallel’s 猶俗云, “as the colloquial expression says,” repairs the base’s 猶俗元.

[^j12-gathering-above]: 上 is disputed: Cheng identifies the top six; Zheng expressly denies that identification and reads the yang above, while Yu Fan’s compressed gloss names movement to the fourth. The English preserves the brevity of Yu’s statement rather than inventing an unprovided transformation diagram. These local readings must not be made to look like one agreed correspondence.

[^j12-gathering-tears]: Cheng treats the top line’s distress as the shameful result of an ill-chosen station. Zhao and the compilers instead emphasize conscientious unease and the desire for rightful reunion, using Shun and the Duke of Zhou as exemplars. The primary tears and nasal discharge are retained, as are the conflicting ethical evaluations. The historical examples are reported as the text’s allusions, not newly verified biographies.

[^j12-ascent-virtue]: The primary translation follows the base’s 順, the compliant or fitting course of cultivation. Zhu reports 慎, careful attention, in Wang Su’s text and other quotations; Hu Bingwen develops that reading. This is a variant reported by the source itself, not a modern replacement silently introduced into the Chinese. Neither reading turns accumulation into a promise of instant attainment.

[^j12-ascent-above]: Cheng identifies “above” with the second nine; Lü and the compilers identify the three yin of Kun above. The two structural explanations remain separate. The fourth-line 亨 is read sacrificially, as the commentaries explicitly explain, and translated “makes offerings.” It has not been mechanically translated “success” merely because that is a common sense of the same transmitted graph.

[^j12-ascent-third]: Su Shi’s third-line gloss is misnumbered 九二 in the base. The parallel has 九三, agreeing with the line being explained, and the English adopts “the third nine.” His refusal to infer good fortune from unimpeded advancement is essential: decisiveness alone does not establish the moral or practical result.

[^j12-ascent-darkness]: 消不富 can describe the decline that follows overextended ascent, as Cheng reads it, or counsel deliberate diminution rather than further aggrandizement, as Hu and the compilers read it. The primary phrase is kept spare so both developments remain possible. The compilers distinguish this warning from the underlying line’s “unceasing steadfastness,” which they take as steadfast work of self-diminution.

[^j12-oppression-life]: 致命 admits the related senses of committing one’s life, surrendering the claim to control it, and entrusting oneself to the mandate or fate. Zhu’s 授命 stresses relinquishment; Cheng and several collected commentators stress accepting what lies outside one’s control while preserving moral resolve. Cheng first requires exhausting prudent precautions: this is not seeking danger or death. The distinction from Limping’s still-available practical progress is made explicitly earlier in this juan.

[^j12-oppression-reading]: Wang Bi’s final question reads 道可志乎 in the base. The parallel has 道可忘乎, “could the Way be forgotten?”, which the English adopts. This restores the argument for preserving the Way under oppression rather than a puzzling question about intending it. The malformed bold markup 困于****葛藟 in the final primary Image is normalized for English presentation without altering the Chinese text or splitting its source block.

[^j12-oppression-associate]: Cheng identifies the fourth’s associate as its first-line correspondent; Su Jun and He Kai identify cooperation with the fifth. The apparent contradiction is retained. “Slowly” is explained as patience suited to obstruction, not universally praised indecision.

[^j12-oppression-cutting]: 劓刖 retains the literal penal images of cutting off the nose and feet. The commentaries apply the resulting helplessness to obstructed relations. 說 is read here in the sense of release, in keeping with the line’s course. Cheng distinguishes 直, uprightness, from 正, correctness, partly because the second is centered yang in an even position. His analogy of sincere seeking with sacrifice does not erase the primary text’s actual sacrificial language.

[^j12-oppression-final]: The concise wording 動悔有悔吉 is difficult. The English preserves movement, regret, repentance, and good fortune rather than substituting a fully harmonized explanation. Tian Chou explicitly separates 行也, “taking action,” as the Commentary’s explanation of the line’s 征吉, “going brings good fortune.” His punctuation is preserved. No extra primary passage is created from the source’s single paragraph.

[^j12-well-wood]: The physical explanation is disputed. Cheng takes wood as equipment bearing water; Zhu takes moisture ascending through a living tree and explicitly objects to calling the pottery jar wooden. The compilers prefer Zhu for the Great Image but allow Zheng’s well-sweep for the Judgment Commentary. These are distinct interpretations, not one reconstructed technology. Zhu’s botanical example is translated as his observation, not offered as a newly tested physiological account. 勞民 is caring for or comforting the people here, not simply imposing labor.

[^j12-well-abandoned]: 舍 is given a pronunciation gloss by Cheng: abandonment rather than the different use in Qian’s 時舍. The English preserves both the gloss and the image of birds failing to approach. The second line’s lack of an associate is explicitly distinguished from the first line’s lack of water; unused ability and absent ability are not made equivalent.

[^j12-well-grief]: 行惻 is sharply disputed. Zhu and Zhao understand the grief of people passing by; Cheng understands grief because one’s ability cannot be put into action. Wang allows others’ grief and advocacy even without the worthy person’s own seeking. The primary English follows the passersby reading while translating Cheng’s alternative in full. Seeking recognition is justified by public benefit, not reduced to personal ambition.

[^j12-well-spring]: The compilers interpret two lines of the Odes through 中 and 正. The first is not a claim that a drying spring has no internal cause: its “none says” is retained, and the interpretation presses the need to examine the source within. Cold water and downward flow remain the concrete features from which the ethical application is drawn.
