[^j12-revolution-calendar]: 治曆明時 is calendrical ordering and clarification of seasonal timing. Zhu explicitly denies that the sentence itself commands calendar reform. The concrete astronomical and seasonal terms are retained rather than reduced to personal “cycles.” 已日 is translated consistently with the earlier line commentary as the day having passed; the delay serves the commentators’ concern with trust and appropriate timing.

[^j12-revolution-three]: 又何之矣 is a rhetorical question after adequate consultation, not an unqualified ban on carrying out the reform. Cheng makes action legitimate because it follows public deliberation rather than private inclination. Xu maps the stages onto the first three lines. Their explanations are preserved without imposing a modern voting procedure or a required number of consultations outside the textual image.

[^j12-revolution-face]: The final disagreement is explicit. Cheng accepts outward conformity by a petty person whose inward disposition may remain unchanged; Yu Yan rejects the claim that the heart has not changed and insists on sincere transformation. Neither voice is softened into the other. Tiger and leopard patterns describe distinct degrees and forms of visible transformation, not literal changes of species.

[^j12-cauldron-mandate]: 命 is not confined to one sense in these glosses. Cheng explains settled commands; others explain the received political mandate; Xiang also compares the human lifespan. “Consolidates the mandate” is the primary rendering, with the alternatives preserved. Zhu’s brief citation 至道不凝 is a lexical comparison for 凝, not a new claim that the highest Way can never be established. The cauldron’s shape and stability remain the basis of the ethical and political analogy.

[^j12-cauldron-overturning]: Overturning is normally contrary to the vessel’s use, but becomes appropriate when removing stale contents. The source extends this to the historical concubine’s status through her son. That hierarchy is retained without turning the woman into a metaphor for a modern relationship. The compilers point out that the Small Image leaves the concubine implicit while explaining “following the honored.”

[^j12-cauldron-counterpart]: 仇 can mean an opposing or matching counterpart, not necessarily an enemy. Cheng identifies the first line; the compilers allow a harmful intention yet distinguish one’s own fault from others’ resentment in 尤. In the third line they likewise distinguish the absence of a structural correspondence from moral wrongdoing. These distinctions prevent positional language from being mechanically turned into ethical condemnation.

[^j12-cauldron-reading]: In Cheng’s fifth-line gloss, the parallel has 是以中為實德也, “it therefore takes centeredness as substantial virtue,” where the base has 足以中為實德也. The English follows the attested 是. The surrounding argument makes 中 the source of the line’s suitability, even though the line itself is yielding and open.

[^j12-cauldron-jade]: 鉉 is the lifting-bar used with the cauldron’s ears, not the ears themselves. Jade supplies the comparison of firmness and warmth. Cheng deliberately distinguishes 大吉, “great good fortune,” from 元吉, “supreme good fortune,” when comparing The Cauldron with The Well. These verdicts have not been collapsed into one English formula.

[^j12-shock-cultivation]: 恐懼脩省 joins apprehension to the active work of correction and examination. The commentators distinguish this from panic that disperses one’s capacities. The base’s malformed bold sequence around 脩 is normalized in the English presentation; the entire Great Image remains one source-aligned block.

[^j12-shock-radiance]: Cheng explains the fourth line’s stagnation through lost firm correctness; the compilers explicitly reject the claim that its virtue is lost and attribute the obstruction to its circumstances. 未光 therefore cannot be assigned one moral meaning in every hexagram. The reference to Joy’s top line anticipates a later section of this juan.

[^j12-shock-center]: In the fifth Image, 大无喪 is rendered closely as “greatly without loss”; Cheng explains that freedom from loss is itself the great concern. In the top Image, 中 can be the heart’s interior, as Zhu and Gong read it, or the ethical mean, as Cheng reads it. The English leaves the primary “within” open and preserves the differing explanations. Taking warning from a neighbor means responding before the same extremity overtakes oneself.

[^j12-stillness-thought]: 位 is the appropriate station or situation, not merely a fixed office boundary. Cheng includes stopping when one should act among the ways of departing from one’s place. The compilers then give 思 substantive weight, distinguishing desire-driven distraction from thought that penetrates principle. Their citation “what thinking, what deliberation?” is not used here to abolish all thought.

[^j12-stillness-following]: The second line’s 未退聽 is assigned by both Zhu and Cheng to the one above, not to the second’s refusal to listen. The English makes that subject explicit. The third-line warning against 強制, forcible repression, is likewise the source’s own qualification of “stillness,” not a modern instruction about bodily posture or treatment.

[^j12-stillness-body]: 身 and 躬 both concern the embodied person, but Wang reads 躬 through a bowed back, while the compilers focus instead on the change from 其, “its,” to 諸, “it at/in.” Cheng criticizes limiting the work to oneself when one bears ministerial responsibility; Kong explains bodily composure more positively. These different arguments are preserved rather than reduced to one definition of meditation.

[^j12-stillness-rhyme]: Zhu proposes deleting 正, “correctness,” on grounds of rhyme. The primary translation retains the base’s word, and Cheng’s explanation of centeredness as correctness is translated in full. The proposal is an emendation made by a historical commentator, not a deletion newly verified by the translator against a facsimile.

[^j12-gradual-virtue]: Zhu questions either 賢, “worthy,” or an omission after 善, “improves.” The primary translation retains the base’s full wording. No conjectured graph is inserted. The subsequent commentary distinguishes cultivation within oneself from the gradual transformation of customs. The commentator named only 楊氏 remains “Master Yang”; no given name is supplied.

[^j12-gradual-tree]: The phrase 自捩手而干霄 is obscure in both consulted transcriptions. “From the reach of a hand to piercing the clouds” is a contextual rendering of the growth contrast, not an attested emendation of 捩手. The corresponding historical page image was also inspected ([page 1442](https://www.eee-learning.com/eeebook/middleyi/1442.png)); it does not yield a clearly different expression that resolves the difficulty. The exact force of 捩手 therefore remains uncertain. The preceding comparison of hand-girth with an arm’s embrace concerns increasing thickness.

[^j12-gradual-sustenance]: 素飽 is unearned sustenance, not simply plain or vegetarian food. The commentaries connect the second line’s stipend to service and reject eating without fulfilling office. The quoted Odes wording places the unprocessed timber by the river; the compilers apply its riverbank image to the goose’s first stage. Their allusive connection is preserved without making the poem originally a hexagram commentary.

[^j12-gradual-household]: 群醜 means one’s fellows or kind, though Cheng also develops the shame of separation. Yang expressly assigns the “wife” role to the third nine, a yang line; both transcriptions support this, so it is not silently changed to a yin line. His interpretation depends on relational roles, not a fixed identification of every yang line as male. Xiong reports 順/慎 as interchangeable, distinguishing careful mutual protection from mere compliance. The reproductive images are translated as the historical text’s images, not medical claims about the cause of pregnancy loss.

[^j12-gradual-feathers]: 儀 includes ceremonial adornment and an exemplary model. The physical feathers are retained, while Zhu, Cheng, and the collected glosses develop public exemplarity and an undisturbed intention. The top’s remoteness from ordinary office is therefore explicitly distinguished from uselessness.

[^j12-maiden-ending]: 永終 combines endurance with bringing a relation or undertaking through to its end. Cheng gives both generational succession and the preservation of a marriage; Zhu and Wu emphasize foreseeing breakdown. The compilers then reject the opening account of the lake merely following thunder and make its prior influence decisive. That disagreement is part of the source. The marriage hierarchy and prescriptions are translated as historical claims, not restated as universal requirements for modern relationships.

[^j12-maiden-attendant]: 娣 and 媵 concern the junior woman accompanying a principal bride within the historical marital institution, not simply a maid or an ordinary modern bridesmaid. “Attendant wife” preserves her subordinate marital status. 君 in Cheng’s first-line explanation is her female superior, the principal wife, not a king. The lame walking is an image of limited but useful participation.

[^j12-maiden-waiting]: The third-line commentary reads 須 through waiting because no suitable reception is available. The fourth is expressly different: delay is her own deliberate choice, not others’ rejection. This contrast must not be collapsed into a single unfavorable judgment on delayed marriage.

[^j12-maiden-sleeves]: The base’s fifth-line primary text has 不知其娣之袂良也. The parallel Siku transcription reads 不如其娣之袂良也, “not as fine as her attendant wife’s sleeves,” agreeing with the line oracle and both explanations. 如 is adopted in English. Zhu emphasizes nobility of virtue; Cheng and Wang also retain the bride’s elevated social station. The transmitted correction was checked in the parallel’s traditional-character page: https://zh.wikisource.org/zh-hant/御纂周易折中_(四庫全書本)/卷12 .

[^j12-maiden-basket]: The empty basket is a failed ritual offering. Cheng applies it to the historical requirement that marriage sustain ancestral sacrificial service. The translation does not silently replace that institution with a purely psychological reading or treat the image as a medical verdict about fertility.

[^j12-abundance-justice]: The Great Image distinguishes adjudicating the facts, 折獄, from imposing the appropriate penalty, 致刑. Zhu contrasts establishing law in Biting Through with applying it in Abundance. His base wording 未有犯威人 is unusual; the English renders the intelligible sense “no one has yet offended its authority,” rather than silently emending the Chinese to an unattested 法. The warnings against excessive punishment are the source’s own. They are not present-day legal advice.

[^j12-abundance-equality]: 旬 is read as equality or matching by Zhu and Cheng, but through completed measure, fullness, and noon by Liu, Hu, Yu, and the compilers. The primary English follows the equality reading; the alternative is identified by the transliteration xun in the collected glosses. The compilers explicitly connect their reading with the warning that noon and fullness give way to decline. A single English paraphrase cannot erase this disagreement.

[^j12-abundance-obscurity]: The dark coverings and Dipper are retained as images of obscuration. In the fourth-line glosses Xiang assigns the second line’s darkness to the fifth, while Wu makes the fourth the principal source of both lines’ screen image. Their differing structural accounts are preserved. The third line’s warning concerns an unsuitable or ineffective partner, not a general denial of the line’s own ability.

[^j12-abundance-house]: 自藏 receives different constructions: Zhu and Zhang stress self-obstruction; Cheng explains other people hiding from and abandoning the self-exalting person. The primary “concealment of its own making” keeps responsibility clear without suppressing Cheng’s different grammatical subject. Ziyun is Yang Xiong. His quoted warning is translated as the commentary transmits it, not asserted as evidence that spirits literally inspect prosperous houses.

[^j12-wanderer-justice]: 不留獄 concerns avoiding unnecessary delay in cases and detention, not rejecting careful investigation. Zhao explicitly warns that caution prolonged beyond due decision becomes harmful. The fire’s temporary passage across a mountain supplies the image; the ethical application is not a technical modern legal procedure.

[^j12-wanderer-servants]: The primary translation follows Guo, Wang, and Huang, whom the compilers endorse: 以旅與下 means treating subordinates as passing strangers. Cheng first explains mistreating subordinates during one’s own sojourn. Both constructions are retained. Servants remain servants in this historically unequal relation; the passage’s own demand for generosity is not obscured by that hierarchy.

[^j12-wanderer-position]: The fourth’s formal yin position is favorable for a firm traveler even though it is not the normal place of a minister beside a ruler. Cheng expressly refuses to make every occurrence of the fifth a king. 資斧 retains supplies and an axe, interpreted here as means of protection rather than as symbols of unlimited authority.

[^j12-wanderer-recognition]: 上逮 admits being heard above, association with those above and below, and attaining high standing. Zhu, Cheng, Hu, and the compilers do not agree on the mechanism. The base’s 大夫土 is corrected to the parallel transcription’s 大夫士, “officers and scholars.” The reference to presentation gifts continues the underlying pheasant image from Juan Eight. This is a correction supported by another transcription, not a claim of facsimile verification of the passage.

[^j12-wanderer-hearing]: 莫之聞 is inward failure to recognize the loss for Cheng, but the absence of anyone willing to warn the angry person for Zhang. The compilers also give “a traveler above” a distinct application: treating responsibility as temporary lodging without due care. The primary “does not hear” permits the two hearing explanations; neither has been silently made the sole reading.

[^j12-penetration-command]: Xun Shuang’s 法教百瑞 is corrected to the parallel transcription’s 法教百端, “laws and teachings have a hundred aspects.” The 端 reading also occurs in another primary compilation, Li Heng’s [周易義海撮要](https://www.shidianguji.com/mid-page/7370076373282701321), in the corresponding Xun Image passage. The duplicated full stop after Guo Yong is simply normalized in English. The instruction insists that reiterated words culminate in action, not in empty verbal display.

[^j12-penetration-many]: 紛若 is the multiplicity of means or persons communicating sincerity, supplied by the underlying line’s ritual officers and invocators. Cheng gives a moral explanation of their sincerity; He Kai relates the multiplicity to reiterated commands and execution. The primary translation does not add a new ritual procedure absent from this Small Image.

[^j12-penetration-repetition]: 頻巽 admits repeated failures followed by renewed submission in Cheng, but Su Jun expressly rejects that account in favor of habitual deference carried too far. The contrast with the first line is also retained: doubt can still be ordered, while exhausted resolve has lost its capacity for renewal. The warrior image signifies firm resolve here, not indiscriminate violence.

[^j12-penetration-final]: 正乎凶 is a direct assertion of inevitable misfortune for Zhu, but Cheng divides it into the question “can this be correct?” and the verdict of misfortune. The primary follows Zhu while Cheng’s punctuation is translated in full. Yang’s final 無斷則改 in the base is corrected to the parallel transcription’s 無斷則敗, “without decision, one fails.” 資斧 remains resources and an axe, with its decisiveness explained by the commentator. The correction is attested in a parallel transcription, not established through full facsimile collation.

[^j12-joy-learning]: 講習 includes discussion that clarifies understanding and repeated practice that matures conduct. It is not conversation alone. Cheng insists on mutual benefit rather than making the image merely a celebration of enjoyable company. His 朋 and 友 are distinguished by Kong as fellow students and those of shared purpose.

[^j12-joy-readings]: Cai Yuan’s first-line gloss has 聽行未有疑惑 in the base; the parallel reads 所行未有疑惑, “its conduct has no doubt.” The English follows the attested 所 rather than adding an unmotivated act of hearing. Zheng’s 未與心疑 is shared by both consulted transcriptions and retained as outward conduct not conflicting with the heart, rather than silently regularized to a different Chinese phrase.

[^j12-joy-position]: The fifth line shows again that an appropriate or honored position does not guarantee favorable conduct. Zhu compares Treading’s fifth nine, where reliance on position itself may create danger. Cheng instead construes the phrase as saying that precisely this position calls for caution. The warning against trusting destructive pleasure is not removed merely because the line is formally correct.

[^j12-joy-missing]: SUPPLEMENTARY PRIMARY IMAGE — TOP SIX. The base omits the Small Image before Cheng’s final paragraph, though it preserves the commentary explaining it. The parallel Siku Quanshu transcription has 上六引兌，未光也: “The top six draws others into joy: not yet radiant.” The sentence is also attested in the quotation of Tian Chou preserved in [Yongle Dadian, juan 15143](https://zh.wikisource.org/zh-hant/永樂大典/卷15143). It is supplied here, not silently inserted into the source-aligned text or counted as a base-source block. Consequently the selected base has 237, not 238, primary Image blocks across the 34 hexagrams. A separate editorial heading identifies the surviving top-line commentary. Cheng reads drawing out exhausted pleasure; Yang reads enticing others into morally obscure pleasure. No additional unattested commentary has been supplied.

[^j12-dispersion-ritual]: 帝 is the Lord on High, and 廟 is an ancestral temple. The ritual means of gathering hearts have not been secularized into a vague metaphor for shared values. Master Cheng says that Gathering and Dispersion both concern these rites; this is his summary of their connected meanings, not a claim that both Great Images have identical wording.

[^j12-dispersion-self]: 机 is a support or small table on which one may lean, developed into a source of security. Cheng relates the second to the first; Wang emphasizes the security of the second’s position. In the third, Cheng understands the person escaping dispersion through correspondence above, whereas Huang treats setting aside oneself as the condition of rescuing the world. The conflicting direction of the image is preserved.

[^j12-dispersion-readings]: Huang’s 聽以能濟 in the base is corrected to the parallel transcription’s 所以能濟, “thereby able to remedy.” In Xiong’s fifth-line gloss, the base punctuation 渙其居，積然 is read as 渙其居積，然: “dispersing his accumulated stores; yet….” This repunctuation follows Zhu’s alternative interpretation, which Xiong then disputes; it does not turn Xiong into its supporter. These interventions are distinguished from systematic facsimile collation.

[^j12-dispersion-blood]: Cheng proposes a missing 去 after 血 and reads the wording through Small Restraint’s 血去惕出. Xiang expressly rejects that construction, retaining 逖, distance, rather than 惕, apprehension, and appealing to rhyme and clause length. Chen supports the distance reading. The primary follows the base’s “dispersing blood,” while the full dispute remains visible. This is textual and ethical interpretation, not a medical prescription concerning bleeding or sweating.

[^j12-limitation-measures]: The disagreement over 議德行 is preserved: most commentators examine one’s own virtue and conduct, while Kong assesses other people for office, a reading preferred by the compilers. Kong’s paragraph is already a source-labeled 附錄, not a translator’s addition. In the editorial judgment, the base’s 受爵祿 is corrected to the parallel’s 愛爵祿, guarding or valuing ranks and stipends. It is not silently emended to 授, “confer.” Together with 慎名器, the phrase treats the dignity and allocation of public distinctions with care.

[^j12-limitation-courtyards]: 戶庭 and 門庭 distinguish the inner and outer courtyards. The opposite verdicts are explained by timing, not by a blanket rule to remain indoors. Weisheng is the traditional example of a promise held to despite rising water. The story illustrates Cheng’s contrast between principled steadfastness and unresponsive literalism; it is not a newly verified historical event.

[^j12-limitation-readings]: Guo Yong’s second-line gloss begins 切為不當有事之地 in the base. The parallel has 初為不當有事之地, “the first is a place where undertaking affairs is not appropriate,” agreeing with the explicit contrast between the first and second lines. The English follows this attested correction. The opposition of knowing obstruction and knowing passage is otherwise translated without alteration.

[^j12-limitation-blame]: Zhu and Cheng understand the third line as having no one else to blame. Shen reports the alternative that remorse and self-correction bring moral blamelessness. Wang Jiefu is Wang Anshi; Cheng Shasui is Cheng Jiong. He Kai’s count of ninety-nine instances is preserved as his bibliographical observation, not presented as a new count independently verified for this repository’s transcription.

[^j12-sincerity-justice]: 議獄緩死 keeps both conscientious review and delay of irreversible punishment. Cheng explains 緩 through generosity as well as time; Xiang gives traditional procedural examples. His ten-day, twenty-day, and three-month sequence is translated as transmitted here, not represented as an independently established universal rule of ancient law. “Completed with trust and submitted with trust” retains his parallel phrases 獄成而孚，輸而孚. No current legal procedure is being prescribed.

[^j12-sincerity-forethought]: 虞 is deliberation or forethought in Cheng’s explanation: assess the object of trust before commitment biases judgment. The compilers instead emphasize preserving the heart’s sincerity without an alien attachment. Both readings of the intention not changing are preserved. The first line is not turned into an instruction to distrust everyone.

[^j12-sincerity-horse]: The paired horse remains the primary image. Cheng identifies severing one’s kind with leaving the first-line correspondent to follow the fifth, not with a literal change of species or a universal demand to abandon family. The compilers compare the third and fourth through their different positional correctness and relationships. The speaker designated 趙氏玉泉 is retained as “Zhao of Yuquan” rather than given an unverified personal name.

[^j12-sincerity-winged]: “A winged cry” preserves 翰音 and the terminology already used in Juan Eight, where the cock and the sound rising without matching substance are discussed. Cheng here emphasizes trust maintained without necessary adaptation; the collected commentators emphasize reputation without inward substance. “How could it endure?” is a warning to change, not merely a detached prediction of collapse.

[^j12-small-excess-image]: The commentators do not agree on the physical image. Zhu and Cheng hear thunder amplified beyond the ordinary upon the mountain; the compilers hear a sound diminishing by the time it reaches the heights. Both accounts are translated as historical interpretations, not presented as verified acoustics. The ethical excess is explicitly limited by timing and measure.

[^j12-small-excess-allusions]: Chao Yuezhi’s examples deliberately contrast pride with humility, curtailed mourning with intense grief, and ostentation with thrift. The base’s 反玷 is corrected to the parallel’s 反坫, the ceremonial cupstand of the Guan Zhong allusion. The parallel names 宰予, Zai Yu, where the base has 宰子, Master Zai; both point to the same figure, and the base’s form is retained in English. 三歸 is left as “Three Returns,” since its precise institutional meaning has been disputed and is not resolved here. Mo’ao is an office title in the allusion. These are selective textual comparisons, not a claim of full facsimile collation.

[^j12-small-excess-minister]: The second line differs in Zhu and Cheng: Zhu explains not passing over the minister while failing to reach the ruler; Cheng warns the minister against overstepping his allotted relation to the ruler. The primary “must not overstep” follows the latter construction while the former is fully translated. The hierarchy is the source’s, not an added modern prescription.

[^j12-small-excess-fourth]: Zhu explicitly leaves both line and Image unresolved. Cheng reads 長 as zhǎng, growth, while the compilers read continued persistence, cháng, and warn against refusing change. The primary follows the latter; Cheng’s pronunciation argument is preserved. Qian’s “third and fourth both lose their positions” is retained as transmitted, though a yang third line is formally correct; the Judgment elsewhere distinguishes the third’s lack of centeredness from the fourth’s incorrect position. This discrepancy is identified, not silently repaired or passed off as an attested variant.

[^j12-small-excess-clouds]: Gong differentiates yang still rising in Small Restraint from yin already high in Small Excess. The compilers reject that distinction and read rising yin in both, then distinguish the practical standpoint of one below from one above. The weather imagery remains a traditional interpretive model, not a meteorological forecast.

[^j12-completion-precaution]: The successful cooperation of water and fire also contains the possibility of their harming one another. The commentators make vigilance the condition of preserving completion; they do not present collapse as an unconditional fate. This qualification is repeated explicitly at the top line.

[^j12-completion-seven]: The regained object, 茀, is supplied in Cheng’s gloss from the underlying line oracle and rendered as the carriage screen. The primary Image itself leaves it unstated. The seven days are retained as the text’s number; this gloss emphasizes eventual recognition of centered correctness rather than supplying a practical calendrical calculation.

[^j12-completion-exhaustion]: Gaozong is the ruler named in the underlying Guifang campaign. Cheng conditions approval on his intention and warns that greed and anger injure the people. The compilers’ medicine analogy acknowledges the costs of forceful success and the need for recovery. It is translated as a historical analogy, not offered as medical treatment guidance.

[^j12-completion-offering]: The primary emphasizes 時, appropriate timing. Cheng and Zhu Zhen identify the neighbors through the second and fifth lines; Wang and Zhang emphasize the ruler’s restraint after success. 禴 is left as yue with “simple” describing the ritual as these commentators understand it. “Great good fortune arrives” must be read with Cheng’s express qualification that it is great relative to the circumstances of this hexagram, whose success is called small.

[^j12-completion-ending]: The compilers distinguish 厲, danger, from 凶, misfortune, and interpret the rhetorical question as an invitation to change before harm is completed. Their reading is retained alongside the more immediately submerged image in Hu Yuan. No additional fatalistic conclusion has been supplied.

[^j12-before-completion-order]: The opening commentators disagree over “proper place.” Zhu says fire and water each have their own place; Cheng says their arrangement is inappropriate for mutual service; Zhu Zhen treats differentiation as the condition for subsequent interaction. The English preserves the distinction between belonging to one’s own kind and being arranged for useful cooperation. The chapter’s ending therefore need not be read as mere failure.

[^j12-before-completion-limit]: 極, “limit,” remains in the primary translation because it is the base reading. Zhu openly doubts it and proposes 敬, reverence, on grounds of rhyme; Zhang develops that reverence reading. Cheng instead construes the phrase as ignorance at its utmost. These are three different treatments, not a unanimously recovered correction. No independent historical-phonological demonstration is claimed.

[^j12-before-completion-center]: This is the compilation’s especially explicit distinction between formal line correctness and ethical correctness through the mean. A yang second or yin fifth is formally out of position, yet centeredness may make its conduct right. The translation retains both uses rather than allowing “incorrect” to mean morally wrong in every occurrence.

[^j12-before-completion-ending]: The concluding wine and wet-head image is retained concretely. Cheng relates excess to failure to rest in rightness and the mandate; the compilers connect it with the Judgment’s warning about not sustaining the crossing to its end. Their explanation closes the lower Image Commentary, not the whole compilation. The remaining juans, preface, and preliminary juan are separate work.
